# wooldid: a usage guide for AI agents and advanced users

This document is the long companion to `help wooldid`. The help file says what each option does; this guide says how to use the program well: which choices matter, what breaks when they are made badly, what every message means, and what every stored result holds. It is written for a reader, human or machine, who will compose and debug `wooldid` command lines and read the results without running the program interactively first, so it is explicit and complete rather than brief. It describes the version of `wooldid` distributed alongside it.

Conventions: options and stored results are in code font (`cluster()`, `e(gteffects)`); a name in backquotes inside a quoted message is a placeholder the program fills in at run time; "the help file" means `help wooldid`. Each chapter opens with the decision rules and then carries the help file's fuller account of the same material, so a chapter can be read top to bottom or used as a reference.

Contents: 1 Orientation; 2 Choosing i, t, ttre and weights; 3 Estimands; 4 Analytic inference; 5 Bootstrap inference; 6 Randomization inference; 7 Event studies; 8 Subgroups; 9 Continuous treatments; 10 Controls and fixed effects; 11 Performance and technical options; 12 safety(); 13 Stored results; 14 Messages; 15 Comparisons to related estimators; 16 Recipes and pitfalls.

## 1. Orientation

`wooldid y i t ttre [if] [aw/pw=weights], options` estimates difference-in-differences treatment effects with staggered treatment onset by the two-way fixed effects approach of Wooldridge (2025): a regression of `y` on cohort and period fixed effects plus one indicator per treated cohort-by-period cell, followed by `margins` calculations that average the cell effects into the estimands you ask for. The four arguments are the outcome `y`, the cohort identifier `i` (positive integers), the period `t` (numeric, on the same grid as `ttre`), and `ttre`, the cohort's treatment onset in the units of `t` (missing for never-treated cohorts). Treatment must be absorbing: once treated, a cohort stays treated.

What to expect from a run:

- A header with the estimation sample (observations dropped, clusters), then one results table per estimand. Rows are `main` (the pooled post-treatment effect), `pretreatment` (the pooled pre-treatment placebo, when pre-treatment horizons are requested or `esfixedbaseperiod` is in force), and the event-study horizons `pre#` and `post#` counted outward from the reference period. Columns are the estimate, standard error, statistic, p-value and confidence interval; under `permute()` and `bootstrap()` the columns are relabelled for the engine that produced them.
- Subgroup tables and pairwise contrasts under `subgroups()`, a joint pre-trend test under `jointtests`, the adversarial line test under `adversarial`, Roth-style diagnostics under `pretrendpower()`, and the cohort-by-period table under `gteffects`.
- Everything is stored in `e()`; typing `wooldid` alone replays the stored results. The chapter on stored results lists every object.

Cautions are printed when a run changes your specification and, with few exceptions, when a quantity is reported without inference; errors stop the run. The messages chapter lists every one with its trigger. Dependencies: Stata 16 or later, `reghdfe`, `ftools` and `gtools` (all three are required and checked at entry), and `ppmlhdfe` for `poisson`.

The remainder of this chapter is the estimation approach as the help file states it, followed by the supporting output options.

### From the help file

### Estimation of Difference-in-Differences Treatment Effects with Staggered Treatment Onset Using Heterogeneity-Robust Two-Way Fixed Effects Regressions

**wooldid** -- Suite for implementing difference-in-differences style analyses with staggered treatment onset using the two-way fixed effects approach proposed in Wooldridge (2025) and the high dimensional fixed effects estimators developed by Correia (2017). Features include estimation of various types of average treatment effects, comparison of treatment effects across subgroups, estimation of event study style estimates and related parallel trends assumption tests, and estimation of average and marginal treatment effects in the presence of a continuous treatment (or treatment intensity) variable [experimental]. This suite also can produce event study plots, histograms of treatment effects by cohort, and plots of treatment effects across the distribution of a specified continuous treatment variable. Options for inference include inference using standard analytic clustered standard errors, inference using standard errors on average treatment effects that account for sampling variation in covariates (unconditional standard errors), Ibragimov and Muller (2010) style cluster-robust inference, and randomization (permutation) inference for settings - most importantly few treated clusters - where cluster-asymptotic inference is unreliable (*permute()*). A fast exploratory mode (*fastnose*) skips standard error estimation entirely for specification search. 

**Shortcuts:** Description, Options, Working with Output, Runtime and What Drives It, Comparison with Related Estimators, Examples, Bugs and Assistance, Citations, Author Info and How to Cite, Disclaimer 

**Dependencies:** `wooldid` requires users have STATA 16 or higher installed, `reghdfe` version 5.7.3, `ftools` (for *reghdfe*), and `gtools`. Poisson estimation also requires the package `ppmlhdfe`. 

**Github Link:** <https://github.com/thegland/wooldid/> - the command *wooldid, update* will cause this program to update itself using the materials at this link. 

### Description of Estimation Approach

**wooldid** estimates various types of difference-in-differences treatment effects in the vein of Wooldridge (2025), with extensions to handle continuous treatments and estimation of effects by subgroup or treatment arm. The program does not currently accommodate cases where treatment is reversible -- once treated, cohorts must remain treated.

Broadly speaking, *wooldid* estimates difference-in-differences treatment effects using a two-step procedure. In step 1, *wooldid* estimates the underlying two-way fixed effects regression, which consists of (at baseline) a regression of the outcome variable on cohort fixed effects, time fixed effects, and a set of indicator variables used to flexibly capture the effect of treatment. Each indicator variable is 1 in a specific cohort-period (i-t) cell, and 0 otherwise. The slate of indicator variables spans all cohort-periods for which treatment effects are to be estimated. In step 2, *wooldid* completes a set of calculations using the `margins` command that convert the large slate of coefficients on the treatment indicator variables from the underlying regression in step 1 into treatment effects of interest. These margins calculations proceed by computing, for all treated observations, the difference between the predicted values from the underlying regression and the counterfactual predicted values that would be observed given each observation was not treated. Various averages of these differences are then presented to obtain various types of user-specified average treatment effects. To flesh this out, the underlying regression used is as follows: 

Y_jt = fe_i + fe_t + sum_over_it_in_Z(beta_it * TreatedFlag_it * Tz_it) 

where i indexes cohorts, t indexes time periods, j indexes individual units (possibly equivalent to cohorts, possibly more numerous than cohorts), and where Z is the set of all (i,t) pairs where i is a treated cohort and t is a treated period for cohort i. In the above regression, Tz_it is a dummy variable that is 1 if, for a given z=(iz,tz) in Z, i==iz and t==tz, TreatedFlag_it is a variable that is 1 if an observation is part of an (i,t) cell in Z and 0 otherwise, fe_i is a cohort fixed effect, fe_t is a time fixed effect, and Y_jt is the outcome variable. 

The output of the underlying Step 1 regression is then used for margins calculations in step 2. The exact margins calculation performed depends on the estimands specified by the user. The overall average treatment effect would be obtained by computing fitted values from the underlying regression among all treated observations (those where TreatedFlag_it is equal to 1), subtracting the counterfactual fitted values for these observations that set TreatedFlag_it to 0, and then taking the average of these differences (applying any sample weights). To target estimands other than the overall average treatment effect, this procedure can be modified by either introducing a set of weights (or modifying the pre-existing sample weights) when averaging the computed differences in the margins calculation or by restricting the sample so that the average only contains observations from a subgroup or time period of interest. For more on the different estimand-types available, see estimands.

With respect to inference, there are several approaches to standard error estimation available. The default estimates standard errors in the underlying regression and then obtains standard errors on the treatment effects produced by the margins calculation using the delta method. These errors are conditional standard errors in the sense that they are conditional on the data: the difference in fitted values potentially depends on the value of various covariates, and this approach takes these values as fixed when performing the margins calculation. Given these standard errors, p-values are then obtained using a t-distribution of same degrees of freedom as the residual number of degrees of freedom in the underlying regression model. The alternative is to have *margins* produce unconditional standard errors (see the option *unconditional* in `margins`), which account for sampling variation in covariates. For more on standard error computation, see standard_errors. 

This overall approach can be extended to cover many additional use cases with little or no modification. The user can switch from standard regression to poisson regression without requiring any essential modification to the regression equation or margins procedure. Additional controls and fixed effects can be added to the underlying regression without requiring adjustments to the margins calculation. Effects specific to particular subgroups may be estimated simply by performing the margins calculation within a specified subgroup. Where multiple subgroups are present, *wooldid* can use `margins` to calculate pairwise comparisons of the subgroup-specific effects and compute joint tests of the hypothesis that all subgroups have the same effect. This can be useful for tests of differences in treatment efficacy across treatment arms and can be used to replicate functionality that interaction terms in a traditional difference-in-differences would have provided. See subgroups for more. 

In a similar vein, event study style estimates that present treatment effects specific to various periods relative to treatment can be obtained by performing the margins calculation restricted to the subsample of observations corresponding with particular periods relative to treatment. If effects in periods prior to treatment are of interest, this requires modifying the underlying regression with additional treatment indicator variables for cohort-periods that correspond with the relative time period of interest in treated cohorts. The regression equation itself remains the same: only the set of (i,t) pairs appearing in Z changes for this approach. The margins calculations involved also remain similar, though may be performed on varying subsets of the data by relative period. When computing event study estimates, *wooldid* can supplement the standard treatment effect estimates with joint tests of the hypothesis that all pre-treatment relative time period effects are zero, as well as of the hypothesis that all relative time period effects fall on a single line (namely, the line of best fit through the estimated relative time period effects). For more on this topic, see event_study.

In terms of implementing event study-style estimates, *wooldid* offers two approaches. The default pooled reference period approach is conceptually similar to estimating results like normal, but with the treatment onset date for all treated cohorts pulled earlier in time by just enough periods to estimate all requested relative time period effects. With this approach, we add no more (i,t) pairs to Z than is necessary and we allow the reference period to potentially pool multiple periods together for each cohort. Event study estimates derived from this approach always have a normalized effect of 0 in the earliest period reported (the pooled reference period). The alternative approach is to use a fixed reference period, in which case Z is modified to include all (i,t) pairs for treated cohorts, except the pair corresponding with the period immediately prior to treatment (or corresponding with some other user specified period). In this case, the regression is saturated with i-t indicator variables and all treatment effects are estimated relative to the single specified reference period. 

With greater modification, the approach above can be extended to handle continuous treatment variables and treatments that come with an associated measure of treatment intensity or dosage. When cohorts are coarse -- i.e., there are multiple units within each cohort-period cell -- and when a continuous treatment variable varies within i-t cells, estimates of the effect of the continuous treatment variable can be computed within each treated i-t cell. To ensure treatment effect heterogeneity is adequately captured, a flexible function of the continuous treatment variable can be used to measure its effect within each i-t cell if needed. Note that this approach is a bit speculative and is not covered in Wooldridge (2025), though Wooldridge has [informally suggested](https://twitter.com/jmwooldridge/status/1695115782739435922?s=61&t=pXC6R4euc7LaN6MMB5VApQ) handling continuous treatments using interactions between treated cell indicators and the continuous treatment variable. 

The particular modification to the underlying regression used for estimation in the continuous treatment context is as follows: 

Y_jt = fe_i + fe_t + sum_over_it_in_Z(beta_it * TreatedFlag_it * Tz_it + TreatedFlag_it * Tz_it * f(Contreat_jt)) + g(f(Contreat_jt)) 

where Contreat_jt is a continuous treatment variable that potentially varies arbitrarily across observations, where f() is a flexible function of Contreat_jt, and where g() consists of interactions between various fixed effects-like indicator variables and f(Contreat_jt). Average treatment effects can be computed using the same margins calculation as before. The average marginal effect of the continuous treatment variable can be computed similar to the main margins calculation, but comparing differences in fitted values after modifying the variable Contreat_jt rather than modifying the variable TreatedFlag_it. In addition to overall and event study style estimates, *wooldid* can also compute average treatment effect estimates at varying points along the distribution of the continuous treatment variable. It can also report the effect of the continuous treatment variable with respect to a proportional change in the dose rather than a unit change. For more detail on options related to continuous treatments, see continuous_treatment. 

A final feature available in *wooldid* is its implementation of Ibragimov and Muller (2010) style cluster robust inference. This approach estimates the same underlying regression as normal, but then modifies the margins calculation significantly. In particular, for any treatment effect requested (be it an average treatment effect or an effect of a continuous treatment variable), this approach has the margins calculation compute that effect separately within each cluster. The final estimate presented is then the simple average of these cluster-specific estimates, with inference being conducted by t-testing the cluster-level estimates. For more on this approach and when it is appropriate to apply, see standard_errors. 

** Comment on Sample Formation, Cohorts Treated Prior to Sample Start:** The estimation sample *wooldid* uses may be more restricted than your overall sample: *wooldid* will drop observations with missing data, observations with 0-valued weights, observations from treated cohorts not observed prior to treatment (or not observed in the user-specified reference year if *esfixedbaseperiod* is specified), and observations from time periods no untreated (including not yet treated) units are observed. If the user requests that cohort-specific time trends be included, this restriction will extend to dropping cohorts that are not observed enough times to estimate a time trend using the pre-treatment data. 

** Comment on Cohorts Treated After Sample End:** By default, *wooldid* handles cohorts treated after the end of the sample identically to cohorts that have never been treated. The option *oostreatedcontrols()* allows the user to specify that cohorts of this type should instead be handled more like cohorts treated within-sample and thus that these cohorts should be allowed to contribute to pre-treatment relative time period-specific estimates in the event study context. Relatedly, when units may respond in anticipation of a known upcoming treatment, the option *anticipation(#)* treats the effective onset as *ttre* − #, so anticipation windows are not misclassified as clean pre-treatment periods. 

### Supporting features

### Supporting Features

`hist:ogramcohorteffects`: Produce a histogram of the cohort-specific treatment effects behind the requested estimand and return those effects in *e(histogramestimates)* for the user to manipulate. The effects are one per treated cohort *i* under every estimand - both margins calls average over *i* - and a run requesting both gets both sets of columns: the *att_it*/*att_itime* column is the cohort-level average of that cohort's period-by-period cell effects, taken under the cell weights, not a distribution over cohort-by-period cells. With `contreat()` a matching histogram of the average marginal effects is drawn as well and its column (*ame_i*, *ame_itime*) is appended to the returned matrix. 

Under `bootstrap()` and `permute()` the histogram outputs are the one reported surface still taken from the stage-1 analytic covariance rather than from the replications; a caution saying so prints with the results. 

`save:plots(string)`: Save all graphs generated to .gph files beginning with the name specified in the option. Also saves .dta files beginning with the same name containing the data used to generate any requested event study plots (one file per subgroup value under `subgroups()`) and, with *makecfxplots*, one *name*`_cfx.dta` holding every plotted continuous-treatment point, written on an ordinary analytic run as well as under `bootstrap()` or `permute()`, wherever there is plot data to write; on the analytic path it is written after the results are posted and draws no figure of its own. If that write fails, typically because a file of the name already exists and `replace` was not given, the run is not lost: a caution says the `.dta` is missing, and the figures, the `.gph` files, the estimates and every stored result are unaffected. Graphs and datasets generated have a suffix appended to the name specifying the exact graph/dataset generated. Everything is saved to Stata's working directory; the argument must be a plain name stem (paths are rejected by the option's syntax).

`replace`: Overwrite existing files when *wooldid* writes materials: the .gph and .dta files named by *saveplots()*, and the replication datasets named by `permsave()` and `bootsave()`. For those two the name is checked before the replications run and an existing file is a hard error (r(602)) rather than a silent skip; *replace* is what turns that error off. 

`sum:marystats`: Compute and present summary statistics of the outcome variable y and, if present, of the continuous treatment variable contreat. Also displays the event-study composition matrix *e(escomp)* itself - and, with `subgroups()`, each per-subgroup *e(escomp_s*sgcase*)* - together with its coverage scalars: the reference-normalization pool's N, treated-unit and onset-cohort counts and att-style weight mass, and, for the entire treated estimation sample, the N and att-style weight mass only (see *e(escomp_*)* under Stored Results). None of this composition material is computed, displayed or returned unless event-study horizons are requested (*esprelength()* + *espostlength()* > 0). Summary statistics are computed overall, within the treatment group by time period (post-treatment vs pre-treatment), and within the control group; under *contreatwithin* the control-group rows are omitted, and under *contreatcontrols(untreatedZero)* the continuous treatment's pre-treatment and control rows are omitted as well. If a fixed reference period is not being used and pre-treatment relative time period effects are requested, statistics on the size of the reference group relative to the rest of the pre-treatment period will also be shown. 

`pois:son`: Implement the specified *wooldid* command using poisson regression, achieved by switching from the regression command *reghdfe* to *ppmlhdfe* (or, where *unconditionalse* or *customvce()* already routes estimation through *regress* on explicit dummies, from *regress* to Stata's own *poisson*). Treatment effects are presented in terms of differences in linear predictions (the poisson *predict(xb)* option). Poisson regression is especially useful in situations where one would like to use log(y) as an outcome, but where y contains natural 0s, as well as when treatment and control groups are only on parallel trends in proportions (e.g., one group is always 2x the other). Poisson estimation accepts pweights but not aweights (refused with an error). Two further refusals apply: an outcome containing negative values is rejected with r(411), and `boottstat` may not be combined with poisson (r(198)), because the poisson replications are estimated on sampling weights, so a replication's own standard error is not the same functional of the resampled data as the observed one and the studentized bootstrap would be anti-conservative; the percentile, bias-corrected and Wald bootstrap families remain available. If *ppmlhdfe* fails on the initial specification with r(3200), *wooldid* retries once with the *ccc_absorb()* and continuous-treatment control terms moved out of the absorbed-effects list into the regression itself (a caution prints); results are equivalent up to numerical implementation. The retry is keyed on r(3200) alone; any other *ppmlhdfe* failure is re-raised unchanged, with no retry. The post-estimation collinearity screen refuses with r(322) when a treated cohort-period dummy has been dropped, and under poisson a dropped dummy whose cell has an outcome of zero on every row is perfect separation rather than multicollinearity: *wooldid* says so by name, giving the *i* value and the relative-time horizon, and exits r(322). The remedies are to coarsen *i*, to drop the cohort, or to specify *safety(off)*, which in this case does not merely silence the message: the run proceeds with the separated cohort-period cells excluded from the estimated effects, which is a different realized sample rather than the same one reported more loudly. A pre-existing variable named *_ppmlhdfe_d* is overwritten by *ppmlhdfe*'s own scaffolding on poisson runs and then removed by *wooldid*'s cleanup, except under *verbose*, which deliberately retains it alongside the retained full model - treat that name as reserved in your data.

## 2. Choosing i, t, ttre and weights

### The finest-i trap

The single most consequential choice in a wooldid call is the level of `i`. The regression carries one indicator per `i`-by-relative-period cell, so `i` sets how much treatment-effect heterogeneity the model allows, how many parameters it estimates, and what a cluster can see. The instinctive choice, `i` = the finest identifier in the data (the person, firm or county), is often the wrong one:

- **Runtime.** Cost is driven by the number of cohort-by-period cells, not by the number of observations, and it grows faster than linearly in that count (roughly its 1.3rd to 1.7th power, and about its 1.9th under `lattice()`). Five thousand units observed over an eight-horizon window is forty thousand interaction terms; that specification is slow at best and exceeds Stata's `maxvar` at worst, and wooldid cautions when `i` takes more than 100 values.
- **Inference.** With a unit-level `i`, every treated cohort-by-period cell is a single observation. Clustering at `i` (or anything coarser) then places each cell inside one cluster, and the cluster-robust covariance cannot see the cell's own variation: the analytic standard errors of the `att` rows are understated, the joint pre-trend and adversarial tests are not calibrated, the `ame` standard errors under `contreat()` are not identified, and the `gteffects` cells' intervals do not cover. The chapter on analytic inference explains the mechanism.
- **Randomization inference** requires `cluster()` to nest `i` (or `permonsetcohorts`), so with a unit-level `i` the reassignment unit can be no finer than the unit.

**The recommended default** is a coarse `i` with unit fixed effects: define `i` as the treatment onset cohort (or onset cohort by stratum, if effects plausibly differ across strata), put the unit identifier in `fe()`, and cluster at the unit level or at any level strictly finer than the cohort-by-period cells. The construction, from the package's worked example `wooldid_simple_example.do`:

```stata
gen ttre_groups = ttre
replace ttre_groups = 9999 if ttre == .      // never-treated need a positive integer code for i
wooldid y ttre_groups t ttre, att cluster(unit) fe(unit) espre(3) espost(3)
```

On a panel that is unbalanced inside its cohorts the `fe()` half of that recipe is what holds the point estimate fixed, not a modelling preference. Keep it.

**When a fine `i` is right:** the panel is small (tens of units); the estimand of interest is unit-level (`att_i` averages over units only when `i` is the unit); Ibragimov-Muller inference is wanted at the unit level; or you need to reproduce `did_imputation` with controls, which matches wooldid only in the fine-cohort case. Even then, prefer `robust` or a `cluster()` finer than the cell for the analytic run, and use `bootstrap()` or `permute()` for the tests.

### Requirements on i, t and ttre

- `i` must be positive integers. Codes above 16,777,216 stored as float are rounded (float cannot represent consecutive integers there), which silently merges cohorts, periods or IDs; wooldid cautions and the remedy is to regenerate the variable as double from its source.
- `t` is numeric and on the same grid as `ttre`: `t - ttre` must be an integer. Zero, negative and non-consecutive values are fine on the default path; under `unconditionalse`, `customvce()` or `interactivecontrols()` `t` enters as a factor variable and must be a non-negative integer. Gaps in the grid (a biennial panel) make the requested event-study window realize fewer horizons than asked for.
- `ttre` must be constant within `i`, and never-treated cohorts are coded as missing (`permute()` additionally requires plain `.`, not an extended missing value). Cohorts treated before the sample starts are dropped; cohorts treated after it ends are controls unless `oostreatedcontrols()` says otherwise. `anticipation()` is expressed in the units of `t`.
- Some cohort must be never-treated or first treated after the estimation window, unless `contreatwithin` or `safety(off)` is in force.

### Weights and the sample

Analytic and probability weights are accepted; frequency weights are refused. `poisson` takes pweights but not aweights. `customweightmultiplier()` multiplies the weights used in the margins averaging only, never the regression weights, and must be non-negative. `unconditionalse` cautions with aweights and is refused with the cell-weighted estimands. The `if` expression keeps only rows where it equals exactly 1, where Stata's own qualifier keeps any nonzero value; write `if flag != 0` for Stata's behaviour. Observations with a missing outcome, weight, continuous treatment or user-supplied lattice value are dropped and counted in the header.

The syntax table below is the help file's, for reference.

### From the help file

### Syntax

` wooldid` *y i t ttre* [if] [aw/pw=weights] [`,` estimands standard_errors supporting_features event_study subgroup_effects continuous_treatment controls_and_FE technical_features ]

**The *if* qualifier:** `wooldid` keeps the observations for which the *if* expression evaluates to exactly 1, where Stata's own *if* qualifier keeps every nonzero value, missing included. The two agree for comparisons such as `if x > 5`, which return 0 or 1, and differ for a bare flag variable holding other values: `if flag` excludes the rows where `flag` is 2 (or missing), which Stata would keep. The rule is deliberate - it is what stops missing and out-of-range values entering the sample - and it applies identically to the estimation sample, to the randomization design under permute() and to the bootstrap frame, so the three always agree. `wooldid` prints a Caution naming the count whenever the expression is nonzero but not exactly 1 somewhere, and those observations are included in *e(N_dropped)*. Write `if flag != 0` for Stata's behaviour. 

- ***y*** Outcome variable. 

- ***i*** Cohort identifier; must consist of integers greater than 0. Cohorts can be fine (i = individual units) or coarse (i = collections of individual units), but should be no coarser than to consist of treatment onset cohorts. Coarser cohort definitions tend to enable faster estimation. For a typical run, prefer treatment onset cohorts as *i* and, on the default analytic path, cluster below that level (see What level to cluster at); `permute()` is the one engine that still ties the two choices together. It draws one onset per cluster, so it needs `cluster()` to nest *i* unless `permonsetcohorts` asserts that *i* **is** the treatment onset grouping, in which case each replication rebuilds *i* from the drawn onsets and a finer `cluster()` is admissible. Choose *i* by the treatment heterogeneity the estimands should average over, not by the level at which you want fixed effects - those are specified separately in `fe()`, so a coarse *i* combines with unit-level ones and, on a panel not balanced inside its cohorts, requires them, since the permanent differences between the units of a cohort are otherwise absent from the model and a shifting cell composition moves the point estimate - and go finer only where that heterogeneity, or a `subgroups()` variable varying inside a cohort, requires it. 

- ***t*** Time period identifier; must be numeric and on the same grid as *ttre* (*t* minus *ttre* must be an integer wherever *ttre* is nonmissing - see the *ttre* entry). Integer values are recommended, but the value itself only labels periods on the default estimation path: zero, negative, and non-consecutive values (gaps) are all fine. With `uncond:itionalse`, `customvce()`, or `inter:activecontrols()`, *t* enters the model as a native Stata factor variable and must then consist of non-negative integers no larger than 2,147,483,647 (Stata's factor-variable cap): recode large calendar codes such as %tc datetimes to small integers first.

- ***ttre*** Variable containing cohort-specific dates of treatment onset; missing indicates never-treated cohorts, cohorts treated before the sample begins are dropped. Binary designs require a comparison group: some units must be never-treated or first treated after the estimation window (an informative error is raised otherwise; see *oostreatedcontrols*). Store *ttre* (and *t*) as `double` when onset codes are large: 8-digit calendar dates exceed float precision, so a float-stored onset variable is rounded before *wooldid* sees it, silently merging adjacent cohorts. *wooldid* prints a caution when *i*, *t* or *ttre* is stored as float with values above 16,777,216 (2^24, beyond which float cannot hold consecutive integers); the values must be regenerated as double from their source, since recasting after the fact does not recover them. *ttre* must take a single value (or be missing on every row) within each group defined by *i* over the estimation sample: *wooldid* builds relative time row by row from *ttre*, so an *i* group carrying different onsets on different rows - including an onset recorded only from the onset period onward, with missing values pre-treatment - would be silently split across cohorts. *wooldid* verifies this and exits with an informative error if it is violated (bypass with *safety(off)*). Onset times must also sit on the same grid as *t* - *t* minus *ttre* must be an integer wherever *ttre* is nonmissing (integer spacing is not required: biennial or five-year panels are fine for estimation, but see *esprelength()* - the event-study window is counted in relative-period slots, so such a panel realizes only every second or fifth horizon of a requested window). *wooldid* rejects fractional offsets at parse time (bypass with *safety(off)*). If every offending onset falls outside the usable estimation window (pre-sample, or beyond the last observed period), the rejection softens to a Caution, since such onsets are dropped or treated as never-treated downstream anyway. 

**Options by Purpose:** 

- **Specifying the Estimand(s):** `att` `att_it` `att_i` `att_itime` 

- **Standard Errors:** `clus:ter(variable(s)[numerical])` `rob:ust` `uncond:itionalse` `customvce(customstring)` `imp:values` `fast:nose` `nofastcollapse` `simult:aneousbands`

- ** *shared by both replication engines:*** `level(#)` `dots(int)` `rseed(#)`

- ** *permutation only:*** `perm:ute(method)` `permreps(int)` `permex:act` `permstr:ata(variable)` `permpr:obs(spec)` `permts:tat` `permt:ci` `permsa:ve(name)` `perman:alyticse` `permab:sp` `permsi:de(left|right)` `permraw:p` `permstep:down` `permonset:cohorts`

- ** *bootstrap only:*** `boot:strap(mode)` `reps(int)` `ties` `bootalp:ha(spec)` `bootd:f(spec)` `bootts:tat` `bootdisp:lay(spec)` `bootsa:ve(name)`

- **Supporting Features:** `hist:ogramcohorteffects` `save:plots(filename_stem)` `replace` `sum:marystats` `pois:son` 

- **Event Study Estimation:** `esf:ixedbaseperiod` `espre:length(int>=0)` `espost:length(int>=0)` `esr:elativeto(-int)` `joint:tests` `adv:ersarial` `altmethod_adversarial` `makep:lots` `oos:treatedcontrols(int>=0)` `antic:ipation(int>=0)` `pretrendpow:er(numlist)` 

- **Subgroup Effects:** `sub:groups(variable[>=0 int])` `suppress:sgprimaryeffects` 

- **Continuous Treatments:** `contreat(variable[numerical])` `contreatelas:ticitytype(dydx or dyex)` `contreatw:ithin` `contreatp:oly(+int)` `lattice(variable[+int] OR latticetype latticenum)` `latticeig:noreweights` `makec:fxplots` `cfxlattice(variable[+int])` `cfxplott:ypes(post, pre, and/or integers specifying relative time periods)` `makecfxplotsbysg` `cfxplotnose` `contreatc:ontrols(conttreatcontroltype)` 

*-latticetype*: *all*, *byTime*, *byTreated*, *byTtre*, *byCohort*, *byTreatedByTime*, *byTtreByTime*, *byCohortByTime*, *baseTreated*, *baseTreatedRef*, *baseTreatedPost*, or *baseTreatedPre* 

*-latticenum*: integer > 1 

*-conttreatcontroltype*: *untreatedZero*, or one or more of *basic*, *byTreated*, *byTtre*, *byCohort*, *byTime* 

- **Controls and Fixed Effects:** `control:s(fv varlist)` `fe(fv varlist)` `timetrend:s` `coarse:cohortcontrols(fv-required varlist)` `inter:activecontrols(varlist)` 

- **Technical Features:** `update` `customw:eightmultiplier(variable[>=0 numerical])` `clean:matrices` `regtol(+int)` `ccno:absorb` `ccc_absorb(varlist[numerical])` `ver:bose` `gte:ffects` `emptycellsoverride` `safety(on|off)` `oldsyntax`

## 3. Estimands

Four averages of the same cell effects are available, and the choice is about weighting, not about the regression:

| option | what is averaged | weight on a cell effect | use when |
|---|---|---|---|
| `att` (default) | every treated observation | its sample weight | the policy-relevant average over the treated population |
| `att_it` | cohort-by-period cells | equal per cell | cells should count equally regardless of size |
| `att_i` | cohorts | equal per cohort | cohorts (units, if `i` is the unit) should count equally |
| `att_itime` | cohorts, each as a simple mean over its periods | equal per cohort, then equal per period | as `att_i`, but with a cohort's periods weighted equally |

Several options treat the cell-weighted estimands (`att_it`, `att_i`, `att_itime`) differently from `att`: `unconditionalse` refuses them unless `safety(off)`, and `bootstrap(bayes)` refuses them whatever `safety()` is set to; `makecfxplots` draws for `att` only unless `safety(off)`; `impvalues` reports the cluster-equally-weighted mean whatever estimand was named. In event studies the three cell-weighted estimands collapse to one horizon block, reported as `att_it`. Under `contreat()` each estimand is accompanied by the corresponding average marginal effect of the dose (`ame`, `ame_it`, `ame_i`, `ame_itime`). Under `poisson` every effect is on the linear-index (log) scale.

### From the help file

### Specifying the Estimand(s)

`att`: Estimate average treatment on the treated (ATT) effects; this option is the default and may be combined with options att_it, att_i, and att_itime to estimate multiple different effect types. ATT effects are computed by performing the margins calculation (see step 2 in Description for more on the margins calculation) using whatever weights (if any) are used in the main regression command, multiplied by the *customweightmultiplier()* variable if one is specified - that multiplier enters the margins weights only and is deliberately not folded into the regression weight.

`att_it`: Estimate simple averages of the i-t cell-specific treatment effects (ATT_it). The treatment effect in each cohort-period is calculated via the margins calculation using any weights that may apply to the observations falling in each i-t cell. The final ATT_it estimate is the simple, unweighted mean of all of the cohort-period-specific estimates. This is done by reweighting the data used in the margins calculation (after estimation of the underlying regression) by w/W_it where w is an observation's sample weight and W_it is the total sample weight associated with all observations appearing in a given i-t cell. If no weights are specified, w is taken to have a value of 1 for all observations. If cohorts are fine and thus each i-t cell contains only one observation, this is equivalent to ignoring any specified sample weights when performing margins calculations. 

`att_i`: Estimate the simple average of cohort-specific treatment effects (ATT_i). The treatment effect for each cohort is calculated using the margins calculation and any sample weights. The final ATT_i estimate is the simple, unweighted mean of the cohort-specific treatment effects. ATT_i effects are computed by performing the margins calculation, but reweighting observations by either w/W_ipre or w/W_ipost, where w is an observation's sample weight, W_ipre is the total sample weight associated with cohort-i in the pre-treatment period (but outside the reference period, and, under *timetrends*, outside the period immediately preceding it, which *timetrends* normalizes to zero as well), and W_ipost is the total sample weight associated with cohort-i in the post-treatment period. Each observation is then reweighted based on whether it falls in the pre- or post-treatment period. If weights are not used, w is taken to have a value of 1 for all observations.

`att_itime`: Estimate the simple average of cohort-specific simple average treatment effects (ATT_itime). Treatment effect calculation here begins like for *att_it*, obtaining treatment effects for individual cohort-periods of interest using weights. Then, cohort-specific effects are obtained by taking the simple mean of the cohort-period-specific effects. The overall presented ATT_itime estimate is then equal to the simple, unweighted mean of these cohort-specific effects. ATT_itime effects are calculated by performing the margins calculation, but reweighting observations by w/(W_it * T_iprepost), where w is an observation's sample weight, W_it is the amount of sample weight falling in a given i-t cell, and T_iprepost contains (depending on the time period) either the number of post-treatment time periods in which a cohort-i is observed or the number of pre-treatment, non-reference period time periods in which a cohort i is observed. If weights are not used, w is taken to have a value of 1 for all observations.

Each of these four options names a weighting concept rather than a single number. Under *contreat()* every requested estimand is therefore reported twice on that same weighting: once as the effect of the binary onset (*att*, *att_it*, *att_i*, *att_itime*), and once as the average marginal effect of the dose (*ame*, *ame_it*, *ame_i*, *ame_itime*). Both families are displayed, and both are stored under the same names with *att* replaced by *ame*. 

**Options that treat the cell-weighted estimands differently:** because *att_it*, *att_i* and *att_itime* give each treatment cell equal weight rather than each observation, several options restrict or reinterpret them. *unconditionalse* is refused with an error under default safety (*safety(off)* proceeds). *bootstrap(standard)* re-identifies the duplicated copies of a resampled cluster for these estimands, so that a twice-drawn cluster contributes its cells twice rather than pooling them into one. Where *cluster()* nests *i* the copies become distinct units, and *i* is replaced by the fresh unit code wherever its name appears in *fe()*, *controls()*, *interactivecontrols()*, *coarsecohortcontrols()* or *ccc_absorb()*, so a covariate keyed to unit identity separates the copies the way the cell dummies do (a variable that encodes *i* under another name, or was merged in on *i*, is not found and keeps its codes across the copies; *e(boot_covsub)* counts the rewritten terms); where it is finer than *i*, or cuts across it, the copies stay inside their cohort and the replication's own standard error clusters on the drawn cluster identity. Which of the two applies is decided once for the run, on the cluster values the estimation sample keeps, so a single *i* that straddles two `cluster()` values puts every unit under the second rule; *e(boot_nstraddle)* counts those units and *e(boot_reid)* records which rule was taken. With no *cluster()* at all the resampling is observation-level. *bootstrap(bayes)* is refused for them whatever *safety()* is set to, because cluster reweighting cannot resample treatment cells and the standard errors would omit the cell-sampling variance component. A *subgroups()* variable that splits a unit across subgroups over time is refused whatever the estimand, and under *safety(off)* such a unit enters each subgroup weighted by its share of sample weight there, which is not the equal unit weighting *att_i* and *att_itime* otherwise imply. Finally, *makecfxplots* draws its continuous-treatment plots, and returns the *e(cfxatt_*)* and *e(cfxame_*)* matrices, for the *att* weighting only: for *att_it*, *att_i* and *att_itime* it prints a caution and produces neither the plots nor the matrices unless *safety(off)* is specified (*contreatwithin* with *contreat()* sets safety off internally and so has the same effect).

## 4. Analytic inference

### Decision rules

1. **Cluster below the level of `i`.** The intended practice is a `cluster()` whose groups nest inside cohorts, so that every treated cohort-by-period cell contains several clusters. With a coarse `i` (onset cohorts) and units in `fe()`, clustering at the unit is the natural choice and satisfies this.
2. **If you must cluster at or above `i`** (the design has few clusters and they are the cohorts), expect the following: the `att` rows' analytic standard errors are somewhat understated, without a caution; `jointtests` p-values are severely oversized (cautioned) and so are `adversarial` p-values (not cautioned); under `contreat()` the `ame` standard errors are not identified and are reported as missing; pairwise subgroup contrasts can be degenerate and are reported without inference; `gteffects` cells are understated (cautioned). Use `bootstrap()` (with `boottstat` for the joint tests) or `permute()` for inference in that design.
3. **`robust`** is appropriate when errors are independent across observations, which is rare in a panel; it does not carry the cell-containment problem, and the analytic `ame` standard errors are identified under it.
4. **`unconditionalse`** when sampling variation in covariates or in the dose matters (small samples, influential weights). It runs through `regress` or `poisson` on explicit dummies, so `fe()` cannot be absorbed (write `i.` prefixes into `controls()`), it is slower, it refuses the cell-weighted estimands unless `safety(off)`, and it turns the default cell collapse off.
5. **`customvce()`** for a variance estimator `reghdfe` does not offer; it has the same regress-path consequences as `unconditionalse`, but keeps the default cell collapse.
6. **`impvalues`** (Ibragimov-Muller) when clusters are few and heterogeneous in size and their estimates can be assumed independent. It changes the estimand to the cluster-equally-weighted mean, refuses subgroups, the joint tests, `makecfxplots` and the replication engines, and returns no event-study covariance.
7. **`level()`** sets every reported interval, including the plot bands and the replication engines' intervals; `pretrendpower()`'s pre-test sizes stay at 5 percent.
8. **`fastnose`** produces point estimates only and is for specification search; re-run without it before reporting.

The rest of this chapter is the help file's account of the analytic options, the cell-containment mechanism, and the p-value conventions.

### From the help file

### Standard Errors

**What level to cluster at.** The intended practice is to cluster **below** the level of *i* - at a level whose units are nested inside cohorts, so that a treated cohort-by-period cell contains several clusters. That third one is not confined to clustering: `robust` with a unit-level *i* leaves each treated cell holding a single observation whose residual its own dummy drives to zero, and the same panel then gives 0.64 and 70%. A run that asks for `gteffects` counts the affected cells, prints a caution and returns the count in *e(gt_ncontained)*. There is no plug-in correction - at that level the analytic covariance is the wrong object rather than a mis-scaled one - so the remedies are a finer `cluster()`, or `bootstrap()` or `permute()`, which build their inference from replications instead. Reaching a finer `cluster()` usually means coarsening *i* to the treatment onset grouping, and on a panel holding one row per unit per period that also gives up the unit fixed effects a finer *i* had been supplying. Put them back with `fe()`: the shape to reach for in a typical run is *i* at the treatment onset cohort, `cluster()` at the unit, and `fe()` naming the unit. On the `regress`-based paths of *unconditionalse* and *customvce()*, which cannot absorb `fe()`, write the same effects into *controls()* with your own *i.* prefixes instead. On a balanced panel the `fe()` term changes nothing; on an unbalanced one it matters, since without it the permanent heterogeneity inside a cohort goes unabsorbed - which costs precision always, and moves the point estimate whenever the holes in the panel are related to treatment. Those two remedies now compose: a `cluster()` finer than *i* is accepted under `bootstrap(standard)` with the cell-weighted estimands and under `boottstat`, and under `permute()` with `permonsetcohorts`. *impvalues* is the one exception to all of this, and it is enforced rather than advised: it wants the opposite geometry and refuses a `cluster()` finer than *i* outright (see `impvalues`). 

`clus:ter(clusvars)`: Variable(s) specifying clustering level. Several variables give multi-way clustering on the default estimation path; *unconditionalse*, *impvalues*, `permute()` and `bootstrap()` each require a single cluster variable and refuse a multi-way *cluster()* with an error. Clustered standard errors are computed analytically on the default estimation path; under `bootstrap()` the reported standard errors are replication quantities, under `permute()` without `permtstat` or `permanalyticse` no analytic standard error is computed at all and the reported inference is the permutation distribution's, and under *impvalues* the reported standard error is the cross-cluster Ibragimov-Muller one. A cluster variable that leaves fewer than two clusters in the estimation sample stops the run with an error, since cluster-robust inference needs at least two of them; the count is taken after *wooldid* has formed its sample, so a variable that looks adequate in the raw data can still fail here. The message names the three ways out: a finer cluster variable, *robust*, or *fastnose*, which asks `margins` for no variance at all and is exempt from the check. The same gate stops a run that is left with no residual degrees of freedom whatever. By default, regression estimation using this option will underlying rely on either `reghdfe` or, for poisson, `ppmlhdfe`. 

`rob:ust`: Heteroskedasticity robust standard errors; relevant only if cluster() is not specified. By default, regression estimation using this option will underlyingly rely on either *reghdfe* or, for poisson, *ppmlhdfe*. 

Note on degrees of freedom: the default estimation path and the `regress`-based path used by *customvce()* and *unconditionalse* apply different rules for fixed effects nested within the clustering variable, so the same specification can return slightly different standard errors depending on which path it takes. The divergence is conservative on the `regress` path rather than anti-conservative, and it does not generalise to the non-cluster variance strings, which is why wooldid documents the difference rather than rescaling one path onto the other. 

`uncond:itionalse`: Compute standard errors that account for sampling variation in covariates. With `cluster()`, a single cluster variable is supported: the variance comes from *margins, vce(unconditional)*, which accepts one. This option also forces *wooldid* to abandon high dimensional fixed effect estimation packages for the underlying two-way fixed effect regression in favor of the commands *regress* and *poisson*. As such, this option should be slower to use on average. This option requires specifying either robust or clustered standard errors (a robust or clustered vce supplied via *customvce()* also satisfies this requirement, as do pweights, which imply robust standard errors). Under the default *safety()* setting it is refused with *att_i*, *att_it* and *att_itime*, whose unconditional standard errors are unreliable (*safety(off)* proceeds anyway), and it cautions with *aweight*s and with *fe()* or *ccc_absorb()*, which the `regress` path cannot absorb - specify those as *controls()* with your own *i.* prefixes. It is refused outright with *impvalues*, *fastnose*, `permute()` and `bootstrap()`, and it turns the default cell collapse off, since *margins* checks the collapsed row count against *e(N)* when building the unconditional variance. 

`customvce()`: Forces *wooldid* to switch to using either the `regress` or `poisson` command for underlying regression estimation, and then passes the user specified text without modification into the *vce()* option for these commands. This allows specifying a broader set of standard error calculation methods than allowed by the default high dimensional fixed effects estimation packages used by *wooldid*. Not compatible with *robust* or *cluster()*: specify the clustering or robustness desired inside the *vce()* text itself. Also refused with *impvalues* and with `bootstrap()`, whose reported standard errors would be replication quantities rather than the custom VCE's. It cannot be combined with `permute()` either, though by construction rather than by a screen of its own: `permute()` requires a single *cluster()* variable to randomize onsets over, and *customvce()* refuses *cluster()*, so the pair cannot be written down. Specifying the clustering inside the *vce()* text does not get you there, because it is the *cluster()* variable itself that the permutation engine needs. With *fe()* or *ccc_absorb()* *wooldid* cautions, since the `regress` path it switches to cannot absorb them - specify those as *controls()* with your own *i.* prefixes. 

`imp:values`: Implements Ibragimov and Muller (2010) style cluster-robust inference for all estimates. The idea behind the Ibragimov and Muller approach is to compute cluster-specific treatment effect estimates, present their average as one's final treatment effect estimate, and conduct inference by t-testing the cluster-specific estimates against 0. Using IM-style inference requires specifying a single cluster variable and is not compatible with the *unconditionalse* option. Cluster at the level of *i* or above it, where the method is sound and where it outperforms the analytic interval. This is the opposite of what the default analytic path wants (see What level to cluster at), and the two cannot be satisfied at once: a design that needs a cluster below *i* wants `bootstrap()` or `permute()` rather than *impvalues*. The screen counts levels of *i*, and levels of *i* crossed with `cluster()`, over the treated event window only, so the common idiom of pooling every never-treated unit into a single level of *i* does not trip it. It is a sufficient condition rather than a necessary one, so a panel in which only a few units change cluster mid-panel (a migration, a crosswalk revision, a firm changing owner) is refused as well, although its inference is barely affected; the screen stands whatever `safety()` is set to. IM-style inference changes *wooldid*'s target estimand, and it does so in every run rather than in special cases: the procedure averages the cluster-specific estimates with **equal** weight, so what it reports is the cluster-equally-weighted mean, whatever estimand was requested and whatever weights that estimand ordinarily carries. Clustering at the cohort level is where this becomes visible - *att* then yields the same effect estimates as *att_i* - and *cluster()* at the level of *i* with *att_i* is the pairing that is coherent by construction, since the cluster-equally-weighted mean is what IM computes in any case. The equal weighting is not an implementation choice that could be reweighted away: it is what earns the *q*-1 degrees of freedom below, so trading it for the requested estimand's weights would cost the inference that motivates the method. Note that *wooldid* does not adjust the weights used for computing different estimands when IM-style inference is requested: in general, IM-style inference will yield incorrect results for non-att estimands if clusters cross-cut cohorts or (worse) cohort-time period cells. IM-style inference is not currently allowed with the use of subgroups, the option *jointtests*, or the option *makecfxplots*, nor with *adversarial*, *customvce()*, *fastnose*, *pretrendpower()*, `permute()` or `bootstrap()`: each of these combinations is refused with an error. Each reported row's test statistic, p-value and confidence interval are on the t distribution with *q*-1 degrees of freedom, where *q* is the number of clusters that contributed a non-missing estimate to that row, so *q* can differ from row to row. *e(df_r)* is set to the SMALLEST of those degrees of freedom, so a `test`, `lincom` or `esttab` run by hand on *e(V)* is conservative relative to the printed table rather than narrower than it; the range is stored in *e(imdf_min)* and *e(imdf_max)* whether or not the degrees of freedom are common, and when they are not common the run additionally says so under the table. The stage-1 regression's residual degrees of freedom - which the *gteffects* cells still use - are kept as hidden *e(df_r_analytic)*. Every IM *margins* call runs with *nose*, so the event-study b/V capture never fires: *e(b_es_)*, *e(V_es_)* and *e(numpre_)* are not returned and the *honestdid* and *pretrends* handoffs have no inputs. *e(wdid_vcvstate)* therefore reads *analytic-diagonal* rather than *analytic-blocks* - the within-family event-study covariance blocks an ordinary run splices into *e(V)* are absent here, so every off-diagonal of *e(V)* is zero. 

`fast:nose`: Fast exploratory mode. Under *fastnose*: *e(V)* is not posted (*e(properties)* is *b*, and the disclosure marker *e(wdid_vcvstate)* reads *absent*), so *test*, *lincom*, *_se[]*, *estat vce* and anything else needing a variance-covariance matrix will refuse. *esttab* and *estout* do **not** refuse, and under a bare *fastnose* they print a coefficient with an empty inference row beneath their usual footer, so read the table rather than trusting the footer. Under the `permute()` fast-mode default they do better than that: the run posts *e(p)*, *e(ci_l)* and *e(ci_u)* under the names *estout* reads **ahead** of anything derived from *e(V)*, so `esttab, p` and `esttab, ci` carry the run's real permutation p-values and shift intervals, with truthful footers, and a plain `esttab` tables the coefficients. No *e(se)* is posted there and none is invented: a permutation run has no standard error to report; *e(V_es_)* is not returned, so *honestdid* and Roth-style diagnostics have no covariance to work from, while *e(b_es_)* and *e(numpre_)* **are** returned - the estimates are meaningful even where their uncertainty is unavailable, and a Caution per family says so (under the `permute()` fast-mode default none of the three is returned); no joint tests of any kind are computed (including the subgroup homogeneity tests); the one exception is the subgroup-by-horizon margins call, which keeps its variance so that the estimability screen described under *subgroups()* still drops a non-estimable horizon (fast mode therefore reports the same event-study rows as the full run, at a small cost per subgroup); the continuous-treatment-effects plot matrices carry only the point-estimate column, exactly as under *cfxplotnose*; and event-study plots are drawn without confidence bands. `simultaneousbands` is accepted rather than refused, but no band is built - the analytic engine needs the event-study covariance fast mode does not compute - and a caution says so; the pointwise interval columns are unaffected and no *e(esband_*)* results are returned. Not compatible with *unconditionalse*, *impvalues*, *pretrendpower()* or `bootstrap()` (each is refused with an error), nor with *permtstat* or *permtci*, which randomize on t statistics fast mode does not compute. *adversarial* and *jointtests* are likewise refused, **except** under the `permute()` fast-mode default described next (no `permtstat`, no `permanalyticse`), where fast mode is already in force and naming it is accepted rather than an error. Under `permute()` without `permtstat` the observed run takes this fast path by default - the permutation inference does not consume the analytic standard errors - and `permanalyticse` restores them; the adversarial and joint-test surfaces are then permutation quantities built after the replications, not the analytic ones fast mode lacks. Point estimates can differ from a full run in roughly the last digit of precision (absolute differences on the order of 1e-15; relative differences are typically similar but can reach roughly 1e-13 on subgroup pairwise contrasts, whose differencing amplifies rounding, up to roughly 1e-14 under *timetrends*, and larger still in relative terms where an estimate is itself near zero), because *margins* follows a different computational path when the variance is skipped. 

`nofastcollapse`: Turn the default cell collapse off, so that every *margins* call is evaluated on the full estimation sample. The option changes the summation order only, not the estimator. Use it when you need byte-for-byte reproduction of an archived result, when you are comparing output across runs at the last digit, or when memory is tight and the extra copy of the data held during the collapsed window is unwelcome. The price is the run time the collapse would have saved, quantified under **When it pays** below. It is forwarded to `permute()` and `bootstrap()` replications. The rest of this entry describes what you are turning off. 

**The cell collapse.** *wooldid* speeds up its *margins* calls by evaluating them on one row per cohort-by-period-by-treatment cell instead of on every observation. *wooldid*'s estimands are weighted averages of cell-level treatment effects, and within the conditions listed below the treatment derivative is constant inside such a cell, so the collapsed row - carrying the cell's summed weight - represents the whole cell exactly. The regression itself is untouched and still runs on the full data, so the coefficients, the variance matrix and the clustering are unaffected; only the averaging step that *margins* performs is handed a smaller dataset. The full data is restored before any plotting or display work. **Continuous treatments are covered too**: with *contreat()* the collapsed row additionally carries the cell's weighted mean of the treatment variable, which reproduces every reported effect exactly because each of them is linear in the dose inside a cell - the treatment-dummy derivative is *delta + beta*dose*, the dose derivative (*ame*) is constant, and *contreatelasticitytype(dyex)* is proportional to the dose. The forms that are **not** linear in the dose are listed below and turn the collapse off. **The collapse is on by default** whenever the request permits it, so there is nothing to type to obtain it, and `nofastcollapse` is the only way to change that. Where a request is ineligible the collapse is skipped and *wooldid* says so, in a caution naming the option responsible, as described under *What turns the collapse off*. The collapse composes with *fastnose*, is applied inside `permute()` and `bootstrap()` replications, and is recorded in the hidden macro *e(wdid_fcol)* (1 when it was applied, 0 when it was not), beside the scalar *e(wdid_fcncell)* - the collapsed cell count - and the macro *e(wdid_fcskip)*, which names the reason when an ineligible run did not collapse; *verbose* reports the decision as the run proceeds. 

**When it pays.** The saving grows with the number of observations per cell and with the size of the regression, and is unaffected by the choice of standard errors. On a 30,000-observation administrative panel with roughly 500 rows per cell it removed 35-60% of total run time; on a 475,000-row version, 56%. It pays for itself inside a single *margins* call at that scale - a bare *att* is already enough, with no event-study horizons requested: a vanilla *att* run with *contreat()* was 1.24 times faster on a 4,000-observation sample, 1.25 times on a 29,000-observation one and 1.33 times on a 145,000-observation one, so building the cell means does not eat the saving. Adding event-study horizons makes it a better deal rather than a worse one, and so does asking for more estimands: the same 29,000-observation run went to 2.0 times faster with all four. It gains little when there are only a handful of rows per cell (roughly four or fewer), where the collapse can cost slightly more than it saves, and it helps least on designs with very many cohorts and periods, where the absorbing regression rather than *margins* dominates the run. See Runtime and what drives it. 

**Results typically agree to machine precision but are not bit-identical.** Collapsing changes the order in which *margins* accumulates its averages, and the controls' contribution cancels algebraically between the treated and untreated predictions without cancelling bit for bit. If you diff output across runs, expect low-order digits to differ between a collapsed and an uncollapsed run; if you need byte-identical reproductions of a result produced without the collapse, run with `nofastcollapse`, which restores the summation order but not a computation that has itself changed since. 

On a panel whose predictions are of order 10, that is about 2e-15. Note this scales with the prediction, not with the estimate: an outcome with a very large mean raises *|xb|* and the absolute differences with it. 

Because the bound is absolute, what it means in **relative** terms depends on how big the estimate is. An ordinary coefficient - say an ATT of order 0.5 - agrees to roughly 15 significant digits (relative differences around 3e-15). An estimate sitting near zero, such as a small pre-treatment coefficient of order 0.01, can show a relative difference around 1e-13, purely because the denominator is small rather than because the error is larger. P-values in a far tail move more in relative terms - a p-value of 2e-07 moved by about 3e-11 relative - which is a property of tail probabilities, not of this option. 

**Continuous treatments carry a second rounding step, and a looser bound.** The figures above are for the binary case, where the collapsed row only re-orders the summation. With *contreat()* the row also carries the cell's weighted mean of the dose, which is itself a rounded quantity, so the bound is set by that mean rather than by one unit in the last place of the prediction. Two decimal orders of that headroom are spent on near-zero cells: an ordinary coefficient still agrees to about 15 significant digits. As in the binary case, no digit *wooldid* displays is affected. 

**Where *margins* is itself inexact, the agreement is looser.** With sampling weights, *controls()* and an event-study window, the collapsed and uncollapsed runs can differ by up to about 1e-6 relative on standard errors - enough to move the last printed digit of a standard error - and by more in relative terms on a p-value far in the tail; the point estimates still agree to machine precision. Neither run is exact there. *margins* builds the delta-method Jacobian by finite differences, and on these specifications that Jacobian carries rounding residue in columns whose exact derivative is zero. The residue depends on the control values of the rows *margins* is handed, so the collapse, which hands it one row per cell, draws a different residue rather than adding an error of its own: against an exactly computed Jacobian, either run can be the one further off. It is not specific to one estimation path: the largest differences measured, about 1e-6 relative on standard errors, were on the default *reghdfe* path with `cluster()`, *contreat()*, *controls()*, *timetrends* and sampling weights, and under *customvce()* the largest was about 2e-7. 

**When should you care?** For almost all work, not at all: outside the case just described no digit *wooldid* displays is affected, and the differences are several orders of magnitude below any sampling uncertainty you are reporting. Two situations warrant attention. First, if you compare output byte-for-byte across runs, or archive results for exact reproduction - then be deliberate about whether the collapse is on or off (`nofastcollapse`) in every run you compare, and bear in mind that a request outside the eligible set is run uncollapsed under a caution, and that a *subgroups()* run can switch to the full data part-way through (see below; *e(wdid_fcguard)* records it). Second, if your analysis differences two nearly equal estimates, or otherwise divides by a quantity that is near zero, since that amplifies the relative size of any rounding difference. 

A useful calibration for the second case: merely permuting the order of the rows in your dataset - leaving the data otherwise untouched, with the collapse off - moves the same near-zero coefficient about 19 times **more** than the collapse does. So if a conclusion is sensitive at this scale, it is already sensitive to the order your observations happen to be stored in, which is worth knowing whether or not the collapse is in use. 

**What turns the collapse off: the complete list.** There are three ways the collapse does not run, and this paragraph is the single place that enumerates them. **(1) You turn it off**, with `nofastcollapse`, and *wooldid* says nothing further about it. **(2) The request is ineligible**, in which case the collapse is **skipped** and the run proceeds on the full data exactly as it does under `nofastcollapse`. That skip is announced: *wooldid* prints a caution naming the option responsible and what it breaks, since a run that quietly declines to collapse is a run whose timing and last digits both change for a reason you cannot see. *safety(off)* does **not** silence the caution, and neither does anything but asking for `nofastcollapse` yourself. **(3) A *subgroups()* run sets it aside part-way through**, which is a screen rather than an eligibility rule and is described in the next paragraph. 

The ineligible list, grouped by the condition each one breaks. **The treatment effect is no longer constant inside a cell:** *interactivecontrols()*, which interacts the treatment dummy with a variable that varies within the cell; and, under *contreat()*, the treatment variable **also** appearing inside *controls()*, *coarsecohortcontrols()*, *fe()* or *ccc_absorb()*, which makes the dose derivative vary within the cell on a variable the collapse does not average.

Everything else is eligible, including several combinations that are easy to assume are not. *contreat()* is eligible on the default *dydx* scale and under *contreatelasticitytype(dyex)*, with every flavour of *contreatcontrols()* and with *ccnoabsorb*. *lattice()* and *makecfxplots* are eligible too: each splits a cell on a dose bin, so the bin joins the cell key - the lattice variable, and under *makecfxplots* the *cfxlattice()* variable - and each collapsed row stands for one bin of one cell. Poisson estimation is eligible, including with *contreat()* and with *contreatelasticitytype(dyex)*. So are *fastnose*, *jointtests*, *adversarial* and *altmethod_adversarial*, *esfixedbaseperiod* and *esrelativeto()*, *pretrendpower()*, *histogramcohorteffects*, *summarystats*, *gteffects* (which *wooldid* refuses alongside *contreat()* for a separate reason), *makeplots* and *saveplots()*, and the replications of `permute()` and `bootstrap()`, which are evaluated collapsed like any other run. 

****subgroups()** is eligible, but screened, for a subtler reason worth understanding.** When *margins* judges a subgroup-by-horizon effect not estimable it does not error - it computes the number anyway and flags it only by marking the name it returns, and that flag rests on a rank decision made at the last digit. wooldid's subgroup code is the one place that reads the flag, and it does so three separate ways: to drop the affected horizon with a caution, to skip the adversarial pre-trend test, and inside the subgroup joint tests. A fourth exact-zero branch is the adversarial line test's own rank truncation, at 1e-12 of the largest eigenvalue of the event-study covariance, which runs on every event study (subgroup or not) and is three orders tighter than the collapse screen's tolerance - so a residue-level direction is dropped identically collapsed or not. So it is the one place where the collapse's last-digit change in summation order can change **what** is reported rather than its low-order digits: a horizon that should be dropped can come back as a meaningless near-zero, or a reported joint test can turn to missing. The screen is a few matrix operations per call. If it finds nothing, the collapsed result stands. If it finds a singular direction, then under the default safety setting wooldid restores the full data at that point and evaluates that call and every remaining subgroup call on it, so everything downstream of the flag is exactly what `nofastcollapse` would have produced (the main results, and any subgroup horizons already read before the flag, were computed collapsed and agree with their full-data values to the last digit as described above); *verbose* reports when this happens. Under *safety(off)* wooldid instead keeps the collapsed rows, as requested, and prints a caution naming what it found, since in that situation which subgroup horizons and joint tests are reported can depend on the summation order. The outcome is recorded in the hidden macro *e(wdid_fcguard)*: empty when nothing was flagged, *fallback*, or *caution*. A fallback carries into the replications of both `permute()` and `bootstrap()`, which are otherwise evaluated collapsed. 

**When does the screen fire?** In practice when a contrast between two subgroups has **exactly** zero variance in the full data too, so the "significance" of that contrast rests on rounding residue whichever way it is computed. The common route is clustering on a variable that nests the cohort variable - *cluster(state)* where the cohorts are state-by-group cells, or clustering on the cohort itself - so that subgroups of identical cohort-and-period composition are perfectly correlated; a handful of coarse clusters, a subgroup that is an exact shifted copy of another, and a subgroup nested inside a unit reach the same place. wooldid's own identical-standard-error, all-zero-variance or not-estimable caution usually accompanies such a run but does not always (a robust variance on a saturated design, or clustering on the period, can be singular there without it), so the screen adds coverage. Healthy subgroup runs pass untouched: realistic heteroskedasticity leaves the screened variance ratios between 0.1 and 1, seven to eight orders of magnitude from the tolerance. The one way a healthy variance matrix reaches it is outcome scales differing between subgroups by more than about a factor of 1e4 (a variance ratio of 1e-9): the cost is then the speedup under the default safety, and under *safety(off)* a caution that overstates the situation. Non-subgroup runs are not exposed: no code outside the subgroup path reads a *margins* result name, and 123 non-subgroup comparisons on aggregating data showed no structural difference at all. (The adversarial rank truncation just described applies to non-subgroup event studies too, but it is a fixed rule of the test itself, not a read of a *margins* result name.) 

**Memory.** The full data is held aside while the collapsed window runs, so peak memory includes an extra copy of your dataset. This is immaterial for typical panels but worth knowing if you are already near your memory limit, particularly if you have issued your own `preserve` before calling *wooldid*; `nofastcollapse` removes the extra copy. 

`simult:aneousbands`: Compute a **simultaneous** (sup-t, or uniform) confidence band over each event study's horizons, at `level()`. Off by default; no figure or table changes unless it is asked for. The pointwise interval printed on each table row covers that horizon with probability `level()`; a simultaneous band covers **every** horizon of the family at once with that probability, which is what a reader scanning a whole event study for "no effect anywhere before onset" actually needs. It is the estimate plus or minus one common critical value times each horizon's own standard error, not the printed pointwise interval scaled up, and it is symmetric about the estimate. So where the printed pointwise interval is a rank interval and not symmetric - under `bootstrap()` with the default `bootdisplay(percentile)`, or with `bootdisplay(bc)` or `bootdisplay(bca)` - the band does not nest it endpoint for endpoint: one of the band's two ends can sit inside the printed interval, even where the band is the wider of the two. The band is a property of the family rather than of a row, so the printed table keeps its pointwise intervals: the endpoints are stored in *e(esband_*type*)* (rows *lb*, *ub*, *relyr*; one column per horizon, per subgroup as *e(esband_*type*_s*sgcase*)*) with the critical value in *e(esband_crit_*type*)*, and the event-study plot draws them as a dashed pair over the pointwise shading. Two engines, chosen by the run: with `bootstrap()` the band is the `level()` quantile, over replications, of the largest studentized deviation from the observed estimates, computed on the listwise-complete replications in one pass over the accumulator that already feeds the joint test (*e(esband_n_*type*)* records how many). Under `boottstat` that deviation is studentized by each replication's **own** standard error and the endpoints use the observed analytic ones - a genuine percentile-*t*, on the same scale as the pointwise intervals printed beside it. The scale is shared, but nesting is not: that pointwise interval is equal-tailed from the signed *t*-quantiles while the band is symmetric about the estimate, so on a skewed studentized distribution one of the band's two ends can sit inside the printed interval - exactly as it can under `bootdisplay(percentile)`, `bootdisplay(bc)` and `bootdisplay(bca)`, and for the same reason; *e(esband_engine)* reads *bootstrap-studentized* there. Without `boottstat` the denominator is the standard deviation of the coefficient draws, which is the only quantity available. Without `bootstrap()` the band is simulated from the family's own analytic covariance *e(V_es_*)* - 10,000 draws from the implied correlation matrix, on a fixed internal seed with the caller's random-number state saved and restored, so the band is reproducible and the user's stream is undisturbed. Where the regression has residual degrees of freedom - the ones the pointwise intervals are built on - each draw's largest absolute deviation is divided by one common sqrt(chi2(*df*)/*df*), so the critical value is the sup quantile of a multivariate *t* with those degrees of freedom, the same *t* distribution the pointwise intervals use. The critical value is the simulated quantile as drawn, with no floor at the pointwise quantile: where simulation noise leaves it below that quantile, as it can when a family's horizons are almost perfectly correlated, the band is drawn narrower than the pointwise interval beside it. Where there are no residual degrees of freedom (the `poisson` path) the draws are normal. *e(esband_engine)* records which. Not available with `permute()`, whose multiplicity object is the `permstepdown` step-down instead, and unavailable under `fastnose` or where a family has fewer than two horizons with a usable variance. The two engines treat an unusable horizon differently. The analytic engine drops a horizon whose variance is missing or not positive, whose coefficient is missing, or whose name does not parse as a relative period, and bands the rest: *e(esband_*type*)* can therefore carry fewer columns than *e(b_es_*type*)*, the critical value is a maximum over the retained horizons only, and the band is simultaneous over those horizons rather than over the family as the table prints it.
