The bootstrap engine refuses the whole family for a single unusable horizon (a missing observed coefficient, a missing or non-positive observed analytic standard error under `boottstat`, or a draw standard deviation of zero), because its critical value is a maximum taken replication by replication, so a horizon that cannot supply a value at every retained replication changes what that maximum is over; it also needs at least two replications that are complete over every horizon of the family. A family with five good horizons and one bad one therefore gets a band from the analytic engine and none from the bootstrap engine. A Caution names every family that was attempted and declined, with its reason, both when no family at all could be banded and when some were banded and others were not, and under the bootstrap engine the reason names the horizon responsible; the figures for those families show their pointwise intervals only. Only families that were attempted are named: one whose inputs are absent from the run altogether (*e(V_es_ame*)* withheld by the cell-containment screen, say) is passed over before there is a reason to give. *e(esband_families)* lists the families that were banded. 

`level(#)`: Confidence level for every reported confidence interval - the analytic tables, the event-study and continuous-treatment plots, the *gteffects* cells and the Roth slope interval, as well as the `bootstrap()` and `permute()` intervals - between 10 and 99.99, with at most two digits after the decimal point, and a level outside that domain is refused by *wooldid* itself rather than by `set level`; the default is `c(level)`, normally 95. The interval columns are labelled *lb_#ci* / *ub_#ci* for the level in force (a non-integer level writes its decimal point as *p*: *lb_92p5ci*), and that level is stored in *e(level)*. The pre-test sizes behind `pretrendpower()`'s *mdt_wald* and *mdt_nis* columns are fixed at 5% and do not follow it; within that entry `level()` reaches the slope interval only.

## 5. Bootstrap inference

### When and how

Use `bootstrap()` when the analytic covariance is not trustworthy for the quantity you need (clustering at or above `i`; joint tests and subgroup contrasts in that design; `ame` inference under cell containment) and the number of clusters is moderate (tens or more). It re-estimates the whole model in every replication, so its cost is the run's cost times `reps()`; coarsening `i` and narrowing the window are the levers that shorten it, and subsampling is not.

- `bootstrap(standard)` is the pairs cluster bootstrap on the `cluster()` variable (or on observations without one). `bootstrap(bayes)` draws Dirichlet weights per cluster so no cluster ever leaves the sample; it is refused for the cell-weighted estimands whatever `safety()` is set to, because reweighting cannot resample treatment cells.
- `reps()` is required and has no minimum, but the p-value resolution is set by the number of completed replications n: 2/n for the percentile families, 2/(n+1) under `boottstat`, 1/(n+1) for the joint tests. Use several hundred replications for reported inference; a run at `reps(20)` is for timing a specification.
- `boottstat` studentizes: the reported standard error is the analytic one and the p-value and interval come from the replications' t-statistics. It is the route to bootstrapped joint pre-trend, subgroup-equality and adversarial tests; without it the adversarial p-value is absent under `bootstrap()`. It is slower and refuses `poisson`, `bootstrap(bayes)` and the `wald`, `bc` and `bca` displays.
- `bootdisplay()` chooses which family the tables show (`percentile` by default, `bc`, `bca`, `wald`); every family except `bca` is stored whichever is displayed.
- With few treated clusters the cluster bootstrap is badly sized and wooldid cautions; use `permute()` there.
- Refused with `impvalues`, `unconditionalse`, `customvce()`, `fastnose`, `oldsyntax`, `suppresssgprimaryeffects`, multi-way `cluster()` and `permute()`. `histogramcohorteffects` is untouched: it draws the observed point estimates and uses no covariance.

The rest of this chapter is the help file's account of the options, the families, and how the bootstrap differs from randomization inference.

### From the help file

`boot:strap(mode)`: Bootstrap standard errors and inference. When specified, the reported standard errors, z statistics, p-values, confidence intervals, joint tests and subgroup contrasts are bootstrap quantities, replacing the analytic ones; point estimates are unchanged, and the analytic covariance is preserved in the hidden *e(V_analytic)* (and *e(V_es_analytic_*)*). Each replication re-estimates the full model, so runtime scales with `reps()`. Two modes. `bootstrap(standard)` is the cluster bootstrap: clusters - the `cluster()` variable, or observations when no `cluster()` is given - are resampled with replacement. For the observation-weighted estimands (*att*, *ame*) replications are computed by cluster frequency reweighting, which is the same statistic as physical resampling and matches Stata's own `bootstrap` prefix replication for replication under a common seed. When a cell-weighted estimand (*att_it*, *att_i*, *att_itime*) is requested, replications instead physically resample, and what becomes of the unit identities on that frame depends on where `cluster()` sits relative to *i*. Where the cluster nests *i*, each drawn copy of a cluster becomes a fresh set of units, which is what makes a twice-drawn cluster contribute its treatment cells twice. Where the cluster is finer than *i*, or cuts across it, the units are kept exactly as they are and the drawn copies arrive as duplicate rows inside their own cells: re-identifying there would split a unit straddling two clusters into two units in **every** replication whether or not anything had been duplicated, turning the unit fixed effects into effects per cluster per unit, so the rule that is right under nesting is silently wrong under a finer cluster and the leg carries both. The choice between them is made once for the run rather than unit by unit, and it is made on the rows the estimation sample keeps: `bootstrap()` resamples the estimation sample, so rows *wooldid* removes by its markout are neither resampled nor counted toward the cluster count, and a straddle visible only on removed rows decides nothing. One straddling unit is therefore enough to put every unit under the keep-*i* rule, which omits part of the composition variance of the cell-weighted estimands and can understate their standard errors; a caution says so when it happens, *e(boot_nstraddle)* counts the straddlers and *e(boot_reid)* records which rule was taken. The remedies are a finer *i* (`egen newi = group(`*i cluster*`), missing`) or a `cluster()` that nests *i*. With no `cluster()` the observations themselves are resampled and the units are again kept whole. None of the three is a compromise for the estimand: *att_i* is a functional of whatever data a replication holds, and each replication rebuilds its weights from that data. The same physical leg is taken by `boottstat` whenever `cluster()` is given, for a different reason: a studentized bootstrap needs each replication's own standard error to be the same functional of the resampled data as the observed standard error is of the original, and only a physical expansion lets the replication cluster on the **resampled** cluster identity - under frequency reweighting a twice-drawn cluster's copies were pooled into one score and the replication standard errors were inflated by roughly the square root of two (their mean square relative to the observed one is 2 - 1/*G* in expectation). So `boottstat` with `cluster()` takes that same physical leg under either regime, since what is given a fresh identity is always the drawn **cluster**, which is what the replication's own standard error has to cluster on, and that is independent of whether the unit identities move with it. Without `cluster()`, `boottstat` replications carry the resample multiplicity as a frequency weight, which is again the same functional as the observed standard error; when a user weight (`aweight` or `pweight`) is also present that frequency form is unavailable, so the replications physically duplicate the drawn observations, carrying the user's weight onto every copy and keeping the original *i* (no re-identification is needed, because the resampling unit is the observation). Re-identification roughly doubles the cost of each replication, because the resampled data carries distinct cohort identities for duplicated clusters and so presents a larger cell count to the regression. What is re-identified is the cell and aggregation identity only. A term inside *fe()*, *controls()*, *interactivecontrols()*, *coarsecohortcontrols()* or *ccc_absorb()* that names the *i* variable itself keeps its original codes across the copies, so a twice-drawn cluster is two cohorts to the aggregation and one unit to those terms, the same convention Stata's own `bootstrap` prefix follows under `idcluster()`. *wooldid* cautions when it sees the bare *i* variable named in one of those options; it is a caution rather than a refusal, because the built-in prefix behaves the same way. The check compares bare tokens, so *fe(state)* is noticed where *fe(i.state)* is not. That cost is inherent to making those estimands' bootstrap correct rather than an inefficiency, but it is worth budgeting for when choosing `reps()`. `bootstrap(bayes)` is the Bayesian bootstrap: rather than resampling, each replication draws one Dirichlet(1,...,1) weight per cluster and multiplies every observation of that cluster by it (on top of any existing weights), so a cluster's total mass stays proportional to its size in every replication - exactly as under the pairs cluster bootstrap, whose first two moments it shares - no cluster ever leaves the sample, and replication estimability is stable; under `bootalpha(size)` the Dirichlet shape is instead the cluster's observation count and the drawn mass is spread equally over the cluster's rows, so on unweighted data the cluster's replicate total is the draw itself. Because reweighting cannot resample cells, `bootstrap(bayes)` combined with a cell-weighted estimand omits the cell-sampling variance component and can understate those standard errors when treatment effects vary across clusters: it is refused whatever `safety()` is set to, while `bootstrap(standard)` carries no such limitation. A caution fires when fewer than 6 treated clusters are available for resampling (beside the existing caution at fewer than 10 clusters in total), and `boottstat` is not a cure for it. Below two resampling units it is a refusal rather than a caution: `bootstrap()` requires at least two clusters in the estimation sample, or two observations on the unclustered leg, since resampling or reweighting a single unit produces no variation at all. That one fires when the options are screened, before the estimation runs. Requires `reps()`. Incompatible with `permute()` (one engine at a time), `impvalues` and `unconditionalse` (each replaces the inference this option produces), `fastnose` (fast mode reports point estimates only, so there is no inference surface left for this option to replace, and the engine already runs every replication in fast mode itself whenever `boottstat` is not requested, `boottstat` being what turns it off, since each replication must then estimate its own variance), `customvce()` (the engine has to know what a replication's variance is; an arbitrary vce string is not inspectable), `suppresssgprimaryeffects` (the suppressed subgroup entries are what the replications resample), `oldsyntax`, and multi-way `cluster()` (the engine resamples one unit, so there must be exactly one). The same requirements in the same shape, beside `permute()`'s, are in the engine-compatibility table. `pretrendpower()` **is now supported**: the Roth diagnostics are computed after the bootstrap rewiring, so Roth's own formulas are fed the bootstrap event-study covariance rather than the analytic one, and the reported power, detectable trend and slope figures are bootstrap-based. The method is unchanged - only the covariance it consumes - and the existing rank guard applies to the bootstrap block as it does to the analytic one, skipping with a caution if it is not invertible. With `jointtests` the joint pre-trend tests (and, with `subgroups()`, the across-subgroup equality tests) are bootstrap quantities in both forms: under `boottstat` each is the bootstrap of its test statistic (each replication's own covariance), and without `boottstat` it is the fixed-metric rank test the permutation engine also uses - the replication draws are recentred at the observed estimates, W is the quadratic form of the tested contrast on the listwise bootstrap covariance of those columns, and the p-value is the add-one share of replications whose W is at or beyond the observed one; *e(boot_jt_family)* records which (*studentized* or *fixedmetric*), the replication statistics are in *e(boot_jointW)* and *e(boot_sgW)* with the observed statistics in *e(boot_jointWobs)* and *e(boot_sgWobs)*, and a test with no usable replication statistic is absent with a caution rather than analytic. Neither is the Wald test on *e(V)* that a hand `test` reproduces; that one is a normal-theory test on the bootstrap covariance and is not printed. That combination is exempt from the analytic joint-test screen described under `jointtests` - bootstrap inference is what that screen recommends. The event-study and continuous-treatment plots are drawn after the rewiring from the bootstrapped tables and matrices, so they and their saved plot data carry the displayed bootstrap family's intervals, and the *gteffects* cells and *e(cfx*)* matrices are rewired too, except under *cfxplotnose*, which truncates the *e(cfx*)* matrices to their point-estimate column before the replications run, so there is nothing for the rewiring to fill and those cells come back with no bootstrap standard error, p-value or interval and their plot rows are drawn as points, without a message. `permute()` does not share that behaviour: it carves *cfxplotnose* out of the truncation precisely so its cells can be rewired. If bands are wanted on the continuous-treatment figures under `bootstrap()`, drop *cfxplotnose*. Only the *histogramcohorteffects* output is computed from the analytic covariance before the replications run and remains analytic, and a caution says so. Stata's own `bootstrap` prefix can also be used on an unweighted wooldid call (`bootstrap _b, reps(#) seed(#) cluster(c) idcluster(nc): wooldid y i t ttre, ... fastnose`), but it rejects weighted commands, cannot deliver the Bayesian flavor, and discards replications whose coefficient vector lost a horizon - the built-in `bootstrap()` option avoids all three. The conventions the reported p-values follow, and the floor each one carries, are mapped at Which p-value is which; why this engine and `permute()` differ where they do is at Why bootstrap() and permute() do not behave identically. 

`reps(#)`: Number of bootstrap replications; required with `bootstrap()`, and refused with an error on a run that does not use it. No minimum is imposed, so small values can be used to time a specification - but bootstrap inference needs many replications: hundreds for standard errors, and a thousand or more before the percentile and bias-corrected intervals are worth reading. Replications that fail to estimate - for example a resample retaining no comparison group - are excluded and counted in *e(boot_failed)*, the convention Stata's own `bootstrap` follows. **Resolution.** A rank-based p-value cannot be finer than its own grid, and `wooldid` prints a Caution naming that grid whenever it is coarser than .05 - the counterpart of the one `permreps()` prints, but without `permreps()`'s hard floor of 20. Which number binds depends on the family the table carries: the `bootdisplay(percentile|bc|bca)` coefficient p-values are plain two-sided shares on a grid of 2/*completed*, whose only value below .05 is exactly 0 (read as "below 2/*completed*", not as strong evidence); `boottstat`'s studentized p-values are add-one ranks with both grid and floor 2/(*completed* + 1), so they can never print 0; and under `bootdisplay(wald)` without `boottstat` the coefficient p-values are analytic and unlimited, leaving only the joint, equality and adversarial family - one-sided add-one shares with floor 1/(*completed* + 1). About 40 completed replications are needed before a 5% rejection is reachable, 100 for .02, 200 for .01 and 1000 for .002. 

`rseed(#)`: Seed for the bootstrap or permutation draws, passed to `set seed` before the first replication; as with the built-in prefixes this advances the session's random-number state. Without it the draws consume the current state and the run is not reproducible. Applies to `bootstrap()` and `permute()`, is refused with an error on a run with neither, and is recorded in *e(boot_seed)* or *e(perm_seed)*. Whether or not a seed is given, the random-number state in force before the first draw is stored in *e(boot_rngstate)* or *e(perm_rngstate)* (as Stata's own `bootstrap` stores *e(rngstate)*), so a run without `rseed()` can still be replayed exactly by `set rngstate` to that value before repeating the call; `bootsave()` and `permsave()` files carry the engine, the seed and that state as dataset characteristics *_dta[wooldid_engine]*, *_dta[wooldid_seed]* and *_dta[wooldid_rngstate]*. 

`dots(#)`: **Requires** `bootstrap()` or `permute()`; on a run with neither it is refused with an error rather than quietly ignored, so `dots(0)` on a plain analytic call is an error and not a way of asking for silence. Show a progress dot every *#* replications, with `dots(0)` suppressing the display; the default is 1. After the fifth replication the engines print, on the dots line, a projected runtime for the whole loop from the time the first five took (Stata timer 100 is used for those five replications). 

`ties`: **Requires** `bootstrap()` - it is refused with an error on its own. Adjust the bias-corrected confidence intervals' median-bias constant for ties between the replications and the observed statistic, exactly as the built-in `bootstrap`'s `ties` option does. It has no effect on continuous statistics, which do not tie, and under `boottstat` it touches only the stored coefficient-draw bias-corrected tables, never anything displayed. 

`bootalp:ha(spec)`: **Requires** `bootstrap(bayes)`; specifying it with `bootstrap(standard)` is an error rather than a setting that is quietly ignored. The Dirichlet convention for `bootstrap(bayes)`. `bootalpha(size)` instead gives cluster *c* the Gamma shape *n_c*, its observation count, and divides the drawn mass by that same count so that every row of the cluster carries *p_c*/*n_c*, reproducing what `bayesboot` does when passed `id(`*clustervar*`)`. That parity is an unweighted statement, because `bayesboot` refuses user weights outright; the divisor is the Gamma shape rather than the cluster's weight total, which is what keeps a weighted run's replications centred on the weighted estimand the point estimate targets. It is available for exact comparison against that command, but it is not a sound default for panel data, because tying the Dirichlet's concentration to the observation count rather than the cluster count shrinks the cluster-weight dispersion by roughly the square root of the mean cluster size and the resulting standard errors are badly understated. 

`bootts:tat`: **Requires** `bootstrap()`, and is refused with an error without it. A studentized (percentile-t) bootstrap: the object bootstrapped is the t-statistic, *t(r)* = (*b(r)* - *b*) / *se(r)*, with each replication's own analytic standard error in the denominator, rather than the coefficient. The reported standard error is the **analytic** one, labelled *se_analytic* in the tables - it is the studentization's own denominator, and a studentized distribution has no standard deviation in coefficient units. The reported statistic, labelled *t_pt*, is *b* / *se_analytic*. The p-value and confidence interval come from the ranks of the studentized draws: by default the equal-tailed percentile-t interval [*b* - *q_hi* *se*, *b* - *q_lo* *se*] from the signed quantiles of *t(r)*, which is not symmetric about *b*, and the two-sided rank of the observed statistic among the draws, 2 (1 + min(#*t(r)* at or below *t*, #*t(r)* at or above *t*)) / (1 + *completed*), under the add-one Monte-Carlo-null convention: the studentized draws are recentred at the observed estimate, so they form a reference set drawn under the null and the observed statistic is a member of it. That is the same convention every bootstrapped joint statistic in the package uses. The interval endpoints stay plain order statistics, so the p and the interval are near-duals rather than exact ones - at the boundary an interval can exclude the null while the p sits just above the level. Studentization applies to every coefficient in *e(b)* - the main, pretreatment and event-study rows and the subgroup tables - and to the *gteffects* cells and the *e(cfx*)* matrices, each by its own replication standard error. The pairwise subgroup contrasts are studentized the same way from each replication's own pairwise standard error, and the joint pre-trend tests, the across-subgroup equality tests and the adversarial line tests are bootstrapped as test statistics: each replication's statistic is recentred at the observed estimates and evaluated on that replication's own covariance (the joint tests on its *e(V_es_*)*, the equality tests on a covariance rebuilt from its level and pairwise standard errors), and the p-value is the add-one share of replications at or beyond the observed statistic. Without `boottstat` the joint pre-trend and across-subgroup equality tests are the fixed-metric rank tests described under `bootstrap()`; the adversarial line tests are not bootstrapped at all, since bootstrapping them means bootstrapping the statistic itself. They are posted as missing with *e(boot_adv_absent)* = 1 and a caution, never as analytic p-values under a bootstrap header. `boottstat` is refused with `poisson` (whose replications run on sampling weights) and with `bootstrap(bayes)` (whose continuous Dirichlet mass is not a duplication count), because neither can produce a replication standard error of the required form; the percentile, bias-corrected and Wald families remain available there.

**Costs and limits.** The replications are not run under *fastnose*, since each one has to estimate a variance to supply its own standard error, so a `boottstat` run is materially slower than a plain bootstrap of the same `reps()`, and with `cluster()` it also pays the physical re-identification leg (see `bootstrap()`). The p-value is a multiple of 2 / (1 + *completed*) with a floor of 2 / (1 + *completed*), so it is never exactly 0 and `reps()` sets the resolution. A replication whose standard error for a given quantity is missing or non-positive is dropped from that quantity's studentized distribution; the row *n* of *e(boot_tstud)* reports how many were used per coefficient, and a caution names any coefficient that used fewer than half of the completed replications. **Under clustering at or above the** *i* **level the analytic pairwise-contrast standard errors can be degenerate** (missing, or numerically zero with a p-value of 0 - the same limitation named in the caution `jointtests` prints under analytic clustered standard errors), and `boottstat` studentizes each contrast by exactly that standard error in every replication, so the studentized contrasts and the across-subgroup equality test can be absent or numerically unstable in that configuration; the non-studentized bootstrap forms the contrasts from the coefficient draws and is the one to use for them there. 

`bootd:f(spec)`: **Requires** `bootstrap()`, and is refused with an error without it. The reference distribution for the reported bootstrap statistics. `bootdf(normal)` uses standard normal critical values and reports z statistics - the convention Stata's own `bootstrap` prefix follows. `bootdf(clusters)` instead uses the t distribution with *G*-1 degrees of freedom, where *G* is the number of `cluster()` groups represented in the estimation sample, which is what *e(N_clust)* reports, and a positive integer sets the degrees of freedom directly; `bootdf(clusters)` without `cluster()` is refused rather than resolved to t(*N*-1). **The default depends on whether you clustered**: `bootdf(clusters)` when `cluster()` was given, and `bootdf(normal)` when it was not. The bootstrap standard error is estimated rather than known, so where a cluster count exists the t(*G*-1) correction is the appropriate one; with no `cluster()` the resampling unit is the observation, and a *G*-1 rule would be *N*-1 and indistinguishable from the normal. An explicit `bootdf()` always overrides the default. This changes only the reference distribution, never the bootstrap standard errors themselves: the Wald-family p-values and normal-theory confidence intervals widen; the percentile and bias-corrected intervals are quantile-based and are unaffected, as are the joint tests, which are formed from the replication statistics (studentized under `boottstat`, on the fixed bootstrap-covariance metric otherwise). Under every setting *e(df_r)* is aligned with the reference distribution actually reported - the same degrees of freedom under `bootdf(clusters)` or an explicit integer, and missing under `bootdf(normal)` so that the fallback is chi-squared and z - so a `test` or `lincom` run by hand afterwards reproduces the reported p-values rather than diverging from them - **this holds under** `bootdisplay(wald)`**, which is what** *e(V)* **and** *e(df_r)* **always carry**. Under the default `bootdisplay(percentile)` and under `bootdisplay(bc)` the printed standard error is still the bootstrap standard deviation that *e(V)* holds, but the printed p-value and interval are rank-based, so a `test` or `lincom` on *e(V)* reproduces the Wald family rather than the printed p; the run's footer says so explicitly. Under `boottstat` this option has no effect on any reported p-value or interval - every one of them, for coefficients, contrasts, joint and adversarial statistics alike, is a rank among the studentized draws - and it only aligns *e(df_r)* as described. The analytic value is preserved as hidden *e(df_r_analytic)*. 

`bootdisp:lay(spec)`: **Requires** `bootstrap()`, and is refused with an error without it. Which p-value and confidence interval the printed tables carry. Every family is computed and stored in *e()* on every bootstrap run regardless; this option chooses only what is displayed, and it does **not** change the reported standard error, which is always the standard deviation of the bootstrap distribution. `bootdisplay(percentile)`, **the default**, takes the p-value and interval from the percentile ranks of the bootstrap distribution. `bootdisplay(bc)` uses the bias-corrected percentile method, shifting the interval's percentiles by the median-bias correction *z0* that *e(boot_z0)* reports and reporting the p-value dual to that shifted interval. `bootdisplay(bca)` uses the bias-corrected and accelerated method (Efron 1987; the family Stata's own `bootstrap` prefix also reports): the same *z0* plus an acceleration *a* per coefficient, estimated from a delete-one-cluster jackknife - *G* extra fast fits, one per resampling cluster, run after the replications - stored in *e(boot_accel)*, with the intervals in *e(boot_ci_bca)* and the dual p-values in *e(boot_p_bca)*; because of that jackknife cost the BCa family is computed only when it is requested, unlike the other three. `bootdisplay(bca)` requires `cluster()`: the jackknife deletes one resampling unit per fit, which without `cluster()` would be one fit per observation. The pairwise subgroup contrasts get a BCa twin from the jackknife contrasts; the *gteffects* and *e(cfx*)* cells carry the bias-corrected family under `bootdisplay(bca)` (a caution says so), since the acceleration would need a jackknife of every cell. `bootdisplay(wald)` instead reports the p-value and interval from a normal or t approximation, the choice between the two governed by `bootdf()`. **Note that a percentile or bias-corrected interval is not symmetric about the estimate**, so the estimate plus or minus a critical value times the reported standard error will not in general land on the reported interval under those two settings; that is the ordinary reading of a bootstrap table rather than an inconsistency, and it is only under `bootdisplay(wald)` that the standard error, statistic, p-value and interval all reproduce one another. Under the percentile and bias-corrected displays the statistic column is therefore labelled *t_wald* (*z_wald* under `bootdf(normal)`): it is the estimate over the bootstrap standard deviation, a Wald object whose tail probability is not the rank-based p printed beside it, and it is kept for reference rather than as the test statistic of that row. Both can print as exactly 0, meaning no completed replication fell on the far side of the null, with resolution 2/*n*, so `reps()` sets how small a p is reachable - when it happens a line under the table and the run's footer say that the 0 means p below 2/*n*, while the cell and *e()* keep the 0; neither moves with `level()`, while the intervals do. A neighbouring edge case withholds the bias-corrected family for a coefficient outright: when every completed replication of that coefficient falls on one side of the rep-0 comparator, the median-bias correction *z0* is *invnormal(0)* or *invnormal(1)* and is undefined, so *e(boot_z0)*, *e(boot_ci_bc)* and *e(boot_p_bc)* are missing in that column - and the BCa twin with them, which starts from the same *z0*. The percentile and Wald families are unaffected. A Caution names the affected coefficients when it happens and *e(boot_bc_absent)* lists them afterwards; under `bootdisplay(bc)` those rows print a blank p-value and interval rather than one borrowed from another family. A low `reps()` makes a one-sided draw set likelier, but it is not the only cause and raising `reps()` is not always the remedy: a replication distribution that sits away from the estimate rather than scattered about it produces a one-sided draw set at any replication count. **With** `boottstat` **only the default** `bootdisplay(percentile)` **is available**: the studentized draws are summarised by their equal-tailed ranks. `bootdisplay(wald)` is refused because there is no bootstrap standard deviation of a studentized statistic for a normal or t approximation to rest on, and `bootdisplay(bc)` because the percentile-t interval is already second-order accurate and a bias-corrected shift of its studentized quantiles moves the interval against the median bias. `boottstat` chooses **what** is bootstrapped; `bootdisplay()` chooses how the coefficient draws are read when nothing is studentized. 

`bootsa:ve(name)`: **Requires** `bootstrap()`, and is refused with an error without it. Also write the bootstrap replication draws to *name*`.dta` in the current working directory (a plain file name; an existing file is overwritten only with `replace`; both rules are checked before the replications run). The file has one row per replication, *_rep* = 1 to `reps()`, with the replication coefficients under their *e(b)* names (a replication that failed keeps its row with every value missing, so *_rep* indexes the draws exactly as *e(bootdist)* does after its missing rows are dropped), plus a row *_rep* = 0 carrying the observed estimates, so the draws can be recentred from the file alone. Under `boottstat` it also carries each replication's analytic standard errors as *se_** (the observed analytic standard errors on the *_rep* = 0 row) and, with `subgroups()`, the replication pairwise-contrast standard errors as *pwse**#**_**, numbered by column of *e(bootdist_pwse)* (missing on the observed row). With `cluster()` it also carries the resample itself: one *w_**value* column per cluster (map-frame order, named by the cluster variable's value) holding that cluster's multiplicity in the replication under `bootstrap(standard)` - the number of times it was drawn, 0 when it was not, summing to the number of clusters - or its Dirichlet multiplier under `bootstrap(bayes)` (the multipliers sum to 1 per replication); the *_rep* = 0 row holds 1 under `bootstrap(standard)` and 1/*G* under `bootstrap(bayes)`, the observed run's weights on the same scale. Under `bootalpha(size)` the column holds the per-observation multiplier actually applied - the Dirichlet draw divided by the cluster's observation count *n_c* - so it is the rows, sum over c of *n_c* times *w_c*, that sum to 1, and the *_rep* = 0 row holds 1/*N*, *N* the number of observations in the estimation sample. Without `cluster()` the resampling unit is the observation and no such columns are written. Under `boottstat` the replication joint-test statistics are added as *jointW_**name* and the adversarial statistic as *advW*, with the observed statistics on the *_rep* = 0 row. The file's characteristics record the engine, the seed and the random-number state (see `rseed()`). It is written from the accumulators, so it is not subject to the 10,000-replication cap on the posted *e(bootdist)*, which a run above the cap announces in a caution. If the file cannot be written the run still completes, with a caution printed below the results tables, and the return code is posted in *e(boot_save_rc)*; a script that reads the file back should test for it (or `confirm file`) rather than rely on the run having stopped. 

** Discussion of Unconditional Standard Errors:** The overall treatment effect estimates presented by *wooldid* depend on not just the coefficients from the underlying regression, but also on the observed values of any included covariates or continuous treatment variables. The standard errors *wooldid* presents for these final estimates are produced during the margins calculation stage using the delta method, taking the observed values of the relevant variables as given (i.e., conditional on those values). This contrasts with approaches that aim to account for sampling variation in those observed values (i.e., unconditional standard errors; see the discussion of *vce(unconditional)* in `margins`). Whether one should wish to take the observed values of one's covariates as fixed likely varies from context to context. 

Does using unconditional standard errors make a large difference? Wooldridge (2025) expresses the view that conditional and unconditional standard errors often should be similar. Intuitively, this ought to be true whenever samples are large enough that sampling variation in covariates is ignorable (more precisely, ignorable within cohort-time period cells). Unconditional standard errors may be more impactful, however, when samples are small, or when sample weights make particular observations very influential. Additionally, when using unconditional standard errors, subgroup-specific estimates (as obtained using *subgroups()* or when computing event study estimates) can be sensitive to data from observations outside the focal subgroup. On a technical basis, this is because *wooldid* uses the *subpopulation* features in *margins* when producing these subgroup estimates. 

** Caution Regarding Unconditional Standard Errors When Used with Certain Weights and Estimands:** When using aweights and estimands other than the ATT, tests using unconditional standard errors sometimes appear to be incorrectly sized, rejecting the null hypothesis at extraordinarily high rates. The issue with non-ATT estimands likely is related to the margins calculation doing computations on predicted values using different weights (weights modified to hit non-ATT estimands) than used in the initial regression. The issue with using aweights does not always occur, though when it proves to be an issue it tends to result in poorly sized test statistics for both *wooldid* and `hdidregress`. Given these issues, if *safety(off)* is not specified, *wooldid* will warn against using unconditional standard errors with aweights and stop estimation if using them with a non-ATT estimand. Broadly speaking, unconditional standard errors tend to be highly sensitive to one's choice of weights. 

** Discussion of Ibragimov and Muller-Style Inference:** The virtue of the Ibragimov and Muller approach is that it can significantly improve on analytic clustered standard errors when the number of clusters is small and when the clusters are very heterogeneous in size. A key cost to adopting this approach is the required assumption that cluster-specific treatment effect estimates are independent. This assumption seems most likely to be satisfied when estimating the effect of continuous treatment variables using only within-cluster variation, or in a bespoke diff-in-diff set up where clusters contain both treated and control units and where time fixed effects are not shared across clusters. In *wooldid* the per-cluster estimate is read over that cluster's treated post-treatment cells alone, so putting control units inside a cluster does not give it its own control arm; what the condition asks for is a specification in which each cluster's counterfactual is estimated from that cluster, for example cluster-by-time effects in `fe()`. In the standard diff-in-diff setting, *wooldid* will still present IM-style estimates by computing cluster-specific treatment effect estimates, despite the fact that the existence of a shared control group (and perhaps a shared reference period, if *esfixedbaseperiod* is not being used) likely causes mischief with the independence assumption. How much mischief is measurable, and it scales with the never-treated pool. The cross-cluster dispersion cannot see the sampling error of the shared control trend, which every per-cluster estimate subtracts, so the reported standard error does not respond to the size of that pool while the estimator's own sampling variation does. There is no plug-in correction - the missing term is the analytic covariance the method exists to avoid - so the remedy is a larger never-treated pool, or `bootstrap()` or `permute()`. 

### Why `bootstrap()` and `permute()` do not behave identically

The two engines differ in what they refuse, what they default to, what they store and what they can even be asked. Most of those differences are not accidents of implementation: they follow from what a bootstrap and a randomization test **are**, and harmonizing them would make one of the two wrong. Three mechanical facts generate almost all of them. 

**1. Recentring.** Bootstrap draws are sampling draws centred at the observed estimate, so a null reference distribution has to be **manufactured** by recentring - an asymptotic device. Permutation draws are null draws by construction, nothing is recentred, and the observed statistic is a member of its own reference set. This is why the p-value conventions differ (see the p-value map), why the permutation interval is a shift interval rather than a quantile interval, and why an exact enumeration under `permexact` needs no add-one while sampled draws do. 

**2. Reweighting.** A bootstrap replication carries duplication or Dirichlet weights, so a replication's standard error is a **different functional** of the data than the observed standard error is of the original sample - which is why `boottstat` has to re-identify duplicated clusters to be a genuine percentile-*t*, and why `bootstrap(bayes)` cannot studentize at all. A permutation replication relabels onset over the same rows, so it is the **same** functional, and `permtstat` costs nothing structural. 

**3. The assignment is the randomized object.** Anything keyed on treatment status or onset has no permutation distribution at all: relabelling onset changes what the quantity **means**, not just its value. This is why the continuous-treatment lattice types whose bins are functions of the assignment have their inference columns blanked under `permute()` and not under `bootstrap()`, why the randomization unit must nest the cohort variable *i* unless `permonsetcohorts` lets each replication rebuild it, and why `pretrendpower()` is refused under `permute()`: Roth's diagnostic consumes an event-study **covariance**, and the permutation engine does not produce a covariance of the estimates - it produces a null distribution of a statistic. That is a difference of kind, not a gap to be filled. 

**What this means in practice.** The two engines' exchangeability assumptions are **orthogonal**, not one strong and one weak. The pairs cluster bootstrap assumes clusters are exchangeable as **sampling** draws and fails with few or atypical treated clusters. Randomization inference assumes clusters are exchangeable in **assignment** and fails when onset is systematically related to the outcome path - which is the identifying assumption the design already needs. Neither is a fallback for the other, and the few-cluster referral from `bootstrap()` to `permute()` is a referral to a **different** assumption, not a weaker one. 

Where a difference does **not** trace to one of the three facts - a threshold number, a message wording, which engine happened to receive a stored object first - it is an accident, and those are being removed rather than defended. The engine-compatibility table below is the one place both engines' requirements are stated in the same shape, with the reason attached. 

### Which p-value is which, and the floor it carries

*wooldid* prints p-values from four different constructions, and they do not all obey the same rule. The split is deliberate: the add-one Monte-Carlo-null form is used wherever the observed statistic genuinely belongs to its own reference set, and the plain share is used where it does not. This table is the map. In it, *n* is the number of **completed** replications - not the number requested - and *s(r)* is the *r*th draw of whatever statistic the row reports. 

- ****surface**** **convention, and the smallest p it can print**

- ***analytic rows*** A normal or *t* tail on the reported standard error. No floor and no grid; a p can underflow to 0 but is not bounded away from it by a draw count. 

- **`impvalues`** A *t* tail on *G*-1 degrees of freedom (Ibragimov-Muller). Same: no draw-count floor. 

- **`permute()`, coefficient rows** **Add-one, equal-tailed**: min(1, 2 min(*P_lo*, *P_hi*)) with *P_lo* = (1 + # *s(r)* **<** *s_obs* )/(*n* + 1) and *P_hi* its mirror. **Floor 2/(*n* + 1)**; it cannot print 0. 

- **`permute()` + `permabsp`** The same add-one rule on absolute values: (1 + # |*s(r)*| **>** |*s_obs*| )/(*n* + 1). **Floor 1/(*n* + 1)**; it cannot print 0. 

- **`permute()` + `permside()`** The one-sided add-one tail, *P_lo* or *P_hi* alone. **Floor 1/(*n* + 1)**; it cannot print 0. 

- **`permute()` + `permrawp`** **The plain share**, # . /*n*, which is what *ritest* and `permute` report. It **can** print exactly 0, and 0 there means "no draw was this extreme", not "p is zero". 

- **`permute()` + `permexact`** Plain shares over the *M* enumerated assignments, because under a complete enumeration the observed assignment **is** one of the *M* - the add-one is already there. **Floor 1/*M***. An element absent from the observed assignment's own replication falls back to the add-one form, and the run says which. 

- **`permute()`, joint and equality tests** One-sided upper tail, add-one: (1 + # *W(r)* **>** *W_obs* )/(*n* + 1) on a fixed metric. **Floor 1/(*n* + 1)**. 

- **`bootstrap()`, `bootdisplay(percentile)` and `(bc)`/`(bca)` coefficient rows** **The plain share**, min(1, 2 min(*m0*, *m1*)/*n*) - no add-one. **Floor 2/*n***, and it **can** print exactly 0. 

- **`bootstrap()` + `bootdisplay(wald)` coefficient rows** A normal or *t* tail on the bootstrap standard deviation. No draw-count floor. 

- **`bootstrap()` + `boottstat` coefficient rows** **Add-one**, 2(1 + min(*m0*, *m1*))/(*n* + 1) on the studentized draws. **Floor 2/(*n* + 1)**; it cannot print 0. 

- **`bootstrap()`, joint, equality and adversarial tests** One-sided upper tail, add-one, under either the fixed-metric or the studentized form: (1 + # *W(r)* **>** *W_obs* )/(*n* + 1). **Floor 1/(*n* + 1)**. 

**Why the split, and why it is not an inconsistency.** Under a permutation null the observed assignment is exchangeable with the permuted ones, so it belongs in the reference set and counts itself. The same argument carries to every statistic whose draws are null draws: the fixed-metric joint tests under both engines, and the studentized bootstrap tail, whose draws are recentred at the observed estimate and so form a null reference set the observed statistic is a member of. It does **not** carry to the bootstrap percentile and bias-corrected coefficient rows: that distribution is centred at the **estimate**, not at the null, so adding the observed value to it would be a conservatism choice rather than an exactness result. There the plain share stands, which is also what `bootstrap`'s own *estat bootstrap* reports. 

**Reading a p of exactly 0.** Only the two plain-share families can produce one: `permrawp` and the bootstrap percentile and bias-corrected rows. It means no draw reached the observed statistic, which at *n* draws is a statement about resolution, not about the evidence - the honest reading is "p **<** 1/*n*" (or 2/*n* two-sided). Every add-one family reports that resolution as its floor instead, which is why those families never print 0. 

**The floor is a property of the run, not of the data. Raising the draw count is the only thing that lowers it, and the two engines differ on what they will accept: `bootstrap()` imposes no minimum at all (`reps()` need only be positive, and a run is refused only when no replication completes), while `permute()` requires `permreps()` to be at least 20 and additionally refuses a run in which fewer than *max(20, half the requested draws)* complete. A `permexact` enumeration is exempt from that completion floor, because the shares stay exact over whatever completed and what incompleteness costs there is resolution.** `reps()` and `permreps()` both print a caution when the floor they imply sits above .05, naming the number, because at that setting no result the run can produce would be conventionally significant. Raising the draw count is the only fix.

## 6. Randomization inference

### When and how

Use `permute()` when treated clusters are few, which is the setting where cluster-asymptotic and bootstrap inference both fail. wooldid reassigns treatment onset across clusters (`shuffle` keeps the count at each onset fixed; `probabilities` draws from a design you specify), re-estimates every requested quantity under each reassignment, and ranks the observed estimate in the resulting distribution. Every p-value and interval the run reports is then a permutation quantity, including the subgroup contrasts and the joint, equality and adversarial tests.

- Requirements: a single `cluster()` that nests `i` (or `permonsetcohorts`, which asserts that `i` is the onset grouping and rebuilds it in each replication); `permreps()` of at least 20, or `permexact` to enumerate every assignment (shuffle only; refused above 65,534 assignments, the Stata matrix row limit); never-treated coded as plain missing; every cluster inside one onset group. An onset more than `oostreatedcontrols()` periods after the last period (net of `anticipation()`) is never-treated in the permutation design, as it is in the estimation, and a caution counts the clusters recoded.
- The observed run and the replications are in fast mode by default, so no analytic standard errors are computed and `e(V)` is not posted; `permanalyticse` restores them for reference, and `permtstat` randomizes on t-ratios instead of coefficients (slower; protects against a treated cluster with unusual variance only when the standard error can see that cluster, which at `cluster()` = `i` it cannot).
- P-value conventions: equal-tailed two-sided by default, `permabsp` for absolute-value, `permside()` for one-sided, `permrawp` for the plain share `ritest` reports. The floor is 1/(permreps+1) (2/(permreps+1) equal-tailed), and wooldid cautions when it exceeds .05. Intervals are shift intervals at `level()` and are calibrated near the null; they widen with the true effect.
- In staggered designs the reassigned onsets move post-treatment outcomes into the replications' pre-treatment horizons, so pre-period p-values rise toward 1 as the true effect grows; a large pre-period p-value is not by itself evidence of no pre-trend (`e(perm_precaveat)` = 1 records that pre-treatment rows were reported under `permute()`, so the caveat applies; it does not test whether onsets are staggered).
- Refused with `impvalues`, `unconditionalse`, `customvce()`, `contreatwithin`, `contreatcontrols(untreatedZero)`, `oldsyntax`, `suppresssgprimaryeffects`, `pretrendpower()`, `simultaneousbands` and `bootstrap()`. `gteffects` cells carry no permutation inference, and `histogramcohorteffects` is untouched: it draws the observed point estimates and uses no covariance.

The rest of this chapter is the help file's account of the options and conventions.

### From the help file

`permute(method)`: Randomization (permutation) inference over the treatment assignment, for settings - most importantly few treated clusters - where cluster-asymptotic standard errors are unreliable.

**What the test assumes.** The reference distribution is generated by reassigning treatment onset across clusters (within `permstrata()` strata), so the p-value tests the sharp null under the assumption that onset was assigned **exchangeably** across clusters: any cluster could have received any onset. It is not robust to the treated clusters being atypical in size or in error variance - relabelling then mixes clusters of different variance, and the reference distribution is narrower than the sampling distribution of the observed statistic (MacKinnon and Webb 2020). The default statistic is the raw coefficient (fast: the replications run without variance estimation); `permtstat` randomizes the studentized statistic, MacKinnon and Webb's recommendation when clusters may be heterogeneous, but it protects only when the cluster-robust standard error is informative about the treated clusters - many observations per treated cohort-period cell, with `cluster()` coarser than the cell. With `cluster()` at *i* and one observation per *i*-period the treated cells are absorbed by their own cohort-period terms, the standard error cannot see their variance, and neither statistic is protected (the same design rejected in 7 of 30 datasets under `permtstat`); a caution says so whenever `cluster()` is at *i*, whichever statistic is being randomized: under `permtstat` it says the studentization cannot see the treated clusters' own variance there, and on the default coefficient path it says the coefficient statistic carries no protection against a treated cluster whose errors are unusually dispersed, pointing at `permtstat` with that same proviso. The remedy the literature recommends for that case - a studentization by a standard error that sees the treated clusters, or the wild cluster bootstrap-t - is not offered here. *wooldid* re-randomizes which clusters carry which treatment-onset dates, re-estimates every requested quantity under each counterfactual assignment, and ranks the observed estimates among the resulting null distributions. **Every p-value and interval reported under permute() is permutation-based**: the main, pre-treatment and event-study rows, the subgroup tables and their pairwise contrasts, the joint pre-trend tests, the across-subgroup equality tests, the adversarial line tests and the continuous-treatment plot cells all carry permutation p-values and permutation intervals, and the event-study and continuous-treatment plots - drawn after the replications from the rewired tables - carry the same intervals. No analytic p-value is ever shown under `permute()`; a quantity with no usable permutation distribution is left blank rather than filled from another source; only the *histogramcohorteffects* output is left as the observed run produced it. It carries no inference of any kind: every one of its margins calls passes *nose*, and it draws a frequency histogram of the per-cohort point estimates, which neither engine changes. The two cautions that used to call this an analytic covariance were deleted on 2026-09-21 as factually wrong. Requires `cluster()` with a single variable - the randomization unit (the randomization design consists of the clusters having at least one row where the *if* expression evaluates to exactly 1 with a nonmissing cluster value; rows *wooldid* later removes by its deterministic markout, e.g. missing outcomes or zero weights, still count toward the design and its possible-assignment count) - and every cluster must sit inside exactly one treatment-onset group (onset constant within cluster; never-treated is its own group; the cluster must also nest *i*, i.e. cluster at or above the unit level, since each replication assigns one onset per cluster, unless `permonsetcohorts` asserts that *i* is exactly the treatment onset grouping, in which case the replication rebuilds *i* from the onsets it drew and a `cluster()` finer than *i* is admissible; never-treated onsets must be coded as plain missing `.` - tagged missing codes `.a`-`.z` are refused, recode them to `.` first). The design reads each onset as the estimation does: an onset after the last period of the design sample, plus `oostreatedcontrols()` and net of `anticipation()`, is never-treated, so it is not a label of its own in the shuffle, the possible-assignment count, the `permexact` enumeration, the ever-treated count, the onset-group screens or the `permprobs()` level set, and a caution counts the clusters recoded (the observed run and the shuffle p-values are unaffected, since the worker makes the same recode inside every replication). Two methods: `permute(shuffle)` permutes the observed onset labels across clusters, holding the number of clusters at each onset fixed (`permstrata(varname)` confines the shuffling to within strata, so each onset group keeps its composition with respect to the strata; the stratum variable must be numeric, a string one being rejected by Stata's option parser before *wooldid* can say anything about it, and it is accepted only with `permute(shuffle)`; with `permute(probabilities)` it is an error, because there the strata design is expressed through the per-cluster rows of `permprobs()` instead; every cluster must sit inside a single stratum, with no missing stratum values); `permute(probabilities)` draws each cluster's onset from user-supplied probabilities via `permprobs()`. Specify `permreps(#)` (draws with replacement) or, for shuffle only, `permexact` (enumerate every distinct assignment; a caution prints above 1,000 assignments and enumeration is refused above 65,534, the Stata matrix row limit; above 10,000 enumerated assignments the draw matrices are not posted and a caution says so). The number of possible assignments is always computed and returned, with a caution when `permreps()` exceeds 10 percent of it; a design admitting only one possible assignment (e.g. every stratum onset-homogeneous, or every `permprobs()` row deterministic) is refused - there is nothing to randomize. **What is reported.** The permuted statistic is the coefficient itself, or its t-ratio under `permtstat`. p-values are two-sided **equal-tailed** permutation p-values: with *P_lo* and *P_hi* the shares of replications at or below and at or above the observed statistic, *p* = min(1, 2 min(*P_lo*, *P_hi*)); sampled draws use the add-one Monte-Carlo form (1 + count)/(replications + 1), and exact enumerations plain shares (the observed assignment is a member of its own reference set, so an exact two-sided p has a floor of 2/*M*; an element that some enumerated assignments could not estimate keeps the exact share over the assignments that completed - its floor is 2/*n* on its own count *n*, and a caution names it. Only an element whose reference set does not contain the observed statistic at all - the observed assignment's own replication failed to carry it - falls back to the add-one form, under its own caution). `permabsp` selects the absolute-value convention instead: *p* is the share of replications whose absolute statistic is at or above the observed absolute statistic, under the same add-one and exact rules. Intervals are **shift intervals**: the estimate minus the equal-tailed quantiles of the permutation distribution (times the analytic standard error under `permtstat`), at `level()`; under `permabsp` they are the symmetric duals of the absolute-value p-value, the estimate plus or minus the `level()` quantile of the permuted absolute statistics, so that in either convention the interval excludes zero exactly when the p-value falls below the level (up to the add-one adjustment). A shift interval inverts the permutation test under the fixed-zero-null pivotality approximation - the estimate's sampling error is taken to have the null permutation distribution - and is **not** a sharp-null randomization-test inversion (which would re-permute adjusted outcomes at every candidate value). This is not the Conley-Taber construction, which draws its reference from placebo assignments among the never-treated units only. The standard-error column is the analytic standard error when one is computed (labelled *se_analytic*; blank otherwise), the statistic column *t_perm* is the permuted t-ratio under `permtstat` and blank otherwise, and the interval-implied standard errors are in *e(perm_se_shift)*. The pooled-estimand summaries in the footer (p, the `level()` null band - the equal-tailed quantiles of the null draws themselves, in either convention - and the SD of the permutation distribution) are the same numbers the tables carry. **Fast-mode default.** Without `permtstat` the observed run and the replications all run in fast (no-SE) mode: nothing the permutation inference reports needs the analytic standard errors, so computing them is wasted *margins* work. *e(V)*, *e(b_es_*)* and *e(V_es_*)* are then not posted and the *honestdid*/*pretrends* handoff is unavailable. **So that the standard table tools still work**, the run posts its permutation inference under the names *estout* looks for before it looks at any covariance: *e(p)* (the same numbers as *e(perm_p)*) and *e(ci_l)*/*e(ci_u)* (the shift interval, the same as *e(perm_ci)*). `esttab, p` and `esttab, ci` therefore carry this run's real permutation figures under truthful footers, and a plain `esttab` tables the coefficients. There is deliberately no *e(se)*: a permutation run has no standard error, and the shift standard error in *e(perm_se_shift)* is a different object, so naming it *se* would put a number in a column that has none. Nothing else changes - with no *e(V)* posted, *test*, *lincom*, *_se[]* and *estat vce* go on refusing, which is correct. `permanalyticse` restores the analytic standard errors (shown for reference only; *e(V)* and *e(V_es_*)* are then analytic, and *test* or *lincom* run by hand on them are analytic quantities). *e(p)*, *e(ci_l)* and *e(ci_u)* are posted on every `permute()` run, not only in fast mode, so under `permanalyticse` and `permtstat` too the p-values, intervals and significance stars `esttab` prints are the permutation ones; its standard-error and t columns are then derived from the analytic *e(V)*, matching the *se_analytic* column of *wooldid*'s own table, and *estimates table*, *ereturn display* and *r(table)*, which read *e(V)* alone, stay analytic. The cell collapse applies to the replications as to the observed run (*nofastcollapse* opts out); its degenerate-subgroup fallback reads a variance, which under the fast-mode default only the subgroup-by-horizon margins call carries, so elsewhere it engages only under `permtstat` or `permanalyticse`. **Joint, equality and adversarial tests** are one-sided permutation tests of a quadratic-form statistic in the **permutation-covariance metric** - the covariance of the replications' coefficients over the relevant elements (replications plus the observed comparator, listwise complete; under a complete `permexact` enumeration the enumerated assignments alone, the observed one being among them) - so they exist in both modes and consume no per-replication covariance: the joint pre-trend tests (`jointtests`) test the pre-treatment horizons jointly, the across-subgroup equality tests use the chain contrast of the subgroup levels, and the adversarial line test (`adversarial` with `esfixedbaseperiod`) is *wooldid*'s line-fit statistic evaluated on that metric. Their p-values, like every pre-treatment row and contrast, test the **sharp null of no effect at any horizon**: in staggered designs the reassigned onsets move actual post-treatment outcomes into the draws' pre-treatment horizons, so the null distribution widens with the true post-treatment effect while the observed pre-period statistics do not, and the pre-period p-values rise toward 1 as the true effect grows. A large pre-period p-value is therefore not by itself evidence of no pre-trend (the tests lose power against pre-trends as the effect grows; with a single onset date the reference distribution is unaffected), and the adversarial p is not the composite on-a-line test of the analytic run. The replication statistics are in *e(perm_jointW)*, *e(perm_sgW)* and *e(perm_advW)*. Pairwise subgroup contrasts are permuted as coefficient differences in both modes. `jointtests` under `permute()` is exempt from the analytic joint-test screen described under `jointtests` - randomization inference is what that screen recommends. **Subgroups** enter the replications. Because any cluster can be assigned treatment in a replication, the subgroup variable must satisfy the subgroup screens (non-negative integers; constant within *i*-by-*t* cells and within units; on all of a unit's rows or on none) over the whole sample, not only on the observed treated units, and the run is refused otherwise, with the unit-level screens standing whatever *safety()* is; a subgroup level absent from some replications contributes fewer draws (*e(perm_n)*). Under `permute(shuffle)` without `permstrata()` the shuffle also re-randomizes which subgroups the treated clusters fall in; `permstrata(*sgvar*)` holds each subgroup's onset composition fixed where membership is cluster-level. With `contreat()` the dose stays with its unit and only onset timing is re-randomized (the null: timing as-if random given dose); the *ame* rows are permuted like the *att* rows, with replication cells that lack dose variation (exact structural zeros) excluded and counted. The *gteffects* cells are keyed by cohort onset - exactly what a replication reassigns - so they have no permutation distribution and their inference columns are blank. The continuous-treatment plot cells are permuted for assignment-invariant lattice types (*all*, *byCohort*, *byTime*, *byCohortByTime*, or a user `cfxlattice()` variable) and blanked, with a caution, for the *baseTreated**, *byTreated** and *byTtre** types, whose bins move with the assignment. These matrices are the one permutation surface whose column names are left exactly as `margins` wrote them. Column 1 is the observed estimate on every type. Column 2 ships under the plain name *se* and holds the analytic standard error, not a permutation quantity. It is not renamed *se_analytic* as it is on the main and subgroup tables, because these names are consumed by *wooldid*'s own plotting code and by `marginsplot`-shaped code outside it, so the provenance is disclosed in the footer instead. On the permuted types, columns 4 to 6 are the permutation p-value and the permutation shift interval, and column 3 is the estimate over that analytic standard error under `permtstat` and missing otherwise. On the assignment-dependent types, "blanked" means columns 4 to 6 are set missing always and column 3 missing unless `permtstat` is in force; column 2 still holds the analytic standard error. So a reader exporting these matrices after a `permute()` run must not take the *se* column for a permutation standard error, and must not read a blank interval as an absent estimate. The shift construction presumes the null distribution is centred at zero, and *wooldid* checks that presumption rather than assuming it: when the central `level()`% band of an element's null draws (the signed equal-tailed quantiles, in whichever p-value convention is in force) excludes zero, the presumption has failed and the interval it would produce would not even contain the estimate. That element's shift interval and the implied standard error in *e(perm_se_shift)* are left blank, a Caution names the elements at run time, and they are listed in *e(perm_offcentre)*; the permutation p-value itself stands. A pairwise subgroup contrast whose null draws fail the same check has its shift interval blanked the same way and is appended to *e(perm_offcentre)* under its *e(pwsgs)* row name (for example *att:1vs0.sg*); the footer line that lists *e(perm_offcentre)* names it, the Caution does not. It fires on quantities that are not zero under reassigned onsets, and under a `permute(probabilities)` design far from the observed assignment. Reassigned onsets can change which periods are estimable in a given draw: each draw is the estimate *wooldid* would compute under that counterfactual assignment, estimability screens included. Replications that fail to estimate are excluded and counted (*e(perm_failed)*), with a caution above 10 percent and a refusal when fewer than max(20, half) succeed (exact enumerations are exempt from that floor, but any exact-mode failure draws a caution). When fewer than 6 of the resampling clusters are ever treated a caution names the count: few ever-treated clusters means few distinct onset assignments, so sampled draws repeat and the reference set (and with it the set of p-values the run can produce) stays coarse however large `permreps()` is. That is a limit of the design rather than of the replication count, and `permexact` answers it by enumerating the assignments and reporting the exact floor instead of sampling them. It is not the same condition as `bootstrap()`'s caution at fewer than 6 treated clusters, which is about resampling variance rather than about how many assignments exist. `permreps()` must be at least 20, and sets the resolution: a sampled-draw p-value cannot fall below 2/(`permreps()` + 1) under the default equal-tailed convention (1/(`permreps()` + 1) under `permabsp`), so 50 replications resolve to .039, 100 to .020, 199 to .010 and 999 to .002; below 39 a 5% rejection is unreachable under the default convention and a caution says so. Exact enumeration has its own floor of 2/*M* (1/*M* under `permabsp`). A degenerate permutation distribution (every completed draw equal) draws a caution; a missing or unreadable observed comparison statistic is refused loudly with r(459). Draws consume the session random-number stream: give `rseed(#)`, or `set seed` beforehand, for reproducibility; `dots()` controls the progress display and `permsave()` writes the draws. Not compatible with *impvalues*, *unconditionalse*, *contreatwithin*, *contreatcontrols(untreatedZero)* (replications reassign onset while the dose path stays fixed, contradicting untreatedZero's dose-zero-outside-treatment premise), *oldsyntax*, *suppresssgprimaryeffects* (the suppressed subgroup entries are what the replications permute), *pretrendpower()* (the Roth diagnostics need an event-study covariance, and randomization inference produces none), `simultaneousbands` (a confidence-band object; the permutation engine's multiplicity tool is the `permstepdown` step-down), or `bootstrap()`. The conventions the reported p-values follow, and the floor each one carries, are mapped at Which p-value is which; why this engine and `bootstrap()` differ where they do is at Why bootstrap() and permute() do not behave identically. 

`permprobs(spec)`: The assignment-probability design for `permute(probabilities)`, structured as `(onsetvar level level ...)` followed by one `(clustervalue p p ...)` group per cluster. The first group names the actual timing variable and lists every in-sample onset level (never-treated is the level `.`, and so is an onset after the last period, which the design reads as never-treated) in the column order the probabilities use; each later group gives one cluster's probabilities over those levels (nonnegative, summing to 1; rows within the acceptance tolerance of 1e-6 are rescaled to sum exactly 1 before drawing). Every in-sample cluster value must appear exactly once, and the `cluster()` variable must be numeric, since the grammar names clusters by numeric value. `permreps()` is required and `permexact` is refused. The possible-assignment count reported is the product of the per-cluster support sizes; if the observed assignment has zero probability under the design, a caution notes that the p-value is a tail probability under that design distribution rather than a randomization test. 

`permtstat`: Randomize on t-ratios instead of raw estimates: the permuted statistic is each replication's estimate over its own analytic standard error, ranked against the observed *t_perm* = estimate/*se_analytic*. The observed run and the replications then go through the full inference path (slower: fast mode cannot be exploited, and requesting *fastnose* alongside is refused); an element whose replication or observed standard error is unusable is left blank rather than filled from the coefficient draws, and a caution counts them. The shift intervals are then the estimate minus the equal-tailed quantiles of the permuted t-ratios times *se_analytic*. The joint, equality and adversarial tests and the pairwise contrasts are unchanged by `permtstat` (they are formed from the coefficient draws on the permutation-covariance metric). The equal-tailed exact-test floor is 2/*M* in both modes (1/*M* under `permabsp` when no other assignment ties the observed absolute statistic). `permtstat` is the MacKinnon-Webb (2020) recommendation when clusters may be heterogeneous, with the proviso described under `permute()`: it protects only when the cluster-robust standard error is informative about the treated clusters, and at `cluster()` = *i* with one observation per *i*-period it is not (a caution says so). 

`permtci`: Retained from the earlier permutation surface: additionally report, for the pooled estimands, the symmetric inverted-|t| `level()` interval - the observed estimate plus or minus the `level()` quantile of the permuted absolute t-ratios times the observed standard error (*e(perm_tlb_*)*/*e(perm_tub_*)*). It rests on the same pivotality approximation as the shift intervals every row now carries and is not a sharp-null randomization-test inversion; under `permabsp` it coincides with the interval the table carries for those estimands. Implies `permtstat`. Unavailable (with a caution) when the observed run has no usable standard error. 

`permsa:ve(name)`: Also write the permutation draws to *name*`.dta` in the current working directory (a plain file name, so `permsave(name, replace)` is refused before the replications run; an existing file needs the separate `replace` option). One row per replication (`_rep` = 1 to `permreps()`, or 1 to *M* under `permexact`; a failed replication keeps its row, all missing) plus a `_rep` = 0 row carrying the observed estimates (the rep-0 comparator the p-values rank against equals this row except under `permanalyticse`, where the comparator is a fast-mode refit; the continuous-treatment plot cells are not written - their draws live in the hidden *e(permdist_*)* twins), one column per coefficient of *e(b)* under its *e(b)* name; under `permtstat` also the replication and observed analytic standard errors (`se_`*name*), so the permuted t-ratio is the coefficient over its `se_`; and one `onset_`*clustervalue* column per cluster holding the onset that cluster carried in each replication (row 0: the observed onset as the design reads it, so an onset after the last period shows as missing). A trailing `.dta` on the argument is stripped, so `permsave(mydraws.dta)` and `permsave(mydraws)` are the same request; it is still a plain file name, with no directory part. The assignment block can come up short in two ways, each of which prints a Caution at the time: it is dropped in full when the cluster-value and observed-onset lists do not conform to the assignment matrix, leaving a file that carries the replication draws and no `onset_` columns at all, and an individual cluster's column is omitted when its `onset_`*clustervalue* name is already taken, either by a coefficient column or by two cluster values that map to the same Stata name. Because the columns are named rather than positional, nothing inside the saved file marks either omission, so check the `onset_` column count against the cluster count before relying on the block. `bootsave()` strips a trailing `.dta` in the same way and skips its own `w_`*clustervalue* columns on the same kind of name collision. Every reported permutation quantity is otherwise reconstructable from the file. If the file cannot be written the run still completes, with a caution printed below the results tables, and the return code is posted in *e(perm_save_rc)*; test for it (or `confirm file`) before reading the file back. 

`perman:alyticse`: Under `permute()` without `permtstat`, compute the analytic standard errors anyway (the observed run then takes the full inference path) and show them in the *se_analytic* column for reference; the p-values and intervals remain permutation-based, and the *t_perm* column stays blank because the permuted statistic is the estimate itself. This also restores *e(V)*, *e(b_es_*)* and *e(V_es_*)* (analytic) and lets the cell-collapse fallback screen engage in the replications. Refused with a user-typed *fastnose*. 

`permab:sp`: Report absolute-value permutation p-values instead of equal-tailed ones: each p-value is the share of replications whose absolute statistic (the coefficient, or the t-ratio under `permtstat`; the coefficient difference for a subgroup contrast) is at or above the observed absolute statistic, with the same add-one form for sampled draws and plain shares under `permexact`. The intervals then become the symmetric duals of that p-value - the estimate plus or minus the `level()` quantile of the permuted absolute statistics (times the analytic standard error under `permtstat`) - so the table's p-value and interval keep agreeing. The joint, equality and adversarial tests are one-sided quadratic-form tests and are unchanged. The null band, the SD and the off-centre check are convention-free. The convention in force is recorded in *e(perm_pconv)* (*equaltailed* or *absolute*; *raw*, *left* or *right* under the two options below). The three are alternative conventions: at most one of `permabsp`, `permside()` and `permrawp` may be specified, and the combination is refused. 

`permsi:de(left|right)`: One-sided permutation p-values: `permside(left)` reports the share of replications whose statistic is at or below the observed one and `permside(right)` the share at or above it, under the same add-one / exact-share rule as the default; the two shares are always stored as *e(perm_p_lo)* and *e(perm_p_hi)*, so the default two-sided p is 2 min of them. The intervals stay the two-sided equal-tailed shift intervals at `level()` (a one-sided test has no two-sided dual). 

`permraw:p`: The plain absolute-value share *c*/*n* with no add-one adjustment: *c* is the number of replications whose absolute statistic is at or above the observed absolute statistic and *n* the completed replications. This is the number `ritest` and Stata's own `permute` prefix report, offered so a referee's cross-check can be reproduced; its intervals are the symmetric duals used under `permabsp`. 

**Which convention, and the floors.** wooldid's default is Rosenbaum's equal-tailed two-sided p-value, 2 min(*P_lo*, *P_hi*), with the add-one Monte-Carlo form (1 + count)/(*n* + 1) for sampled draws (Davison and Hinkley 1997; Phipson and Smyth 2010) - the convention `boottest`'s ptype(equaltail) uses - because it is the exact dual of the equal-tailed shift interval and spends alpha/2 in each tail, which matters for asymmetric nulls. `ritest`, Stata's `permute` and Young (2019) instead report the absolute-value share *c*/*n* without the add-one adjustment (`permrawp` here; `permabsp` is the same statistic with the add-one form), and MacKinnon and Webb (2020) use the absolute-value convention too. The conventions differ in their floors: with *n* sampled draws the smallest attainable p is 2/(*n* + 1) under the default, 1/(*n* + 1) under `permabsp` and 0 under `permrawp`; under `permexact` with *M* assignments it is 2/*M* under the default and 1/*M* under the absolute-value conventions (2/*n* and 1/*n* for an element that some assignments could not estimate, with *n* its count in *e(perm_n)*) - with one treated cluster among 12 the exact default p cannot fall below .167 and the absolute-value p below .083. Every convention also comes with its Monte-Carlo precision: *e(perm_p_se)* is the standard error of the reported p-value's underlying share, sqrt(*p*(1 - *p*)/*n*) (doubled under the equal-tailed rule), and *e(perm_p_ci)* its `level()` Clopper-Pearson interval, as `permute` and `ritest` print. Under *permexact* the shares are exact and carry no Monte-Carlo error, so both are left missing; the exception is an element that fell back to the add-one form, whose share is over a set that excludes the observed statistic and does carry both. 

**Multiplicity across the horizons.** `permstepdown` reports, for each family and subgroup with two or more horizons, the max-|studentized| step-down of Westfall and Young / Romano and Wolf from the same replications: each horizon's draws are studentized by the standard deviation of its null draws (the fixed metric the joint tests use), the horizons are ordered by the observed |t|, and the j-th adjusted p-value is the share of replications whose largest |t| over the horizons not yet rejected reaches the observed one, made monotone. It is exact under the sharp null of no effect at any horizon of the family and controls the family-wise error rate across those horizons, which the per-horizon p-values in the table do not. The post-treatment and pre-treatment horizons are run as **separate** families, adjusted among themselves and reported and stored apart: pooling them would change each post-treatment adjusted p-value by enlarging its family, and the two answer different questions. The pre-treatment family inherits the pre-treatment caveat below - in staggered designs the reassigned onsets widen the null for pre-period statistics - so its adjusted p-values are conservative in a way the post-treatment family's are not. The procedure is permutation-only; under `bootstrap()` the corresponding object is a simultaneous band (`simultaneousbands`), which is a uniform confidence band rather than a family-wise-error test.
