The stored kernel's *p_raw* row (and the footer's first column) is the two-sided |t| share, add-one, in the step-down's own studentized metric - not the table's convention - so compare it with the step-down p rather than with the table. The results are stored in *e(perm_stepdown)* (*_i* for *att_it*, *perm_Cstepdown* for the continuous-treatment family, *_pre* for the pre-treatment family, *_s#* per subgroup; rows *t_obs*, *p_raw*, *p_singlestep*, *p_stepdown*, one named column per horizon) and summarised in the footer. The step-down runs on the replications that are listwise complete over **every** horizon of the family rather than horizon by horizon, so its reference set can be considerably smaller than the per-horizon counts in *e(perm_n)*, which is exactly what happens on a design where a reassigned onset makes some horizon inestimable in a draw. A family's step-down can also fail to be formed at all, when fewer than two replications are complete over the family, when the comparator row is missing a horizon, or when some horizon's null draws have no dispersion; the matrix is then simply not posted and nothing is printed, so test for *e(perm_stepdown*)* by name rather than reading the footer's silence as evidence that multiplicity is not a problem. A family whose stored name would run past Stata's 32-character limit is skipped the same way. The footer summarizes the four overall families and their pre-treatment twins; the per-subgroup matrices are returned in *e()* and are not summarized there.

`permstepdown`: Compute and report the max-|studentized| step-down described above. Requires `permute()`; off by default. 

`permonset:cohorts`: Assert that *i* **is** the treatment-onset grouping, with exactly one level of *i* for each distinct onset and the never-treated forming a single level of their own, and on that assertion accept a `cluster()` finer than *i*. The onset is the one the permutation design reads, so a unit first treated more than `oostreatedcontrols()` periods after the last period (net of `anticipation()`) belongs to the never-treated level of *i*, not to a level of its own. The two choices answer different questions. *i* says which cohorts the estimands average over; `cluster()` says which observations are dependent and what the design randomized. An *i* set at the treatment-onset level can therefore sit above the randomization unit, which is the pairing What level to cluster at recommends and the one this engine refused. The refusal was mechanical rather than methodological: each replication assigns one onset per cluster and writes it to every row of that cluster, so a unit straddling two clusters would carry two onsets at once, which the estimator has no reading for. Under this option each replication instead rebuilds *i* from the onsets it drew, since the units sharing a drawn onset **are** that draw's cohort, so the onset is single-valued within the rebuilt *i* by construction. 

**Why it has to be declared.** *wooldid* cannot tell from the data whether *i* is an onset grouping or a substantive unit definition that happens to coincide with one on this sample, and rebuilding the wrong one would silently redefine the estimand. The declaration is then checked, on the permutation design sample and its design onset and with the never-treated included: an *i* carrying two onsets, or an onset spread across two levels of *i*, stops the run with r(459), and *safety(off)* does not lift it, since without the one-to-one correspondence the rebuild is not a defined map rather than an unwise one. Where `cluster()` already sits at *i* the rebuild has nothing to do and is skipped, so the option is then a declaration that moves no number, and *e(perm_irebuilt)* records which of the two happened. 

**What it composes with.** `permstrata()` composes, since the strata constrain which onsets a cluster may draw and the rebuild sits downstream of that; a rebuilt cohort can then pool clusters from different strata, which is the cohort definition doing its job, a cohort being an onset and not a stratum. `permute(probabilities)` composes as well, and there an onset can go undrawn, so a replication's rebuilt *i* can hold fewer cohorts than the observed sample and the cohort-averaged estimands are then averages over the cohorts that draw produced, which is the ordinary reading of a functional of the replication's own data. `subgroups()` is refused: a rebuilt cohort-by-period cell pools the clusters that drew one onset, those clusters come from different observed cohorts, and the subgroup variable then varies inside the cell, which the estimator refuses in every replication. A finer `cluster()` is also what makes `permtstat` protective, since the studentized statistic guards against a treated cluster atypical in variance only where the cluster-robust standard error can see that cluster's own variance, which at `cluster()` equal to *i* it cannot.

## 7. Event studies

### Decision rules

- **Pooled or fixed reference period.** The default pools every pre-treatment period outside the requested window into one reference per cohort; `esfixedbaseperiod` compares every horizon with a single relative period (`esrelativeto()`, default -1). Prefer the fixed reference when the outcome trends within the pre-period, when cohorts have different pre-period lengths, and whenever a downstream tool needs a single omitted period: the adversarial p-value, `pretrendpower()`, and the `honestdid` handoff all require it.
- **Window.** `esprelength()` and `espostlength()` count consecutive integer relative periods; on a panel with gaps in relative time the realized window is shorter. The pooled `main` row always averages every estimable post-treatment period, whatever the window. With `subgroups()` and `esprelength()` above 0, a zero `espostlength()` is raised to 1 under a caution.
- **Anticipation.** `anticipation(#)` shifts every onset earlier by # units of `t` before relative time is built (equivalent to replacing `ttre` by `ttre` - #). Too small a value leaves a visible residual pre-trend; too large attenuates the effect with no diagnostic. Raise `espostlength()` by # to keep the same number of genuine post-treatment horizons.
- **Pre-trend tools** and when each is calibrated:

| tool | what it tests | needs | not calibrated when | refused with |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `jointtests` | all requested pre-treatment horizons are zero | two pre horizons (three under `esfixedbaseperiod` with `timetrends` at `esrelativeto(-1)`) | analytic `cluster()` at or above `i`; few observations per cell under `contreat()` | `impvalues`, `fastnose` |
| `adversarial` | horizons and the reference lie on one line, slope free | `esfixedbaseperiod` for a p-value; one pre and one post horizon | as above, and unless each cell is spanned by many clusters | `impvalues`, `fastnose` |
| `pretrendpower()` | Roth (2022) power of the pre-tests against linear trends | `esfixedbaseperiod`, a pre-period covariance | analytic `cluster()` at or above `i` (cautioned); skipped with a caution when the pre-period block is unavailable or ill-conditioned | `impvalues`, `fastnose`, `permute()` |
| `simultaneousbands` | sup-t band over a family's horizons | two horizons with variance | none; unavailable under `fastnose` | `permute()` |

Under `bootstrap()` the joint and adversarial tests are bootstrap quantities (the adversarial one only with `boottstat`); under `permute()` they are permutation quantities. `makeplots` draws the event-study figures; `saveplots()` writes them and the plotted data.

The rest of this chapter is the help file's account of the options.

### From the help file

### Event Study Estimation

`esf:ixedbaseperiod`: Use a fixed reference period for estimation, rather than a pooled reference period.

One reading note about the output under this option. The *pretreatment* row of the results table averages EVERY estimable pre-treatment period, not the window named by *esprelength()*: it is a whole-pre-history placebo, and it does not narrow when you shorten the requested pre-window. The joint pre-trend test on the same run does use the requested window, so the two quantities can cover different sets of periods in one table. Read the row as a placebo over all available pre-treatment time, and the joint test as a statement about the horizons you asked for. 

`esr:elativeto(-integer)`: The relative time period used as the fixed reference period, if a fixed reference period is being used. The default is -1, the time period immediately prior to treatment. Under *esfixedbaseperiod*, values above -1 (the onset period or later) are rejected with an error: the fixed reference period must lie strictly before onset. Without *esfixedbaseperiod*, this option has no effect and a Caution is printed. 

`espre:length(integer)`: The number of pre-treatment relative time period-specific effects to be estimated. If any pre-treatment effects are requested and *esfixedbaseperiod* is specified, all relative time period effects between the *esrelativeto()* period and treatment onset will be presented.

Both this option and *espostlength()* count CONTIGUOUS INTEGER relative-period slots, not realized horizons. On a panel whose relative-period axis has gaps - a biennial panel, or any design whose onsets sit off the observed time grid - the requested block contains slots no cell realizes, each of which consumes one horizon from the far end of the window. A biennial panel asked for two pre and three post horizons therefore returns one and two, and a realized horizon that the stage-1 regression estimates and that enters the reported *att* - which pools every post-onset cell, not only the reported window - can fall outside both the reported event-study table and *e(b)*. Widen the request until the window reaches the horizons you want.

`espost:length(integer)`: The number of post-treatment relative time period-specific effects to be estimated. With *subgroups()* and *esprelength()* > 0, a value of 0 is raised to 1: subgroup estimation requires at least one post-treatment horizon. The caution announcing the raise prints either way, since the run is reporting a post-treatment horizon you did not ask for and saying so is a matter of disclosure rather than of safety; what *safety(off)* suppresses is only the two lines of accompanying advice. Specify *espostlength(-999)* to override the raise and keep zero post-treatment horizons; every other negative value is an error. 

`joint:tests`: Calculate joint statistical significance tests of all requested pre-treatment relative time period-specific effects. At least two pre-treatment horizons must fall in the estimation window; with only one the option is accepted, no joint test is produced, and nothing is printed to say so. Under *esfixedbaseperiod* with *timetrends* and the default *esrelativeto(-1)* the requirement is one period deeper: the estimation sample must realize at least three pre-onset periods rather than two, so two requested pre-treatment horizons are not enough, and a window that is short by this rule is dropped in the same silent way. An *esrelativeto()* deeper than -1 lifts the extra requirement. Two further reliability conditions apply. First, *jointtests* is unreliable under wooldid's analytic clustered SEs when clustering is at the level of the cohort variable *i* or above: the joint test p-values are severely oversized in that configuration (the cluster-robust covariance cannot capture the within-cluster cross-horizon variation the tests depend on, a problem that does not diminish with more cohorts or larger cells), so *wooldid* prints a caution and computes the test anyway - under both safety settings - reporting the p-value as-is; it should not be read as a calibrated test. If joint-type inference is needed in such settings, use bootstrap-based clustered standard errors or randomization inference (see *permute()*), or cluster strictly finer than the cohort-by-period cells, which helps only where the finer clustering leaves enough distinct clusters inside each treated cohort-by-period cell; with only a few clusters per cell the test stays oversized. Second, in the continuous treatment case the joint tests are only correctly sized when the i-by-t cells each contain many observations: the tests aggregate within-cell dose-slope variances that carry a downward small-sample bias which compounds across horizons, so designs with only a handful of observations per cohort-period cell yield oversized joint tests even under robust or finely clustered SEs (note that *lattice()* refines cells smaller and *contreatpoly* adds within-cell parameters, both of which sharpen this concern). Two options refuse *jointtests* outright: `impvalues` and `fastnose` (fast mode reports point estimates only; that refusal stands down on the fast path `permute()` takes by default). Under `bootstrap()` and `permute()` the analytic joint tests are not computed at all: the reported joint pre-trend p-value is a bootstrap or permutation test instead, and where one cannot be formed it is reported as absent rather than as an analytic test. 

`adv:ersarial`: Tests the composite null hypothesis that all estimated relative time period-specific effects fall on a single line together with the reference period - some line through the omitted base period, with the slope unknown - a null that is adversarial toward finding pre-trend violations in that the slope is chosen to fit the estimates as well as possible. Under *esfixedbaseperiod* each reported coefficient is a difference from the reference period, so an underlying path that is linear implies estimates proportional to (relative period - reference period): one free parameter, not two. The p-value comes from a calibrated minimum-distance test on the event-study coefficients and their full variance-covariance matrix (the same objects returned in *e(b_es_type)* and *e(V_es_type)*): the nuisance line is estimated by generalized least squares under a generalized inverse of that covariance, and the minimized quadratic distance is referred to an F distribution whose numerator degrees of freedom equal the residual rank (the rank of the event-study covariance minus one, the single line parameter identified), with the regression's residual degrees of freedom in the denominator (a chi-squared reference is used when no residual degrees of freedom are available). The p-value requires *esfixedbaseperiod*: under the pooled reference regime no single omitted period exists, so a caution is printed and the fitted line is reported without a p-value. Under *timetrends* two periods are normalized to zero (the reference period and the one immediately before it), so the only line passing through both of them is the zero line, and the primary null collapses from "the coefficients lie on some line through the reference period" to "every coefficient is zero". There is then no free line parameter to estimate: the statistic is the whole quadratic form on the event-study covariance and its numerator degrees of freedom are that covariance's rank, one more than the rank-minus-one rule that governs every other run. A p-value is produced whenever the covariance has positive rank, which is the ordinary case under *robust*; it is *cluster()* at the level of *i* that typically leaves it entirely zero. The test under *timetrends* is therefore a strictly stronger one than this option's description, and a rejection there is not by itself evidence that the effects depart from a line. If the event-study covariance is degenerate (as is typical under *timetrends*), a caution is printed and no p-value is computed. For display, wooldid also reports a line fit by the unweighted OLS regression of *coef_rt* on *time_rt* (over the pre-treatment horizons only under *altmethod_adversarial*), where *coef_rt* is the estimated coefficient in relative time period *rt* and *time_rt* is the relative time period; the variable *coef_rt* includes values of 0 for the reference period (or periods, if *timetrends* and *esfixedbaseperiod* are specified). These tests will not be conducted if an insufficient number of relative time period-specific treatment effects are requested (either overall, or within specific subgroups). The conditions are these, and each of them leaves the rest of the run intact and prints a caution rather than stopping it. The requested window must hold at least one pre-treatment and one post-treatment horizon, and under *timetrends* at least three event-study coefficients in all, the reference period counting as one of them. The pre-and-post rule is then applied a second time to the horizons the estimation sample actually realizes, so a window that was adequate when the options were parsed can still lose the test after the sample is formed. With *altmethod_adversarial*, *esfixedbaseperiod* and the default *esrelativeto(-1)*, a single pre-treatment horizon leaves nothing but the reference row from which to fit the pre-period line, and the test is cancelled. Under *subgroups()* the test is skipped for any subgroup that lost a relative period as inestimable (its coefficients no longer span a complete relative-time grid) or that is left with too few of them. Under `permute()` the adversarial p-value is a permutation p-value of the line-fit statistic on the permutation-covariance metric and tests the sharp null of no effect at any horizon (see `permute()`). Under `bootstrap()` the adversarial p-value is produced only with `boottstat`, which bootstraps the test statistic itself; without it the p-value is absent rather than reported as an analytic test, and *e(boot_adv_absent)* is set. *adversarial* is refused with an error by `impvalues` and by `fastnose` (whose refusal stands down on the fast path `permute()` takes by default). 

The test is close to correctly sized when every treated cohort-by-period cell is spanned by six or more clusters, several times oversized when cells are spanned by only two clusters, and an order of magnitude oversized when each cell lies entirely within a single cluster (as happens automatically when clustering on the cohort variable *i* itself). Enlarging cells does not substitute for this: cell size does not repair the test's size when the cluster requirement is not met. Small overall cluster counts degrade the test independently. Two further conditions are worth keeping in view. Under a continuous treatment the line test additionally requires cells containing many observations - on the order of fifty - because each cell's dose slope is otherwise too imprecisely estimated for the test's covariance to be trustworthy. And where errors contain a common shock at the cohort-by-period level, no analytic variance estimator can capture it: such a shock is absorbed into the cell's own coefficient and leaves no trace in the residuals, so the test becomes badly oversized regardless of the clustering chosen, increasingly so as cells grow. Where these conditions are not met, prefer randomization inference (see *permute()*) or bootstrap-based standard errors for pre-trend inference. *wooldid* does not test for these conditions at run time; they are a matter for the user's judgement about the design.

`altmethod_adversarial`: Modifies *adversarial* and does not stand alone: specifying it without *adversarial* is an error. It is an alternate method of implementing the test above, which projects a trend forward using the pre-treatment coefficients only (plus the planted zero-valued reference row or rows), and then tests whether the post-treatment coefficients collectively fall in line with the projected values. The test propagates the full forecast covariance: writing the post-treatment coefficients as *b_Q*, the pre-treatment fit set as *b_P*, and the linear operator taking *b_P* to the forecasts as *A*, the forecast error *b_Q - A b_P* is tested against its complete covariance *V_QQ + A V_PP A' - A V_PQ - V_QP A'*, so uncertainty in the fitted pre-period line and the pre/post covariance both count against the null. Like the primary implementation, the p-value requires *esfixedbaseperiod*. On a relative-period axis with gaps it additionally requires at least one real pre-treatment coefficient beyond the planted reference row or rows; where none remains, the adversarial test is cancelled with a caution (for that subgroup, under *subgroups()*). 

`pretrendpow:er(numlist)`: Reports Roth (2022)-style pre-trend power diagnostics for each requested power level (specified in percent as integers strictly between 5 and 100, e.g. *pretrendpower(50 80 90 95)*): a GLS estimate of the linear pre-trend slope with its standard error and confidence interval (fit through the reference period on the pre-treatment event-study coefficients using their full variance-covariance matrix from *e(V_es_*type*)* — a more efficient and validly-inferenced line than the display-oriented *e(adversarial_line_*type*)*, which is an unweighted fit over all horizons, or over the pre-period horizons only under *altmethod_adversarial*); the minimum linear pre-trend each pre-test would detect with the requested power (*mdt_wald* powers the joint pre-trend test, an F test with the regression's residual degrees of freedom; *mdt_nis* powers the test that any pre-period coefficient is individually significant — the convention of Roth's *pretrends* package, whose size is also reported since it exceeds its nominal level when there are several pre-periods, and which is under the normal reference throughout: a normal critical value, with its size and power computed under the normal law, even where the regression has residual degrees of freedom — both pre-tests are conducted at the 5% level and, unlike the slope interval, neither follows `level()`); and the bias each such trend would imply for that block's pooled estimand **if it continued into the post-treatment period** (the minimum detectable trend itself depends only on the design's precision, never on the estimated coefficients). When only *att_i* or *att_itime* is requested, the reported block is the shared *att_it* event-study curve (*ame_it* under `contreat()`) standing in for them: its hbar and bias columns describe *att_it*-family post-period weighting only and do **not** quantify the bias such a trend would imply for the requested *att_i*/*att_itime* (or *ame_i*/*ame_itime*) estimands, whose post-period weights differ. With `subgroups()`, diagnostics are computed for the overall event-study types only (*att*, *att_it*, *ame*, *ame_it*); subgroup event-study objects (*e(b_es_*_s#)*/*e(V_es_*_s#)*) are not analyzed and no *e(roth_*_s#)* results are returned. Requires `esfixedbaseperiod` (under the pooled reference the single omitted reference period the linear-trend normalization needs is not defined) and at least one pre-treatment event-study coefficient; not compatible with `impvalues`, `fastnose`, or `permute()` - each is refused with an error, since none of the three produces the event-study variance-covariance matrix the diagnostics are computed on. Under `bootstrap()` the diagnostics do run, and that covariance is then the bootstrap one rather than the analytic one; a family whose bootstrap covariance is unavailable is skipped with a caution rather than computed on the analytic matrix. Results are returned in *e(roth_power_*type*)* and companion scalars (see Stored Results) and skipped with a caution when the pre-period variance-covariance block is unavailable (e.g. under `timetrends`) or carries missing or non-positive variance entries; rank-deficient blocks are handled with a generalized inverse and reduced degrees of freedom, with a caution (note that on an exactly rank-deficient block every reported statistic reflects only the linearly independent subset of pre-period coefficients the generalized inverse retains - not a summary of all pre-period evidence - and depends on which columns are retained, so treat those numbers as one representative of an underdetermined family and interpret with care). A requested power level at or below the NIS test's actual size is achievable by a zero trend, so *mdt_nis* and *bias_nis* are exactly 0 there (a missing cell always means the computation was unavailable, never a boundary answer). The exact-quadrature *mdt_nis* column is computed only for up to 4 pre-periods (its cost grows steeply with dimension); for larger designs, feed *e(b_es_*type*)*/*e(V_es_*type*)*/*e(numpre_*type*)* to Roth's *pretrends* package (subject to the same positional-geometry conditions as the honestdid handoff — see the *e(b_es)* Stored Results entry), which implements the same convention with the same normal critical value. When the underlying estimator posts no residual degrees of freedom (e.g. the ppmlhdfe path), the joint test and *mdt_wald* use chi-squared rather than F distributions and the slope CI uses a normal critical value. Under `poisson` the diagnostics are on the linear-index (xb) scale like the event-study coefficients themselves. 

`makep:lots`: Produce event study plots. No plot is drawn unless at least one event-study horizon is requested via *esprelength()* or *espostlength()*. Note on graph management (applies to all of *wooldid*'s plotting options): *wooldid* names the graphs it draws (e.g. att_fullmodel, hist_att_i, cfxatt_cfxpost) and creates them with the graph-name *replace* suboption, so re-running overwrites *wooldid*'s own prior graphs of the same name; it does not clear other graphs from memory. Two consequences: a user graph whose name exactly matches a *wooldid* graph name will be overwritten when that plot is recreated, and *wooldid* graphs from an earlier, differently-configured run linger in memory until that configuration is run again (or the user drops them). 

`oos:treatedcontrols(integer)`: By default, *wooldid* uses cohorts treated after the end of the sample the same way as never-treated control cohorts (i.e., such cohorts are "out-of-sample treated controls"). When this option is specified with a value of T, cohorts treated within T periods of the end of sample are instead handled the same as other treated cohorts. Such cohorts are potentially allowed to contribute to pre-treatment relative time period-specific effects when this option is specified, but not otherwise. Note that if *esfixedbaseperiod* is specified, the out-of-sample-treated cohorts will be dropped if the specified reference period is not usable for these cohorts (not observed within the sample, or removed because no comparison unit exists in that calendar period). When *anticipation()* is also specified, the out-of-sample window applies to the **effective** (shifted) onset, so a cohort whose recorded onset lies just beyond the window can become usable once its effective onset falls within it. Negative values are not allowed. 

`antic:ipation(integer)`: Treats the effective treatment onset as *ttre* − #, so that periods in which units may act in anticipation of a known upcoming treatment are counted as treated periods rather than clean pre-treatment periods (which would otherwise contaminate the reference period and pre-trend estimates). The shift is applied once, before relative time is constructed, and is exactly equivalent to re-running *wooldid* after replacing *ttre* with *ttre* − # (this is also the convention of *did_imputation*'s *shift()* option): all event-study horizons are counted from the **effective** onset, so relative period 0 is # periods before the recorded onset, and you may wish to raise *espostlength()* by # to keep the same number of genuine post-treatment horizons. Note an inherent asymmetry: specifying too SMALL an anticipation length leaves a loud residual pre-trend signal, but too LARGE a value attenuates the estimated effects with clean pre-trends and no diagnostic - prefer the smallest defensible value. Notes: # is expressed in the units of *t* (not in panel-period counts — with, e.g., biennial data an odd # lands between observed periods, and *wooldid* prints a caution); units whose effective onset falls at or before their first usable period are dropped (with a caution reporting the count), and under `esfixedbaseperiod`, units whose **shifted** reference period falls on a period unusable for them (not observed, or dropped for lack of a comparison unit) are dropped, with a caution when the drop is attributable to the shift itself; with `subgroups(*ttre*)`, subgroup labels remain the recorded (unshifted) onset values. When active, the shift is recorded in *e(anticipation)*. Negative values are not allowed. 

** Two Approaches to Estimation -- Pooled Reference Period vs Fixed Reference Period:** Difference-in-differences treatment effect estimates involve comparing average outcomes during treated cohorts' treatment periods to treated cohorts' average outcomes during a pre-treatment reference period. By default, *wooldid* does comparisons using a pooled reference period, meaning that treated periods for a given cohort are compared to a reference period containing all pre-treatment observations for the cohort. If the user specifies the option *esfixedbaseperiod*, *wooldid* will switch to employing a fixed reference period, meaning that all treatment effect estimates will be formed by comparing treated cohorts' average outcomes in post-treatment periods to their outcomes in a particular pre-treatment reference period (specified in relative time period terms). By default, this pre-treatment reference period is the time period immediately prior to treatment (relative period -1), though the user can specify alternative behavior. For more on how this is implemented, see discussion.

With respect to which type of reference period should be used, fixed reference periods offer the virtue of transparency: all treated cohorts are compared to one reference period, which is the same for all cohorts in relative time terms. By contrast, pooled reference periods can be more opaque, with different cohorts having reference periods that contain varying numbers of time periods. In exchange for reduced transparency, pooled reference periods offer the possibility of greater precision in treatment effect estimates, owing to the fact that they pool together more data and thus have greater opportunity to average out idiosyncratic variation in outcomes within the reference period. 

While there is no general solution to this transparency-precision tradeoff, use of a pooled reference period is likely disadvantageous whenever the outcome variable is evolving over time within the pre-treatment period. It also tends to be disadvantageous when one is seeking to estimate more pre-treatment event study style treatment effects than can be estimated for all cohorts, as can occur if one has staggered treatment onset and wants to estimate the largest number of pre-treatment coefficients possible. In this case, cohorts with fewer observed pre-treatment periods will have their earliest observed period set as their reference period, even if this period is a period for which treatment effects are reported for other cohorts. If this issue combines with cohorts not being on the same outcome trends, the resulting event study plots may show treatment effect time paths that evolve in counterintuitive and difficult to interpret ways, possibly obscuring even severe parallel trends assumption violations from detection. 

** Invalid Estimands:** For relative-time period specific estimates, distinctions between the estimand types *att_i*, *att_itime*, and *att_it* collapse. The latter estimand will yield estimates that weight each cohort's contribution to the relative-time period specific estimate equally; the former two are differentiated from this only in terms of how they treat i-t cells not relevant to a given relative-time period specific estimate. As such, only *att* and *att_it* type results will be presented when relative-period specific estimates are requested. For the *att_i* and *att_itime* concepts, *att_it* type results will be shown.

## 8. Subgroups

### Rules of the road

- The subgroup variable partitions treated cohorts: non-negative integers on treated rows, missing on every row of a unit to leave that unit out of every subgroup. A unit labelled on some rows and missing on others is refused; so is a subgroup that varies within a cohort-by-period cell or changes over time within a unit. `safety(off)` proceeds with share weighting, which changes what `att_i` and `att_itime` mean. Under `permute()` the partial-labelling refusal covers every unit, never-treated ones included, and `safety(off)` does not lift it. The usual remedy for a subgroup finer than `i` is to define `i` at the finer level (`egen newi = group(i sg), missing`, and note the `missing` option).
- Subgroup values are embedded in stored-result and plot names; recode ID-style codes to small integers first.
- wooldid reports each subgroup's effects, every pairwise contrast, and a joint homogeneity test. With `cluster()` at or above `i` the pairwise contrasts' analytic standard errors can be degenerate and are reported without inference; use `permute()`, `bootstrap()` without `boottstat`, or a finer `cluster()`.
- A triple difference is `subgroups(g) fe(i.g##i.t)`: the contrast between the two subgroup estimates is the triple-difference estimate.
- Under `permute()` with subgroups, add `permstrata(sg)` when subgroup membership is fixed at the cluster level (fill `sg` on every in-sample row first, since `permstrata()` refuses a missing stratum value), so reassignment does not mix subgroup composition with onset timing.
- `suppresssgprimaryeffects` computes the contrasts only and is refused by both replication engines. `makecfxplotsbysg` draws the continuous-treatment plots per subgroup.

The rest of this chapter is the help file's account.

### From the help file

### Subgroup Effects

`sub:groups()`: Specifies the subgroup (SG) variable, whose levels specify subgroups for which *wooldid* will compute subgroup specific estimates. The SG variable must contain only non-negative values (or missing values) on in-sample observations of treated units - both pre- and post-onset rows - and only integers (or missing values) on those rows at or after the unit's onset, which are the rows whose values become the subgroup levels; values on never-treated observations and on rows wooldid's deterministic screens discard (e.g. missing outcomes) are ignored by the check, since no subgroup computation uses them and its levels must specify collections of cohorts i. Under `permute()` these screens, and the containment screens described below, instead run over every in-sample row rather than over treated units' rows only, because any cluster can be assigned treatment in a replication (see the `permute()` entry). A given cohort i cannot be split across multiple subgroups: *wooldid* exits with an informative error if it is. Under *safety(off)* such a run proceeds using share weighting (see the subgroup discussion below). Observations given missing values are not placed in any subgroup and are ignored when performing subgroup computations. When *esprelength()* is greater than zero and *espostlength()* is 0 - its default, and equally when 0 is requested explicitly - *wooldid* raises *espostlength()* to 1, because subgroups for which pre-treatment effects are not estimable can otherwise crash the run; the change prints a caution under default safety and is made silently under *safety(off)*. Specify *espostlength(-999)* to keep the post-treatment window empty. Because subgroup values are embedded in the names of stored results and plots, values 8 or more digits long - 7, with `makecfxplotsbysg` and 3-digit relative-year plot requests - can hit Stata's 31/32-character name limits and crash some option combinations with r(198); recode ID-style subgroup codes to small integers first (e.g., `egen newsg = group(oldsg)`). *impvalues* is refused outright alongside *subgroups()*: Ibragimov and Muller inference over subgroup estimates is not implemented, and the combination exits with r(198) rather than dropping either request. 

`suppress:sgprimaryeffects`: Skips estimation of subgroup-specific treatment effects and computes only the differences in effects between SGs. This option should speed estimation if these treatment effects are not otherwise of interest. `bootstrap()` and `permute()` both refuse it, because the suppressed subgroup entries are exactly what their replication vectors need in order to resample the subgroup statistics. The rank-deficiency screen described below still applies: it reads the subgroup level standard errors that the pairwise comparison itself returns, posted hidden as *e(wdid_sglev)*. 

** Description of Approach to Subgroup Effect Estimation:** Given a variable dividing cohorts i into different subgroups, *wooldid* will estimate treatment effects separately for each subgroup (i.e., each level of the SG variable). This is achieved without modification to the underlying regression specification in step 1 (see the Orientation chapter). Rather, it is done by carrying out the margins calculations in step (2) within the subsamples of the data specified by the subgroup variable. Additionally, *wooldid* computes all possible pairwise comparisons of the overall effects (and pre-treatment effects) estimates by SG (e.g., SG 1 vs 2, 1 vs 3, 2 vs 3, etc.), presenting for each comparison the difference in effect estimates and a p-value for each difference. A joint test of the hypothesis that all SGs share the same effect estimate (normalized to be the effect estimate of the numerically lowest numbered SG) is also computed, except under `fastnose` (no such test is posted at all) and for any family the rank-deficiency screen below withholds (posted as missing). Note: standard errors for subgroup-specific estimates can fail when using clustered standard errors in conjunction with subgroups that contain few (or only one) cluster.

Separately, when the clustering is at the level of the cohort variable *i* or above, the analytic covariance of two subgroups' effects can be numerically rank-deficient in the contrast direction - the two level standard errors agree to several digits and the contrast standard error is the cancellation residue between them, not an estimated variance (the same degeneracy named in the `jointtests` caution). *wooldid* screens each pairwise row on the implied correlation between the two subgroups' estimates and, above 1 - 1e-2 (or where the contrast standard error is itself missing, the extreme of the same failure), reports that row's standard error, statistic, p-value and interval and the across-subgroup equality test covering it as **absent**, with a caution naming the count; the point estimates are unaffected. `safety(off)` reports them with the same caution instead of withholding them, and the screen is inert under `fastnose`, which computes no standard errors at all, and under `bootstrap()` and `permute()`, whose replication shells rewrite these cells with their own inference. Use `permute()`, `bootstrap()` without `boottstat` (which studentizes each contrast by exactly that standard error), or a clustering strictly finer than the cohort-by-period cells, which helps only where that finer clustering leaves enough distinct clusters inside each treated cohort-by-period cell. 

Each treated unit of *i* should lie wholly within one subgroup. Because subgroups never enter the regression itself (they only select and reweight the unit-by-period effect cells), a subgroup variable that varies **within** a unit-by-period cell makes subgroup contrasts structurally unable to reflect within-cell subgroup differences, and *wooldid* exits with an informative error (bypass with *safety(off)*; the usual remedy is defining *i* at the finer level over which the subgroup varies, e.g. *egen newi = group(i sg), missing*). The *missing* option matters: a bare *egen ... group()* returns missing wherever any grouping variable is missing, so on data whose subgroup variable has gaps it would blank *newi* on those rows and *wooldid*'s markout would then drop them from the estimation sample, silently changing the pooled *att*, the never-treated control mass and *e(N)*. Note also that splitting *i* this way redefines the estimand: *att_i* and *att_itime* then average over the split pseudo-units rather than over units. A subgroup variable that changes over time within a unit is likewise refused under default safety. With *safety(off)* such a run proceeds with share weighting - *att_i* and *att_itime* enter a split unit into each subgroup weighted by its share of sample weight there rather than with equal unit weighting, while by-subgroup *att* and *att_it* are unaffected - and a caution prints. Observations with missing subgroup values remain allowed and belong to no subgroup. 

A special case to consider is the difference-in-differences-in-differences (triple diff) case. In the triple diff case, all cohorts are categorized into two groups, with both groups existing among treated cohorts and control cohorts. The target estimand may be thought of as the difference in the diff-in-diff estimates obtained separately for each of the two groups. To implement a triple diff design in *wooldid*, one can specify the variable containing the two triple diff groups as a SG variable and then manually specify inclusion of SG by period fixed effects (i.e., specifying *subgroups(sgvarname) fe(i.sgvarname##i.t)*). The difference in SG level estimates returned will be the triple diff estimate.

## 9. Continuous treatments

### Decision rules

The continuous-treatment model gives every treated cohort-by-period cell its own intercept and its own dose slope, and a control function `g()` sets the reference dose effect the treated cells are compared with. The choices, in order:

1. **`contreatcontrols()` is required** and encodes the identifying assumption. `basic` (one common baseline dose slope), `byTreated`, `byTtre` and `byCohort` are the dose analogues of unit fixed effects at increasing granularity; `byTime` adds period-specific dose slopes, the analogue of the second difference, and can be combined with one or more of the others. `untreatedZero` drops `g()` entirely and is only for a dose that is genuinely zero outside treated cells. Every type needs dose variation across the reference-period observations of each group it fits a baseline slope for; `byTime` with a zero dose for never-treated units after the last onset is refused.
2. **`contreatwithin`** identifies from within-treated-cohort dose variation with no untreated group; `byTime` is then refused, since the period slopes would absorb genuine effects.
3. **`lattice()`** lets the dose slope and intercept vary within cells across dose bins. It is the most expensive option, it needs at least two distinct dose values inside every cell-by-bin piece (a rule of thumb: several units contributing to a cell per bin), and the lattice intercepts can make `ame` and `att` disagree. Prefer the smallest `latticenum` that answers the question, and read `att` where the intercepts carry the effect.
4. **Inference.** With `cluster()` at or above `i` the `ame` standard errors are not identified and are reported as missing, with `e(V_es_ame*)` withheld; use `bootstrap()`, `permute()`, `impvalues`, `robust` where errors are independent, or a `cluster()` finer than the cells. Joint tests under a continuous treatment also need many observations per cell.
5. **Identification screens.** A dose constant within each cohort leaves every treated-cell slope collinear; under default safety wooldid reports the `ame` results as missing rather than the regression's structural zeros (the raw `e(b)` still carries the zeros). With a lattice or polynomial, the screens are less able to detect a partialled-out slope, so check the design yourself.
6. `contreatelasticitytype(dyex)` reports the effect per proportional change in the dose; under `poisson` `dydx` is already a semi-elasticity. `makecfxplots` draws effects by dose bin (for `att` only unless `safety(off)`); `gteffects` is unavailable under `contreat()`.

The rest of this chapter is the help file's account, including the fuller discussion of `f()`, `g()` and the lattice.

### From the help file

### Continuous Treatments

`contreat(varname)`: The continuous treatment variable. Must be defined for all observations (set to 0 if not naturally defined within untreated cohort-periods); observations on which it is missing are dropped from the estimation sample, appearing only in the header's dropped-observation count. With continuous treatments, reliable analytic inference additionally requires that cohort-by-period cells contain a large number of observations relative to the number of within-cell parameters estimated. If the continuous treatment carries no identifying variation for any treated cohort-period cell's dose slope (for example, a dose constant within each cohort - every treated-cell slope term is then dropped for multicollinearity), the within-cell average marginal effects are not identified: under default safety *wooldid* names this situation, reports the ame results as missing rather than as the regression's structural zeros, and continues with the unaffected att results (expected zero doses in untreated periods, as under *contreatcontrols(untreatedZero)*, do not trigger this). Only the raw *e(b)* coefficient vector retains the structural zeros, since Stata cannot post missing coefficients, and the check is skipped under *fastnose*. Not available with `gte:ffects`: under a continuous treatment the cohort-by-period interaction coefficients are dose-zero intercepts rather than treatment effects, so that combination is rejected with an error; aggregate *e(b_es_ame)* or the cfx materials instead.

`contreatelas:ticitytype(dydx/dyex)`: Specifies which type of marginal effect of the continuous treatment to compute. *dydx* is the default and reports the average marginal effect itself (change in y/unit change in continuous treatment). *dyex* reports a semi-elasticity: the change in y per proportional change in the continuous treatment. A supplied value outside this list is rejected with an error, whether or not `contreat(varname)` is specified; without `contreat(varname)` the option is otherwise inert, its value read by nothing and no message printed. **Under *poisson*** results are on the linear index *xb* = ln E[y|x], where *dydx* already gives the semi-elasticity of E[y] and *dyex* already gives the elasticity of E[y] with respect to the dose. 

`contreatw:ithin`: Specify this when you wish to use only within-treated cohort variation in the continuous treatment variable. This option disables some of *safety*'s features (the estimability-relaxation checks; the data-integrity screens, such as onset constancy within units, and the dose-slope screen described under `contreat(varname)` with its interactive-control twin, still run unless *safety(off)* is given explicitly), turns off estimability checks that would otherwise cause *wooldid* to stop estimation in the absence of any untreated cohorts, and causes *wooldid* to skip sample cleaning routines that drop time periods for which a control cohort is not present. Requires `contreat(varname)`: because this option disables those checks, requesting it without a continuous treatment is rejected with an error rather than silently ignored. 

`contreatp:oly(integer)`: Includes polynomial terms in *contreat* of the specified degree. The value is validated whether or not *contreat()* is given, but without *contreat()* the option is otherwise inert: nothing reads it and nothing is said about it, unlike *contreatwithin*, *lattice()* and *contreatcontrols()*, each of which is an error on its own. The degree must be a positive integer (default 1; 0 and negative values are rejected). Be cautious using higher degree polynomials. 

`lattice(latticevar OR latticetype latticenum)`: Specifies a lattice variable, a variable specifying groups of observations across which the effect of *contreat* is allowed to vary, even within cohort-periods and within the reference period. If just the name of a variable is passed to *lattice()*, this variable will be used as the lattice variable. If instead a lattice type and a lattice number are passed, this option will generate a lattice variable using *latticenum* quantiles, where *latticenum* must be an integer greater than 1, with the quantiles being formed within groups according to the *latticetype* (see below). Any specified sample weights will be used when constructing the lattice, unless the option *latticeignoreweights* is specified (which constructs the lattice quantiles without using weights). If *lattice* is not specified, *wooldid* will proceed without using a lattice variable (as though you specified a lattice variable that is always 1); *lattice()* without `contreat(varname)` is rejected with an error rather than silently ignored. A user-supplied lattice variable must be numeric and contain only non-negative integers (it is used as a factor variable; the value check can be bypassed with *safety(off)*); a lattice type passed without a quantile count is rejected with a specific error at parse time. Observations on which a user-supplied lattice variable is missing are dropped from the estimation sample, surfacing only in the header's dropped-observation count; a generated lattice instead places null values in bin 0. Be aware that a lattice is the most expensive scaling axis in *wooldid*: runtime grows at roughly the 1.9th power of the cohort-by-period cell count once a lattice is present (versus about the 1.3rd to 1.7th power without one), and the absorbing regression rather than *margins* becomes the dominant cost. Each additional lattice point multiplies the terms the regression must carry, so prefer the smallest *latticenum* that answers the question. See Runtime and what drives it. When a lattice is combined with *contreatcontrols(basic)*, the baseline dose level is always partialled out, within each lattice level: the lattice-level dose control is in the model, and because it spans the pooled one the default estimation path absorbs that one alone, while the paths carrying these terms as explicit regressors (*ccnoabsorb*, *unconditionalse*, *customvce()*) carry both, the spelling there governing which column the collinearity drop takes.

*latticetype:* Specifies groups in which to estimate *latticenum* quantiles of *contreat*. Type names are matched case-sensitively and must be typed exactly as spelled below. Choice of lattice type can be important for both interpretive reasons (e.g., when interpreting the output of *makecfxplots*) and for estimation feasibility reasons: clustered standard error computation will often fail if multiple clusters are not represented at each lattice point. Lattice type options include: 

- ***all*** Quantiles are formed looking at the full, within-sample distribution of the continuous treatment variable, including values for untreated observations. 

- ***byTime*** Quantiles are formed using the distribution of the continuous treatment variable within each time period t separately. Observations will thus be placed in the lowest quantile bin if their value of *contreat* is in the lowest *latticenum*-th percent for their time period, even if their value would be high in the sample overall. 

- ***byTreated*** Quantiles are formed separately in the treated and untreated groups. 

- ***byTtre*** Quantiles are formed separately for the control group and for each treatment onset cohort. 

- ***byCohort*** Quantiles are formed separately within each cohort i. 

- ***byTreatedByTime*** Quantiles are formed by time period t, separately for the treated and untreated groups. 

- ***byTtreByTime*** Quantiles are formed by time period t, separately for the control group and each treatment onset cohort. 

- ***byCohortByTime*** Quantiles are formed separately by cohort and time period t. 

- ***baseTreated*** Quantiles are formed within the treated group. These quantile range definitions are then used to define lattice points for the rest of the sample. 

- ***baseTreatedRef*** Quantiles are formed within the reference period for the treated group. These quantile range definitions are then used to define lattice points for the rest of the sample. 

- ***baseTreatedPost*** Quantiles are formed within the treated period for the treatment group. These quantile range definitions are then used to define lattice points for the rest of the sample. 

- ***baseTreatedPre*** Quantiles are formed within the full pre-treatment period for the treatment group. These quantile range definitions are then used to define lattice points for the rest of the sample. 

`latticeig:noreweights`: When constructing a lattice, this option forces *wooldid* to ignore sample weights when computing quantiles for lattice production. 

`makec:fxplots`: Produce plots presenting the average marginal effect of *contreat* and the average treatment effect within each bin specified by either the lattice variable or, if specified, the variable in *cfxlattice()*. One of *lattice()* or *cfxlattice()* must be present: requesting the plots with neither is rejected with an error. These plots are only sure to be valid for the ATT estimand type, but may be valid for others if lattice groupings do not crosscut cohorts (or, less stringently, cohort-periods, for ATT_it). For that reason *wooldid* produces them only for the *att* estimand unless *safety(off)* is in force; *contreatwithin* relaxes safety internally and has the same effect. Requires a continuous treatment (`contreat(varname)`), and is not compatible with `imp:values`. 

`cfxlattice(varname)`: A variable containing the bins in which effects are to be calculated for the continuous treatment effects plots. If not specified, the lattice variable will be used. The variable must be numeric and, on observations with a nonmissing treatment onset within the sample restriction, contain only non-negative integers (*margins* accepts only non-negative integers in *over()*; the value check can be bypassed with *safety(off)*). Values on never-treated observations are ignored: no continuous treatment effects plot uses those rows. If the variable has missing values on observations with a nonmissing onset, *wooldid* prints a Caution: *margins* silently omits those observations from the plotted bin averages (this Caution, like the value check above, is suppressed by *safety(off)*). These screens apply only when *makecfxplots* is actually requested; *cfxlattice()* without *makecfxplots* has no effect and draws a Caution. Note that *makecfxplots* requires a continuous treatment (*contreat()*); previously, requesting it with *cfxlattice()* alone was silently ignored. 

`cfxplott:ypes(pre post integers)`: A list of types of cfxplots to be produced. If *post* is among the types specified, plots will be produced giving treatment effects across the continuous treatment variable within the post-treatment period. If *pre* is among the types specified, plots will be produced within the pre-treatment period. If any integers are specified, plots will be produced just within the specified periods relative to treatment. If nothing is specified, the option post is assumed. *pre* and out-of-window relative-period integers are dropped from the request: *pre* requires *esfixedbaseperiod* or *esprelength()* > 0, a positive integer must be no larger than *espostlength()*, and a negative integer's magnitude must be no larger than *esprelength()*. *esfixedbaseperiod* lifts both integer limits but discards the *esrelativeto()* reference period itself. Dropping and rejection are different things: a token that is neither *post* nor *pre* must be a number, and one that is neither (*postpost*, *2post2*, a variable name) stops the run with an error naming the entry, as does a numeric token that is missing or not an integer. A leading plus sign is accepted and stripped, so `cfxplottypes(+2)` and `cfxplottypes(2)` are the same request and the repetition is removed; repeated relative periods are collapsed to one, first occurrence kept. Only the window screens described above drop silently, so a request in which nothing survives completes normally with no continuous treatment effects plot and no message. 

`makecfxplotsbysg`: Produce continuous treatment effects plots for each group specified in the subgroup variable. It needs both *makecfxplots* and *subgroups()* and is silently inert without either: no error and no caution, unlike *cfxlattice()* without *makecfxplots*. Being part of the *makecfxplots* output, it carries that option's estimand restriction; see `makec:fxplots` above. Each subgroup's plot data comes back in a matrix named *e(cfx*item*_*type*_sg*sgcase*)*, for example *e(cfxatt_cfxpost_sg1)* or *e(cfxame_relyr2_sg3)*, where the suffix is `_sg` followed by the subgroup value. Note that this is the one family of stored results using `_sg`: the subgroup twins elsewhere use `_s` (*e(escomp_s1)*, *e(b_es_att_s1)*) or a bare underscore (*e(wdidsgresults_1)*). *e(wdid_cfxmats)* lists every cfx matrix name a run actually posted. 

`cfxplotnose`: Do not estimate standard errors for the cfxplots; speeds estimation. Without *makecfxplots* the option is inert: nothing reads it, and no message is printed, unlike *cfxlattice()*, which draws a caution when it is specified on its own. Under `permute()` the truncation does not apply: the six-column shape of the returned *e(cfx*)* matrices is kept, missing-padded because `margins` returns a short *r(table)* when it computes no standard errors, precisely so the permutation rewiring can fill the p-value and interval columns from the replications. A `permute()` run that specifies *cfxplotnose* therefore still gets permutation inference on the plot cells wherever the lattice type is assignment-invariant, and the blanking caution and *e(perm_cfx_blank)* apply to it as to any other `permute()` run. Under `permtstat` (or `permtci`, which implies it) *cfxplotnose* is dropped with a caution when `permute()` and *makecfxplots* are given: the studentized statistic divides each plot cell by its own standard error, in the observed run and in every replication, so without those standard errors every cell would be left without inference. The cells are then computed, and permuted, exactly as under `permtstat` without *cfxplotnose*. `bootstrap()` does not carve it out, so there the cells stay analytic. 

`contreatc:ontrols(controltype)`: Specifies what controls for *contreat* will be added: the specified controls are always equal to f(Contreat_jt) (the function used to capture the effect of contreat within treated i-t cells) interacted with a set of fixed effects-like indicator variables (time period, cohort, etc.). The specified controls will partial out the effect of *contreat* in a particular way, affecting the nature of the reference effect of *contreat* to which estimated effects of *contreat* in treated cohort-periods are compared. This option must be specified to proceed with a continuous treatment (and is rejected with an error if specified without one), and care must be chosen when specifying it: different options correspond with very different substantive assumptions. See more on this topic in the description section below. 

*conttreatcontroltype*: untreatedZero, which cannot be paired with any other type; or one or more of the following: basic, byTreated, byTtre, byCohort, and byTime 

- ***basic*** Controls for the effect of contreat (i.e., for f(Contreat_jt)) are added without any interactions.

- ***byTreated*** Controls for the effect of contreat are interacted with indicators for membership in the treatment group and in the control group. 

- ***byTtre*** Controls for the effect of contreat are interacted with indicators for membership in each treatment onset group and in the control group. 

- ***byCohort*** Controls for the effect of contreat are interacted with cohort (i) membership indicators. Each treated cohort must therefore have variation in *contreat* across its untreated/reference-period observations; a cohort that does not is named and the run stops, with no *safety(off)* escape. 

- ***byTime*** Controls for the effect of contreat are interacted with time period (t) indicator variables. 

- ***untreatedZero*** Special option to formulate the regression to work when a continuous treatment variable is always equal to 0 outside of treated cohort-periods. Setting the continuous treatment variable to always equal 0 outside of treated cohort-periods can be an appropriate way to handle cases where the variable is unknowable or undefined outside of treated cohort-periods. If this option is specified when the always 0 condition is not met, *wooldid* will return poorly identified estimates. 

**On Specifying contreatcontrols() and lattice:** As discussed in the Orientation chapter, the regression equation *wooldid* adopts when estimating the effects of a continuous treatment variable is: Y_jt = fe_i + fe_t + sum_over_it_in_Z(beta_it * TreatedFlag_it * Tz_it + TreatedFlag_it * Tz_it * f(Contreat_jt)) + g(f(Contreat_jt)) 

The idea behind this approach is that, just as each i-t cell is allowed its own treatment effect when a binary treatment is specified, here, each i-t cell is allowed its own treatment effect intercept and its own marginal effect of the continuous treatment variable. If cohort-period cells are large enough that concerns remain that the effect of Contreat_jt may be non-linear within those cells, the function f() can be used to allow for use of a more complicated function of the effect of Contreat_jt within i-t cells.

The options *contreatpoly()* and *lattice()* determine the contents of the function f(), which in general is a stepwise function of contreat. If no lattice is specified, f(Contreat_jt) = beta_it1*Contreat_jt^1 + beta_it2*Contreat_jt^2 + ... + beta_itp*Contreat_jt^p, where p is the polynomial order specified in *contreatpoly(p)*. If a lattice variable is specified, this is modified to f(Contreat_jt) = sum_over_l_in_L[L_l + L_l * Poly(Contreat_jt)], where L_l is an indicator variable that is one if a given observation falls in lattice bin l and is 0 otherwise and where Poly(Contreat_jt) is a term capturing the user specified number of polynomial terms of Contreat_jt. 

It is important to note that specifying a lattice has two effects on the function f(). First, it allows the marginal effect of Contreat_jt to vary within i-t cells, whereas before it could not. Second, it adds additional treatment effect intercept terms that exist within i-t cells. A similar effect to including a lattice thus could be achieved via an alternative, finer definition of the cohort variable i. This has some implications for interpreting average marginal effect estimates in the presence of a lattice. AME-type estimates do not capture the impact of discontinuities in the effect of Contreat_jt -- more precisely, they do not capture the effect of the intercept terms a lattice variable might add to f() (nor that of the intercept terms associated with the cohort-period indicator variables themselves). When a specified lattice has many groups and almost all of the effect of Contreat_jt is captured by the intercept lattice terms alone, the identification of marginal effects of Contreat_jt can be threatened. AME and ATT estimates can also be discordant under certain circumstances: for example, if within each i-t cell, Y = -Contreat + 100*floor(Contreat/10), the entire positive effect of Contreat could be loaded onto the intercept terms and yield a positive ATT coupled with a negative AME. In such cases, it may be wise to either use a coarser lattice or to focus on average treatment effect estimates, which do capture the impact of those intercept terms -- this is especially the case when considering continuous treatment effects plots. 

A second, distinct requirement is support, and the short form of it is this: **take care not to over-saturate the specification with lattice terms.** Each treated cohort-period cell must still hold at least two distinct values of Contreat_jt within each lattice bin it occupies, since that is the only variation identifying that piece's slope. This is not the functional-form threat described just above -- it bites even when the dose response is exactly linear with no bin structure at all. When Contreat_jt is close to constant within units and i is coarser than the unit, a lattice can cut cells into pieces of one or two observations; the pieces with fewer than two distinct values are dropped for collinearity, the reported average marginal effect then averages only the pieces that survived, and the analytic standard errors on such a design can be very much smaller than the estimates' sampling variability. A useful rule of thumb is that the number of units contributing to a treated cell should be several times the number of lattice bins. Unlike the cohort trend case discussed under *timetrends*, this degeneracy does not depend on `cluster()`: where it is severe enough that the estimation command returns an all-zero covariance, it does so under *robust* as readily as under `cluster()`. Where nothing was clustered, *wooldid* names the lattice as the likely cause when it reports the all-zero covariance; where `cluster()` was given, the cell-containment caution speaks first.

The function g(f(Contreat_jt)) is a control function of Contreat_jt intended to serve as the continuous treatment-equivalent of the binary treatment's fixed effects. As such, g() determines to a significant degree what source of variation is used to identify the effect of Contreat_jt. This leads to a complex set of choices: since average marginal effects of Contreat_jt can be identified using only within cohort-period variation in treatment intensity, many approaches to identification exist.

The first set of options for specifying g() include the control types *basic*, *byTreated*, *byTtre*, and *byCohort*. These options are analogous to specifying different types of unit fixed effects, and are implemented by including interactions between f(Contreat_jt) and then either a constant (*basic*), a treatment vs control group indicator (*byTreated*), a set of treatment onset group indicators (*byTtre*), or a set of cohort indicators (*byCohort*). For example, when *basic* is specified, g(f(Contreat_jt)) = f(Contreat_jt), while when *byCohort* is specified, g(f(Contreat_jt)) = sum_over_i[fe_i * f(Contreat_jt)]. Since the treatment effects of Contreat_jt are estimated using interactions between treated i-t cell indicator variables and f(Contreat_jt), these different g() options set the reference effect of Contreat_jt relative to which Contreat_jt effects are estimated in treated cells. 

In addition to the above control types, *byTime* can be specified to add interactions between time period indicator variables and f(Contreat_jt) to g(). These time period interactions can serve a role analogous to that of the second diff in the binary difference-in-differences case, partialling out time period specific effects of Contreat_jt. The control type *byTime* can be specified on its own, or in conjunction with another control type. For example, if the control types specified are *byCohort* and *byTime* are both specified, one would have g() = sum_over_i[fe_i*f(Contreat_jt)] + sum_over_t[fe_t*f(Contreat_jt)]. 

If the continuous treatment variable is not meaningfully defined outside of treated cohort-periods, the control type *untreatedZero* can be used to specify g() = 0. By not including a g() function at all, identification of marginal effects of Contreat_jt will occur using only within treated cohort-period variation. Note: specifying *untreatedZero* makes other technical changes to the estimation process in order to facilitate this case and should not be specified outside of this narrow situation. 

If attempting to identify the average marginal effects of a continuous treatment variable without a strictly untreated control group (i.e., by exploiting within treated cohort variation in treatment intensity), the option *contreatwithin* should be specified. This will prevent *wooldid* from objecting to the presence of time periods without a control group and to the presence of i-t fixed effects (if specified). When specified, *wooldid* will still present ATT-type average treatment effects estimates, which in this case should essentially be the integral of the estimated average marginal effects across the distribution of the continuous treatment variable. Note: the control type *byTime* is refused in this case, since in the absence of an untreated group, the time period interactions will force *wooldid* to partial out genuine treatment effects for some group, threatening identification. Specify *safety(off)* before the run to proceed anyway, which downgrades the refusal to a caution.

## 10. Controls and fixed effects

### What each option does to the regression

| option | enters the regression as | effect on reported treatment effects |
|---|---|---|
| `controls()` | passed to `reghdfe` unchanged | partialled out |
| `fe()` | absorbed fixed effects (`i.` assumed on the default path; explicit on the regress path) | partialled out |
| `timetrends` | cohort-specific linear trends `i.i##c.t`, plus the sample and normalization changes they need | partialled out; with `cluster()` on `i` the covariance is typically unidentified |
| `coarsecohortcontrols()` | the control plus its interaction with every treated cell dummy (factor notation required) | partialled out, cell by cell |
| `interactivecontrols()` | the control interacted with period dummies, plus its cohort-demeaned value interacted with every treated cell dummy | reported as part of the effects (the Wooldridge covariate approach) |
| `ccc_absorb()` | as `coarsecohortcontrols()`, continuous variables only, absorbed | partialled out |

### Identification traps

- A `fe()` term whose groups are no coarser than the cohort-by-period cells (unit-by-period or cohort-by-period effects) absorbs the treatment dummies themselves; a unit-level variable under a unit-level `i` is merely collinear with the cohort effects and harmless. With an event-study window, `ccc_absorb()` of a variable constant within cells absorbs the treatment interactions and the run stops with an error. wooldid's collinearity screen catches most binary-treatment cases and errors under default safety.
- `interactivecontrols()` slopes exist only where the control varies within cells; a unit-level control with a unit-level `i` reduces the run to `controls(i.t#c.x)` under a caution. The option also turns the default cell collapse off.
- Never put the continuous treatment variable itself in `controls()`; a renamed copy is fine.
- On the regress path (`unconditionalse`, `customvce()`) a bare name in `fe()` is a continuous regressor; write `fe(i.state)`.

The rest of this chapter is the help file's account.

### From the help file

### Controls and Fixed Effects

`control:s()`: The contents of this option are passed directly to *reghdfe* as controls, without any modification (to *ppmlhdfe* under *poisson*, and to `regress` or `poisson` under *customvce()* or *unconditionalse*). 

`fe()`: The contents of this option are passed directly to *reghdfe* - or to *ppmlhdfe* under *poisson* - as fixed effects (into its absorb statement) without modification. Variables given will be interpreted as categorical variables, though i.X##c.X2 type notation is also permitted for passing heterogeneous slope controls and the like.

This holds on the default estimation path. Under *customvce()* or *unconditionalse*, estimation runs through `regress` - or `poisson` under *poisson* - rather than *reghdfe*, and a BARE token there is read as a CONTINUOUS variable: *fe(state)* fits a linear term in the state code rather than a set of state indicators. Write the prefix explicitly - *fe(i.state)* - whenever those options are in use, or on any specification you may later run both ways.

`timetrend:s`: Control for cohort specific time trends in the regression (i.e., for i.i##c.t). Importantly, this option is required for heterogeneous time trend controls to work, even when they are specified manually, as it also updates *wooldid*'s various estimability checks, drops cohorts without sufficient pre-treatment data to estimate a time trend, and, in the fixed reference period case, ensures that the two reference periods that must be normalized to 0 to estimate the time trend are chronologically adjacent. This option does not automatically include any time trends that are a function of any included continuous treatment variable. 

With `cluster()` on the cohort identifier *i*, the cohort trend terms typically leave the covariance non-identified: the estimation command returns an all-zero covariance, *wooldid* says so and continues, the reported estimates stand, and every standard error, test statistic, p-value and interval in the run is missing. *e(V_es_*)* is then not posted and *e(wdid_vcvstate)* reads *absent*, so *honestdid*, *pretrendpower()*, the joint tests and *simultaneousbands* have no input. The same specification under *robust* is unaffected. 

`coarse:cohortcontrols()`: Includes the specified controls as well as interactions between them with all Tz_it dummies included in the model, meaning one interaction with each cohort-time period for which a treatment effect is estimated (if *esfixedbaseperiod* is specified, that includes every estimable treated cohort by relative time period effect). Note that reported treatment effects will not vary as a direct function of these controls; the effect of these controls are partialled out from the reported treatment effects. This option differs from simply specifying cohort by time period interactions with a given control in that it pools the effect of the controls in the reference period and the control group. Note that all specified coarse cohort controls must be specified using factor notation, so that *wooldid* knows if they are to be interacted as a continuous variable (prefixed with "c.") or as a categorical variable (prefixed with "i."). 

`inter:activecontrols()`: Includes the specified variables in the regression in two capacities. The cell slopes exist only where a control varies within cohort-period cells; if it does not (for example *i* is the unit and the control is a unit-level variable), every slope term drops out and the run is identical to *controls(i.t#c.x)* - *wooldid* then prints a caution naming the control (that check does not run under *fastnose* or *safety(off)*, where the reduction is silent). First, the variables are included as controls interacted with time period-specific indicator variables. Second, the variables are de-meaned by cohort (i) and then treatment effects are permitted to vary linearly with the de-meaned variables. This is achieved by including interactions between the de-meaned controls and all Tz_it dummies included in the model. The specification used is similar to as with *coarsecohortcontrols*, but differs in that these terms are marked so that variation associated with them is reported as part of the treatment effects (as opposed to being partialled out from the treatment effects). This specification most closely mimics the approach to including covariates that appears in Wooldridge (2025). When using only one interactive control, ATTs obtained using this option should be similar to those obtained when specifying that variable as a continuous treatment variable and then separately including controls that interact the continuous treatment variable with time dummy variables. The treatment-effect interactions are built for the treated cohort-by-period cells only: the pooled reference cell (untreated observations) carries no interaction slope, so its de-meaned-control variation is partialled out through the time-period interactions and the reference-period effect is a structural zero.

This option also puts the run outside the default cell collapse: the *margins* stage is evaluated on the full estimation sample, under a caution naming the option (see *nofastcollapse*). 

**Caution Regarding Continuous Treatment Variables and Controls:** If seeking to manually specify additional controls as a function of a continuous treatment variable, be sure not to include the continuous treatment variable itself among your controls, as this can adversely affect estimates produced in *wooldid*'s margins calculation stage. It is okay to include a control with values identical to that of the continuous treatment variable: it just needs to have a different variable name. It is recommended that any custom such controls be specified in conjunction with the option *contreatcontrols(basic)*, or any option other than *untreatedZero*. 

**Caution Regarding Continuous Treatments and Cluster-Robust Standard Errors:** Valid clustered standard errors cannot be obtained analytically for the ame-family quantities when clustering at the cohort (*i*) level or above, necessitating use of bootstrapping or other methods (see standard_errors above). With `contreat()`, the regression gives every treated cohort-by-period cell its own treatment-intensity slope. When any such cell lies entirely within a single cluster of the specified `cluster()` (which is automatic when clustering on the cohort identifier *i* itself), the cluster-robust variance estimator is structurally blind to within-cell variation in the continuous treatment: the "standard errors" it would deliver for the ame-family quantities do not measure their sampling variability (in the *contreatcontrols(basic)*/*byTime* cases they collapse to the variance of the absorbed baseline dose control, one number repeated across every ame quantity — including configurations where the covariance appears full rank). A cell holding a single observation is not counted: its own intercept fits it exactly, so it adds nothing to the cluster-robust covariance, and a `cluster()` finer than the cells does not trip the screen just because an unbalanced panel thins some treated cell to one row. *wooldid* detects this design, prints an explanatory caution, sets the ame-family standard errors, tests, and joint diagnostics (adversarial ame p-values, ame joint pre-trend tests, subgroup ame comparisons, ame pre-trend power) to missing, and withholds *e(V_es_ame*)* - and with it any simultaneous band (*simultaneousbands*) for the ame families, which is dropped without a further caution whenever some other family still gets one. The same screen blanks the inference columns of every *e(cfx**ame item**_**type**)* matrix and its per-subgroup twins, keeping the point-estimate column alone, and so draws the ame continuous-treatment figures and their saved `.gph` files with no confidence interval: `marginsplot` is called with *noci* rather than the usual shaded band. The att-side cfx matrices and figures are untouched. Keep this case apart from the dose-slope refusal described under *e(b_es_**type**)*, where the point-estimate column goes too and the ame cfx matrices come back entirely missing. The run still returns the unaffected point estimates and *e(b_es_ame*)*. The screen runs only on the default analytic path: it is skipped under *fastnose*, which asks *margins* for no variance at all; under *impvalues*, whose cross-cluster dispersion inference stays valid here; and under *unconditionalse*, which is a different variance estimand and reports ame standard errors on such a design without this caution.
