- **SG Effect Homogeneity Tests** For each estimate type (ATT, ATT_it, AME, etc.), the joint test that all subgroups have the same such post-treatment effect (namely, the same effect as the lowest numbered SG) may be found in the scalar *e(joint_att_allsgssame)* and analogously named scalars for other treatment effect types. For pre-treatment effects, the test can be found in objects with names like *e(joint_pre_att_allsgssame)*. A test whose pairwise covariance is rank-deficient is stored as missing rather than computed from that covariance - see the Subgroups chapter.

*e(esband_engine)* takes three values: *analytic-simulated* when `bootstrap()` was not used, drawing from the family's own analytic correlation matrix (a multivariate *t* with the regression's residual degrees of freedom where it has them); *bootstrap* under `bootstrap()` without `boottstat`, where the deviations are scaled by the coefficient-draw standard deviation; and *bootstrap-studentized* under `bootstrap()` with `boottstat`, where each replication's own standard error is the denominator and the observed analytic ones set the endpoints. Code keying on the exact string must accept all three, since *bootstrap-studentized* is still a bootstrap band. Note also that the column count of *e(esband_*type*)* is the number of horizons actually banded, which on the simulated engine can be fewer than the family has; see `simult:aneousbands`. Absent when the option was not given. 

- ***e(wdid_vblocks)*, *e(wdid_vblockn)*** The event-study families whose genuine covariance blocks were spliced into *e(V)*, and the number of off-diagonal pairs written. **What this means for** *e(V)*: it is a **mixed** matrix. Within one event-study family - the relative-period rows of *att*, *att_it*, *ame* or *ame_it*, per subgroup - the off-diagonals are the real joint covariance of those estimates, because a family's horizons all come from a single `margins` call whose *r(V)* is exactly that covariance, so a `test` or `lincom` **across the horizons of one family** is valid. Everywhere else the off-diagonals are still zero and still mean "not estimated", not "zero covariance": a test spanning two families, or a family and a pooled estimate, is not valid on this matrix and will overstate its precision. The general cross-family matrix is deliberately not built - three modes (`impvalues`, `unconditionalse`, `fastnose`) have no common delta-method object, and a pooled effect is a linear combination of its own horizons, so the full matrix would be rank-deficient by construction. A family is spliced only when every horizon carrying a variance in *e(V)* matches a row of *e(V_es_*)* by name and agrees with it numerically; rows whose variance was never identified keep a zero row and column. **Under** `bootstrap()` these two results describe the **preserved analytic** matrix, not the reported one: the splice runs before the replications are wired in, so the blocks are in the hidden *e(V_analytic)* while *e(V)* holds the full replication covariance (*e(wdid_vcvstate)* reads *bootstrap* there and is the field to check). 

- ***e(wdid_vcvstate)*** What *e(V)* is on this run, in one word, so downstream code can check rather than infer: *analytic-diagonal* (variances only, every off-diagonal zero - a `test` or `lincom` spanning two estimates is NOT valid on it), *analytic-blocks* (as diagonal, plus the genuine within-family event-study blocks described under *e(wdid_vblocks)* - the default on any analytic run that produced an event study), *bootstrap* (the full replication covariance, with *e(df_r)* aligned so a hand-run `test` reproduces the `bootdisplay(wald)` table), or *absent* (no *e(V)* posted at all - the `permute()` fast-mode default, every `fastnose` run, and any run whose variance could not be posted, which the run says at the time). Under `permute()` with `permanalyticse` or `permtstat` the posted matrix is the ANALYTIC one, shown for reference: it is not a permutation quantity. 

- ***e(N)*, *e(N_dropped)*, *e(Nclus)*, *e(N_clust)*, *e(clustvar)*, *e(vce)*, *e(vcetype)*, *e(vce_stage1)*** Estimation sample observation count, number of observations dropped from initially specified sample (including rows removed because the *if* expression was nonzero but not exactly 1 - see the *if* note under Syntax; rows where it was missing are excluded from the sample and, by that same rule, are not counted here), and cluster count. *e(N_clust)* is Stata's conventional name for the same count *e(Nclus)* carries, and *e(clustvar)*, *e(vce)* and *e(vcetype)* are the conventional variance-type entries, posted so that `estout`, `esttab` and anything else keying on them finds what it expects. *e(vce)* is *cluster*, *robust*, *unconditional*, *bootstrap*, the first word of `customvce()`, or *ols* (*oim* under `poisson`) when none of those applies; *e(vcetype)* is *Robust*, *Unconditional* or *Bootstrap* where one of those fits and is absent otherwise. *unconditionalse* is a second stage on top of a clustered or robust first stage, and both stages are named rather than one: *e(vce)* and *e(vcetype)* describe the unconditional stage the reported standard errors actually come from, and *e(vce_stage1)* records the first-stage type underneath it (*cluster* or *robust*), with *e(clustvar)* still naming the design. Any run that posts no *e(V)* - see *e(wdid_vcvstate)* - leaves *e(vce)* and *e(vcetype)* empty, since they name what that matrix is; *e(clustvar)* names the design and stands either way. 

- **R^2 measures** For standard estimation, these include e(r2), e(r2adj), e(r2_within), e(r2_withinadj). For poisson estimation, these include e(r2pseud), a pseudo-R^2 measure. 

- ***e(histogramestimates)*** The cohort i-specific treatment effects used to generate histograms, when requested. Row names carry internal temporary-variable identifiers, not cohort values; the histogram itself is the intended output.

- ***e(summarystats)*** Summary statistics on the outcome variable and (if present) continuous treatment variable, when requested. The columns are *mean*, *median*, *sd*, *iqr*, *p25*, *p75*, *min* and *max*. The rows are fixed keys rather than variable names: *y_insample*, *y_pretreat*, *y_posttreat* and *y_control* for the outcome, and *c_insample*, *c_pretreat*, *c_posttreat* and *c_control* for the continuous treatment, the *c* block only under `contreat()`, the *_control* rows omitted under *contreatwithin*, and *c_pretreat* and *c_control* omitted under *contreatcontrols(untreatedZero)*. Thirteen of these are also returned directly as scalars, and only when `summarystats` is given: *e(ypre_mean)*, *e(ypre_med)*, *e(ypre_sd)* and *e(ypre_iqr)* are the mean, median, standard deviation and interquartile range of the outcome over treated units in the periods before their own onset (the *y_pretreat* row) taken with the regression weights; and under `contreat()` the same three moments of the continuous treatment on three samples, *e(contreat_mean)*/*e(contreat_med)*/*e(contreat_sd)* over the whole estimation sample, *e(contreatpost_mean)*/*e(contreatpost_med)*/*e(contreatpost_sd)* over treated units at or after onset, and *e(contreatpre_mean)*/*e(contreatpre_med)*/*e(contreatpre_sd)* over treated units before it. The *contreatpre* trio is not posted under *contreatcontrols(untreatedZero)*, following the matrix row that is omitted there for the same reason. 

- **CFX Plot Info** Information used to generate the continuous treatment effects plots. The information used to produce each plot will be stored in a matrix with the following naming pattern: e(cfx[item]_[type]), where [item] is either one of att, att_i, att_it, or att_itime (for overall average treatment effects) or one of ame, ame_i, ame_it, or ame_itime (for marginal effects of the continuous treatment). Meanwhile, [type] is either cfxpost (estimated in the post-treatment period), cfxpre (estimated in the pre-treatment period), or relyrZ (estimated in relative time period Z, where the letter n represents a negative sign). Under *makecfxplotsbysg* each plot's matrix carries a third component, *e(cfx[item]_[type]_sg[sgcase])*, where [sgcase] is the subgroup value (*e(cfxatt_cfxpost_sg1)*, *e(cfxame_relyr2_sg3)*), with the same row keys and column shape as the twins above. That suffix is `_sg`, and this is the one family of stored results that uses it: the subgroup twins elsewhere use `_s` (*e(escomp_s1)*, *e(b_es_att_s1)*) or a bare underscore (*e(wdidsgresults_1)*). *e(wdid_cfxmats)* lists every cfx matrix name a run actually posted. Matrix rows are keyed *lattice_#* (the lattice or `cfxlattice()` level, in *margins* order) with a null row equation; the same keys apply to the one-column matrices produced under `cfxplotnose`/`fastnose`, and AME rows no longer carry the dose-variable equation name. 

- ***e(sample)*** A function characterizing the estimation sample used; useful for identifying which observations are dropped by *wooldid* during its sample formation and sanitation procedures.

- ***e(cmd)*, *e(depvar)*, *e(cmdline)*** *e(cmd)* holds *wooldid* on every successful estimation, which is what a bare `wooldid` replay and any post-estimation wrapper dispatching on *e(cmd)* keys on; *e(depvar)* holds the outcome variable, the first variable of the varlist; and *e(cmdline)* holds the full *wooldid* command line for the successful estimation, beginning with the command name. These are posted, like the conventional variance entries above, so that `estimates store` and `estimates restore`, `estout`, `esttab` and anything else keying on the conventional eclass identifiers finds what it expects.

- ***e(estimate_type)*** A macro recording whether your treatment effect estimates use a pooled reference period or a fixed reference period. 

- ***Other Items:*** *e(ll)*, *e(ll_0)*, *e(rmse)*, *e(rss)*, *e(F)*, and *e(chi2)* (poisson only) are passed through directly from the underlying regression estimator, wherever available. 

In addition to these objects returned in e(), if saveplots() is specified, a dataset containing the information used to produce event study plots along with .gph files containing all graphs generated will be saved to a file with your specified name in STATA's working directory. 

If the option *verbose* is specified, the stage 1 underlying regression that *wooldid* estimates will also be stored to a model called *___wooldidfullmodel*, which can be accessed using standard *estimates restore* type syntax. Note that *___wooldidfullmodel* is a reserved scratch estimate name: *wooldid* overwrites and removes a stored estimate carrying this name on every run, including runs that end in an error (under *verbose*, *___wooldidfullmodel* is retained on failed runs as well, for inspection). Do not store your own estimates under this name. Under *verbose* with *poisson* (in the high-dimensional-fixed-effects estimation path), the *_ppmlhdfe_d* scaffolding variable that *ppmlhdfe* creates is also retained in the dataset rather than dropped at exit. Note that coefficient-level replay of the stored model (*lincom*, *test*, *matrix list e(b)*) works, but data-touching replay (*predict*, *margins*) does not: the model's regressors are internally constructed temporary variables that no longer exist after *wooldid* returns. 

**Tips for accessing output and remaking graphs:** Use the *matlist* command to print the contents of one of *wooldid*'s output matrices. To convert one of *wooldid*'s output matrices into a dataset that you can directly manipulate (e.g., to remake the automatically generated event study graphs), use the *svmat* command. For example, the command *svmat e(histogramestimates), names(col)* will create in memory a dataset containing the contents of *e(histogramestimates)* (i.e., the cohort i-specific treatment effects), using as variable names the names of the matrix's columns. The program `event_plot` can also be used to automatically generate event study plots using the information stored by *wooldid* in *e()*, though the user will need to point *event_plot* to the name of the results of interest (e.g., for ATT-type event study coefficients, by specifying *stub_lag(ES_att_post#)* and *stub_lead(ES_att_pre#)*). If taking this approach, be aware that the *event_plot* does not automatically insert 0s into the event study plot to represent the missing reference period. 

**Compatible Post-Estimation Commands:** *estimates save*, *estimates store*, *estimates restore*, *esttab*, and similar commands. 

**Replay:** Typing *wooldid* by itself after a successful estimation redisplays the full stored results from *e()* - the header, summary statistics, all results tables, the joint, adversarial, and pre-trend power diagnostics, and the event-study composition, cohort-by-period, and randomization-inference blocks when present - byte-for-byte as originally shown. Replay works after *estimates restore* of a stored *wooldid* model (subject to the failed-call refusal below), after *cleanmatrices*, and with no dataset in memory. It does not re-draw plots, does not repeat estimation-time Caution messages (which describe the estimation run, not the results), and accepts no options: any non-empty command line - including *wooldid, update* - is processed as a normal call. If a *wooldid* call fails at the parse or estimation stage, *e()* still holds the previous successful estimation (standard Stata behavior); if it fails later - diagnostics, display, or plots, after the results were posted - *e()* holds the failed call's own partial post. Either way replay refuses with an informative error until a *wooldid* call completes successfully: redisplaying results that predate the failed call would be misleading. The refusal is session-scoped (an internal *WOOLDID_LASTRC* marker; *WOOLDID_** global names are reserved by *wooldid*): it also blocks replay of models explicitly selected with *estimates restore* until some *wooldid* call completes. A failure inside the replication loop of a `permute()` or `bootstrap()` call is the one case that preserves replayability: that call's own completed ordinary estimation is restored, valid, and remains replayable, the marker is cleared, and the call exits with the loop's return code (the engine request itself failed loudly). Everything after the loop behaves like any other post-estimation failure (the rewiring of *e()* to the engine's inference, the deferred display, and the deferred continuous-treatment and event-study plots, `pretrendpower()` and the `bootsave()`/`permsave()` write), because *e()* is then a half-rewired mixture of observed and replication results, and replay is refused until some *wooldid* call completes successfully. A failure during the rewiring reports a message naming that state; a failure in one of the deferred plots is caught and reports the ordinary form. Either way, refit before replaying. Replay requires results posted by a *wooldid* version with replay support; internally, the display state travels in hidden *e(wdid_*)* results, which are implementation details and not part of the documented *e()* surface, with the exception of those that carry their own description somewhere in this help, whether in Stored Results or in an option's entry, which **are** documented and supported. Today those are the macros *e(wdid_vcvstate)* and *e(wdid_vblocks)* and the scalar *e(wdid_vblockn)* (see Stored Results), and the cell-collapse results *e(wdid_fcol)*, *e(wdid_fcskip)* and *e(wdid_fcguard)* with the scalar *e(wdid_fcncell)* (see `nofastcollapse`). Being hidden, none of the *e(wdid_*)* results appear under a plain *ereturn list*; read them by name, or use *ereturn list, all*. 

**Incompatible Post-Estimation Commands:** *lincom*, *test*, and all other post-estimation commands that rely on receiving a valid variance-covariance matrix from an estimator. While *wooldid* does return a coefficient vector (*e(b)*) and a variance-covariance matrix (*e(V)*), these are for display purposes only. On an analytic run that produced an event study the variance-covariance matrix is a **mixed** one: within a single event-study family its off-diagonals are the genuine joint covariance of those horizons, so a *test* or *lincom* across the horizons of one family **is** valid on it; everywhere else - across two families, or between a family and a pooled estimate - they are still 0 and still mean "not estimated". *e(wdid_vcvstate)* records what *e(V)* is on any given run, and *e(wdid_vblocks)* names the families whose blocks were spliced. So while *esttab* and the like will present correct estimates using these inputs, efforts to combine or otherwise test different estimates will not, in general, yield valid results. This limitation applies to the summary *e(b)*/*e(V)*; the event-study coefficients are additionally returned with a genuine full variance-covariance matrix in *e(b_es_*type*)* / *e(V_es_*type*)* (see Stored Results), and *lincom* and *test* **are** valid when applied to those (inspect the diagonal first when *e(es_ndegen_*type*)* is present: structurally degenerate rows carry zero or missing variance). *honestdid* is valid only under the positional-geometry conditions listed in the *e(b_es)* Stored Results entry. For *lincom*/*test*, first repost the event study with *ereturn post e(b_es_*type*) e(V_es_*type*)*. 

`gte:ffects`: Returns (and, for tables of up to 200 rows, prints) the individual cohort-by-relative-period effect estimates underlying *wooldid*'s aggregates (the exact building blocks for the plain binary designs the identity notes below describe; under transformations, time-varying interactive controls, or `impvalues` the aggregates are not simple reweightings of these rows), in *e(gteffects)* — one row per estimated *i*-by-relative-period cell, read directly off the underlying regression's coefficient vector at essentially zero additional computation. Note that a coefficient dropped by the regression (collinearity omission) simply has no row: the table does not distinguish a partially identified horizon from a complete one on its own - under default safety the dropped-coefficient screen errors first, but under *safety(off)* compare the row count against the expected cohort-by-period cells. Columns: *i* (the cohort/unit identifier value), *onset* (that unit's effective treatment onset, after any `anticipation()` shift), *caltime* (the calendar period the cell covers), *relyr* (period relative to onset), *estimate*, *se*, *p*, *lb_95ci*, *ub_95ci* (both interval columns are named for the run's `level()`: `level(90)` gives *lb_90ci* and *ub_90ci*, and a fractional level writes the point as a p, `level(97.5)` giving *lb_97p5ci* and *ub_97p5ci*), *inwindow* (1 if the cell falls inside the reported event-study window), *nobs* (estimation-sample observations in the cell), and the cell's total estimation weight under each requested estimand (*w_att*, *w_att_it*, *w_att_i*, *w_att_itime*; missing for estimands not requested). The weighted mean of *estimate* under any of these weight columns, taken over the rows that make up the aggregate in question, reproduces the corresponding aggregate exactly: *relyr* >= 0 for a main row, *relyr* < 0 for a pretreatment row, and a single *relyr* for an event-study horizon. The post-treatment filter is on *relyr*, not on *inwindow* - the main aggregate averages every estimable post-treatment period, so out-of-window rows belong in it - and the weight columns are positive on pre-treatment rows as well, so a weighted mean taken over every row of the table reproduces no reported aggregate. With that filter the table supports arbitrary custom aggregations and is directly comparable to the group-time tables of *csdid* or *xthdidregress* (the cells are ATT(g,t)-style group-time effects when *i* indexes cohorts; with a fine unit-level *i* they are unit-by-period effects and the table can be large). Notes: standard errors are the regression's coefficient-level (conditional) VCE — *unconditionalse* and *impvalues* do not alter them, and where a treated cell lies wholly inside one cluster (or, under `robust`, inside one observation) they are understated and the cell interval does not cover at its nominal level, which What level to cluster at measures and *e(gt_ncontained)* counts; under *poisson* the estimates are on the linear-index (xb) scale; under *timetrends* the coefficient-level variances are typically not identified and the *se* column is missing. A degenerate cell variance is always reported as missing, never as a standard error, and the test is a relative floor rather than an exact zero: a robust or cluster-robust stage-1 covariance that is rank-deficient in a cell's direction leaves cancellation residue of order 1e-30 rather than a zero, so a cell whose standard error falls below 1e-10 of its own estimate, or whose variance falls below 1e-12 of the largest cell variance in the fit, is reported with a missing *se*, *p* and interval. The count is in *e(gt_ndegen)* and a caution names it; with `subgroups()` a single full-sample table is returned; with time-varying `interactivecontrols()` the interaction coefficients absorb control variation and the cell-level reading is unreliable. Not available with `contreat()`, where the cohort-by-period interaction coefficients are dose-zero intercepts rather than treatment effects. Fully available under *fastnose* (the regression's own VCE is intact even though the *margins* stage skips variances), making *gteffects* the one inference surface that survives fast mode. Under `bootstrap()` the cell standard errors, p-values and intervals are bootstrap quantities as well: each replication's cells are harvested by cohort and relative period, and the columns follow `bootdisplay()` and `boottstat` exactly as the main tables do (the standard error is the bootstrap standard deviation of the cell, or its analytic standard error under `boottstat`). The same holds for the *e(cfx*)* matrices behind the continuous-treatment plots, and under `bootstrap()` those plots are redrawn from the bootstrapped matrices after the rewiring (as `twoway` renderings of the matrices rather than `marginsplot` output, so they can differ cosmetically from an analytic run's plots, and `saveplots()` additionally writes one *name*`_cfx.dta` with every plotted point); only the *histogramcohorteffects* outputs remain analytic under `bootstrap()`. Under `permute()` the *gteffects* cells are keyed by cohort onset, which the replications reassign, so they have no permutation distribution and their inference columns are blank (the points stay); the *e(cfx*)* matrices are permuted for assignment-invariant lattice types and blanked otherwise, and the plots are redrawn from them as under `bootstrap()`. If no cohort-by-period coefficients can be read off the fit (heavily collinear designs), no table is returned and a caution prints. Two reading notes. First, under `interactivecontrols()` the CELLS are approximate: the interaction coefficients absorb control variation, as the caveat above says, so a cell is not the clean cohort-by-period treatment effect it is elsewhere, and the weighted-mean identity that aggregates them inherits that approximation. Recovering exact cells under that option would need an estimation-weighted within-cell mean per control and would turn each cell standard error from a diagonal read into a quadratic form; wooldid does not do this. Second, when the reported event-study window has no post-treatment horizons (e.g. `espostlength(0)`), *inwindow* is 0 on every post-treatment row by construction.

## 14. Messages: every caution, note and error, with its trigger

This chapter is generated from the program source. Each row gives the subprogram that prints the message, the condition under which it prints (the Stata expression as written in the source, with wooldid's internal macro names), and the message text as written, with macros unexpanded (a name in backquotes is filled in at run time; a message assembled from several quoted pieces shows the pieces). Cautions never stop the run; errors do. Read the condition to learn what triggered a message you saw, and the message for what to do about it.

### Cautions and notes

| program | prints when | message |
|---|---|---|
| `wooldid` | ``wdsh_rc' == 907` | Note: r(907) is Stata's variable limit; maxvar is currently `=c(maxvar)'. Consider setting your cohort variable i to a higher level of aggregation (minimally, groups specifying treatment onset years), and separately using your unit or cluster variable as an input to fe(). If the issue is not an overly granular i variable, consider raising the variable limit using 'set maxvar'. |
| `wooldid` | ``wdpts' < . & `wdpts' > 0 & `wdpq' < .` | Caution: the inverted-t CI for `wdpe' is unavailable (no usable observed standard error). |
| `wooldid` | ``wdp_r_done' > 10000` | Caution: `wdp_r_done' completed replications exceeds the 10,000 cap on posting draw matrices, so e(permdist), e(permdist_b) and e(permdist_se) are not posted. Use permsave() to write the draws. |
| `wooldid` | ``wdsh_dc' != 0` | Caution: the continuous-treatment plot-data file `e(wdid_cfxsave)'_cfx.dta could not be written (r(`wdsh_dc')), usually because a file of that name exists and replace was not specified. |
| `wooldid_worker` | `_rc != 0` | Caution: estimability checks found time periods exist in sample without a control unit, causing them to be dropped. |
| `wooldid_worker` | `"`adversarial'" != ""` | Caution: adversarial pre-trend testing was skipped due to insufficient event study horizons.  |
| `wooldid_worker` | `"`adversarial'" != "" & "`altmethod_adversarial'" != "" & "`esfixedbaseperiod'" != "" & `esrelativeto' == -1 & `espre' <= 1` | Caution: adversarial pre-trend testing was cancelled due to insufficient event study horizons.  |
| `wooldid_worker` | `"`adversarial'" != "" & "`esfixedbaseperiod'" == ""` | Caution: the adversarial pre-trend p-value requires esfixedbaseperiod (the pooled reference has no single omitted period); the fitted line is reported without a p-value. |
| `wooldid_worker` | ``si8ilev' > 100` | Caution: the cohort variable `i' takes `si8ilev' distinct values. wooldid builds one interaction term per cohort-by-period cell, and runtime grows faster than linearly in that cell count, so a granular `i' can dominate runtime. Coarsening `i' is normally the single largest speedup available: specify unit-level fixed effects via fe(), which on a panel unbalanced within cohorts is what holds the point estimate fixed, and swap `i' to a coarser level of aggregation, such as cohorts or treatment onset groups. (safety(off) suppresses this check.) |
| `wooldid_worker` | `(unconditional at that point)` | Caution: `=cond("`jointtests'" != "", "jointtests", "pretrendpower()")' was combined with analytic clustered SEs clustered at the level of the cohort variable i or above (`i' is nested within the clustering variable `jtcoarse'). The saturated cell dummies leave the joint test's covariance degenerate, so its p-value is not a calibrated test. permute(), bootstrap(), or a cluster() strictly finer than the cohort-by-period cells that leaves many clusters inside each cell gives a joint test that is valid under this design. |
| `wooldid_worker` | `_rc == 3200` | Caution: initial estimation of ppmlhdfe failed, possibly due to inclusion of an absorbed categorical fixed effect with only 1 level. |
| `wooldid_worker` | `r(N) > 0` | Caution: ppmlhdfe excluded `r(N)' treated observation(s) from the estimation sample (separation, or a singleton fixed-effect group), so their cohort-period cells contribute nothing to the reported effects. |
| `wooldid_worker` | ``wdvzx' == 0` | Caution: the estimation command returned `wdr4noun', so no standard error in this run is identified. The reported estimates stand; `wdr4st'. `=cond(`amescrub' == 1, "See the cell-containment caution above for the mechanism and for the alternatives", "The alternatives are")' (`wdr4alt'). |
| `wooldid_worker` | `(unconditional at that point)` | Caution: for one or more Ibragimov-Muller estimates the per-cluster estimates were numerically identical, so the cross-cluster (IM) standard error is not identified; the standard error, test statistic, p-value and confidence interval are set to missing for the affected estimate(s) while the point estimate is retained. This usually reflects deterministic or noiseless data. |
| `wooldid_worker` | `"`adversarial'" == "adversarial"` | Caution: adversarial pre-trend testing was cancelled: too few event-study coefficients were estimated to fit the reference line. |
| `wooldid_worker` | `"`adversarial'" == "adversarial"` | Caution: adversarial pre-trend testing was cancelled: too few event-study coefficients were estimated to fit the reference line. |
| `wooldid_worker` | ``sgndrop' > 0` | Caution: the adversarial pre-trend test was skipped for subgroup `sgcase': `sgndrop' relative period effect(s) were dropped as not estimable, so its coefficients no longer span a complete relative-time grid and a line fit through them would not be interpretable. |
| `wooldid_worker` | `"`advstatus'" != "gapskip"` | Caution: the adversarial pre-trend test was skipped for subgroup `sgcase': too few event-study coefficients. |
| `wooldid_worker` | `"`advstatus'" != "gapskip"` | Caution: the adversarial pre-trend test was skipped for subgroup `sgcase': too few event-study coefficients. |
| `wooldid_worker` | ``evpostrc' == 0` | Caution: standard errors were not identified for `evpostmiss' of `evposttotal' reported estimates; their e(V) entries are placeholders that display as missing standard errors, and all other estimates retain their variances. |
| `wooldid_worker` | ``evpostmiss' > 0 & "`fastnose'" == ""` | Caution: standard errors were not identified for `evpostmiss' of `evposttotal' reported estimates, so no variance-covariance matrix e(V) was posted. Point estimates remain in e(b); per-estimate results remain in e(wdidmainresults) and the displayed tables. |
| `wooldid_worker` | ``evpostmiss' == 0` | Caution: wooldid could not post a variance-covariance matrix e(V) for this run. Per-estimate results remain in e(wdidmainresults) and the displayed tables. |
| `wooldid_worker` | ``f2ok' == 0 & !(`amevoid' & strpos("`f2ky'", "ame") == 1)` | Caution: no horizon of the `f2ky' event study has an identified variance, so e(b_es_`f2ky') posts without e(V_es_`f2ky'). The estimates stand; anything needing their covariance - honestdid, pretrendpower(), a joint test - has no input on this run. |
| `wooldid_worker` | ``f2vrc' == 0 & `f2nbad' > 0` | Caution: e(V_es_`f2ky') contains `f2nbad' of `f2k' horizons with zero or missing variance (structurally degenerate rows); inspect the diagonal before joint inference - honestdid/lincom on those rows is not meaningful. |
| `wooldid_worker` | ``gtprc' != 0` | Caution: e(gteffects) could not be posted (rc `gtprc'). |
| `wooldid_worker` | ``gtprc' == 0 & `gtcontain' > 0` | Caution: `gtcontain' of `gtn' e(gteffects) cell(s) lie wholly inside a single `gtcmode', so their se, p and interval columns are understated and the cell interval does not cover at its nominal level; the aggregate rows are a separate question, discussed under Standard Errors in the help. A cluster() strictly finer than the cohort-by-period cells, bootstrap() or permute() gives valid cell inference. |
| `wooldid_worker` | `"`bootstrap'`permute'" == ""` | Caution: `gtndegen' of `gtn' e(gteffects) cell(s) have a stage-1 variance that is cancellation residue rather than an estimated variance, so their se, p and interval columns are reported as missing; the point estimates are unaffected. bootstrap() rebuilds the cell inference from replications, and a cluster() strictly finer than the cohort-by-period cells avoids the degeneracy. |
| `wooldid_worker` | `"`boottstat'" != ""` | Caution: `gtndegen' of `gtn' e(gteffects) cell(s) have a stage-1 variance that is cancellation residue rather than an estimated variance. Under boottstat that standard error is the studentizing denominator, so those cells report a missing se, p and interval, and the p-values of the other cells move too; the point estimates are unaffected. |
| `wooldid_worker` | ``gtprc' == 0 & `gtndegen' > 0` | Caution: gteffects found no cohort-by-relative-period main-effect coefficients to report. |
| `wooldid_validate` | `"`safety'" != "off" & "`unconditionalse'" == "unconditionalse" & ("`weight'" == "aweight" )` | Caution: unconditional standard errors may be unreliable with aweights. |
| `wooldid_validate` | `("`customvce'" != "" \| "`unconditionalse'" != "") & ( "`fe'" != "" \| "`ccc_absorb'" != "")` | Caution: unconditional SEs and custom VCE specifications rely on the plain regress command and so are not compatible with absorbed fixed effects. |
| `wooldid_validate` | `"`safety'" == "off"` | Caution: interpretation of continuous treatment effects plots for estimands other than the ATT is difficult unless i-t cells (and sometimes cohorts i) are wholly contained within lattice bins. |
| `wooldid_validate` | `(else branch of the preceding condition)` | Caution: interpretation of continuous treatment effects plots for estimands other than the ATT is difficult unless i-t cells (and sometimes cohorts i) are wholly contained within lattice bins. |
| `wooldid_validate` | `"`subgroups'" != "" &   `esprelength' > 0  & `espostlength' == 0 & "`esposttrip'" == ""` | Caution: espostlength set to 1 due to use of subgroups in conjunction with esprelength > 0. |
| `wooldid_validate` | `r(N) > 0 & max(abs(r(min)), abs(r(max))) > 16777216 & max(abs(r(min)), abs(r(max))) < .` | Caution: `wdfv' is stored as float and holds values above 16,777,216 (2^24), beyond which float cannot represent consecutive integers, so adjacent periods, cohorts or IDs may have been merged before wooldid saw them. If it is an 8-digit calendar code or a %tc datetime, regenerate it as double from its source; recasting cannot recover the lost values. |
| `wooldid_validate` | `"`fcbad'" != "" & "`nofastcollapse'" == ""` | Caution: the default cell collapse does not apply with: `fcskip'. The margins calls are evaluated on the full estimation sample instead; the run is slower and no answer changes. |
| `wooldid_validate` | `"`safetyuser'" == "off"` | Caution: contreatwithin is specified in conjunction with the byTime contreatcontrol type. |
| `wooldid_validate` | `"`cfxlattice'" != "" & "`makecfxplots'" == ""` | Caution: cfxlattice() has no effect without makecfxplots and is being ignored. |
| `wooldid_validate` | `r(N) > 0` | Caution: the cfxlattice() variable is missing on `r(N)' observation(s) with a nonmissing treatment onset; margins omits those observations when computing the continuous treatment effect plot points, so the plotted averages reflect only the nonmissing-cfxlattice subsample. |
| `wooldid_validate` | `(unconditional at that point)` | Caution: some treatment onset times are not on the same grid as t (t minus ttre is non-integer), but all such onsets fall outside the usable estimation window and will be dropped or treated as never-treated downstream. |
| `wooldid_validate` | ``esrelativeto' != -1 & "`esfixedbaseperiod'" == ""` | Caution: esrelativeto() has no effect without esfixedbaseperiod and is being ignored. |
| `wooldid_validate` | `"`impvalues'" != ""` | Caution: fastnose is unnecessary with impvalues; impvalues already employs this trick to the maximum amount possible. |
| `wooldid_validate` | ``esprelength' <= 0 \| `espostlength' <= 0` | Caution: adversarial pre-trend testing was dropped: the test fits a line through the reported relative-period coefficients and needs at least one pre-treatment and one post-treatment horizon, but this window requests `esprelength' pre and `espostlength' post. Specify esprelength() and espostlength() of at least 1 to run it. |
| `wooldid_validate` | `"`timetrends'" != "" & `esprelength' + `espostlength' + 1 < 3` | Caution: adversarial pre-trend testing was dropped: with timetrends the test needs at least three event-study coefficients in the requested window (the reference period counts as one), and this window requests `esprelength' pre and `espostlength' post. Widen the window or drop timetrends to run it. |
| `wooldid_anticgrid` | `"`anticoffgrid'" != ""` | Caution: anticipation() is expressed in the units of `tvar', and the effective onset value(s) `anticoffgrid' fall on period(s) not observed in the data. Depending on the specification, this can merely relabel horizons, move the reference period in unintended ways, or cause estimation to fail. Verify that the anticipation length matches the spacing of `tvar'. |
| `wooldid_anticesf` | `r(N) > 0` | Caution: anticipation(`antic') places the effective reference period of `r(N)' unit(s) on a period unusable for them (not observed, or dropped for lack of a comparison unit); those unit(s) and their `anticdropobs' observations are dropped (their reference period would have been usable without the anticipation shift). |
| `wooldid_ifrule` | ``wdvifn' > 0` | Caution: the if() expression is nonzero but not exactly 1 on `wdvifn' observation(s). wooldid keeps only the rows where it equals exactly 1, where Stata's own if-qualifier keeps any nonzero value, so those observations are excluded and counted in the dropped-observation total. Write the restriction as a comparison, for example if <var> != 0, for Stata's usual behaviour. |
| `wooldid_fcdeg` | `(unconditional at that point)` | Caution: wooldid found `fcdegwhy' at a subgroup margins call, a numerically singular direction of the VCE where an exact zero can be cancellation residue rather than an estimate, so which subgroup horizons and joint tests are reported can depend on the summation order, which the default cell collapse changes. Under default safety the subgroup results are evaluated on the full data from this point; with safety(off) the run proceeds on the collapsed rows. Specify nofastcollapse to evaluate everything on the full data. |
| `wooldid_permctl` | ``wdp_floor' > .05` | Caution: with permreps(`permreps') the smallest attainable per-coefficient permutation p-value is " %5.3f `wdp_floor' " (" cond(`wdpcv' == 0, "2", "1") "/(permreps + 1)), above .05, so no coefficient p-value can reject at the 5% level; a p-value printed at that floor is the resolution limit rather than evidence. Use at least " cond("`absp'" != "", "20", "40") " replications for a 5% test (roughly 100 for .02, 200 for .01 and 1000 for .002). The joint, equality and step-down tests have floor 1/(permreps + 1). |
| `wooldid_permctl` | `"`ts'" != "" & "`clus'" == "`ivar'"` | Caution: permtstat with cluster() at the i level. The studentized statistic protects against treated clusters being atypical in variance only when the cluster-robust standard error can see their variance, which needs many observations per treated cohort-period cell and cluster() coarser than the cell; with one observation per i-period it cannot, and permtstat gives no protection there. |
| `wooldid_permctl` | `"`ts'" == "" & "`clus'" == "`ivar'"` | Caution: cluster() is at the i level and the permuted statistic is the coefficient itself. Randomization inference presumes clusters are exchangeable in assignment, not alike in variance, so the coefficient statistic carries no protection against a treated cluster whose errors are unusually dispersed; permtstat protects only when the cluster-robust standard error can see the treated clusters' own variance, which at cluster() = i with one observation per i-period it cannot. |
| `wooldid_permctl` | ``wdpntc' > 0 & `wdpntc' < 6` | Caution: only `wdpntc' of the resampling clusters are ever treated, so the number of distinct onset assignments is small: sampled draws will repeat and the set of p-values the run can produce is coarse however large permreps() is. permexact enumerates the assignments and reports the exact floor instead. |
| `wooldid_permctl` | `"`permstrata'" == "" & "`permmethod'" == "shuffle"` | Caution: subgroups() with permute(shuffle) and no permstrata(): the shuffled assignments also re-randomize which subgroups the treated clusters fall in, so the reference distribution of the subgroup-specific statistics mixes subgroup composition with onset timing. When subgroup membership is fixed at the cluster level, permstrata(`sg') holds each subgroup's onset composition fixed across replications. |
| `wooldid_bootctl` | `"`bts'" != ""` | Caution: fastnose-style speedup tricks are automatically employed with bootstrap() to the maximum extent possible; it need not be specified. |
| `wooldid_bootctl` | `(unconditional at that point)` | Caution: bootstrap(bayes) standard errors for `cellwtd' omit the cell-sampling variance component and can be too small when treatment effects vary across clusters; the joint tests, pairwise subgroup contrasts and confidence intervals for those estimands inherit the understatement. Read them alongside a bootstrap(standard) run, which does not have this limitation. |
| `wooldid_bootctl` | ``wdbgrid' > .05 & "`bts'" != ""` | Caution: with reps(`reps') the smallest studentized coefficient p-value this run can produce is " %5.3f `wdbgrid' " (2/(reps + 1)), above .05, so no coefficient p-value can reject at the 5% level; a p-value printed at that value is the resolution limit rather than evidence. Use at least 39 replications for a 5% test (roughly 100 for .02, 200 for .01 and 1000 for .002); failed replications are excluded, so the realized resolution can be coarser. |
| `wooldid_bootctl` | ``wdbgrid' > .05 & "`bdisp'" == "wald"` | Caution: with reps(`reps') the joint, equality and adversarial p-values - one-sided add-one shares over their own completed replication counts - cannot fall below " %5.3f `wdbgrid' " (1/(reps + 1)), above .05, so none of them can reject at the 5% level; the coefficient p-values under bootdisplay(wald) are analytic and carry no such limit. Use at least 19 replications for a 5% joint test (roughly 50 for .02, 100 for .01 and 500 for .002); failed replications are excluded, so the realized resolution can be coarser. |
| `wooldid_bootctl` | ``wdbgrid' > .05` | Caution: with reps(`reps') the coefficient p-values fall on a grid of " %5.3f `wdbgrid' " (2/reps), so the only value below .05 this run can produce is exactly 0, which records that no completed replication reached the far side of the null rather than strong evidence. Use at least 40 replications for a 5% test (roughly 100 for .02, 200 for .01 and 1000 for .002); failed replications are excluded, so the realized grid can be coarser. The joint, equality and adversarial p-values have floor 1/(reps + 1). |

This happens when a quantity is not zero under reassigned onsets, or under a permute(probabilities) design far from the observed assignment. |

Lattice types all, byCohort, byTime and byCohortByTime, or a user-supplied cfxlattice() variable, give assignment-invariant bins that are permuted. |

| `wooldid_bootrun` | `r(N) < 6` | Caution: only " r(N) " treated cluster(s) are available for resampling. What is implemented is the " cond("`mode'" == "bayes", "Bayesian bootstrap, which shares the pairs cluster bootstrap's first two moments", "pairs cluster bootstrap, which keeps only the replications that draw at least one treated cluster") "; with few treated clusters it is badly sized, and boottstat is not a cure for it. Consider egen newi = group(`i' `clus'), missing (the missing option keeps unlabelled rows as their own group) or a cluster() that nests `i'. e(boot_nstraddle) and e(boot_reid) record this. |
| `wooldid_bootrun` | `"`wdb_cellkeydup'" != ""` | Caution: the cohort-by-period cells could not be keyed uniquely for the bootstrap; e(gteffects) inference is left analytic and blanked. |
| `wooldid_bootrun` | ``nfail' > 0` | Caution: `nfail' of `reps' bootstrap replications failed to estimate and were excluded (official-bootstrap convention: complete replications only). |
| `wooldid_bootrun` | ``nnonv' > 0` | Caution: `nnonv' of the `ndone' completed bootstrap replications produced no usable standard error: every standard error they reported came back below 1e-8 of the outcome's own standard deviation, which happens when a resample draws too few distinct clusters and the model fits it exactly. The count is in e(boot_novar). |
| `wooldid_bootrun` | ``nnonv' > 0` | Caution: those `nnonv' replications' sampled values are kept as they came back, in e(bootdist_se) and in the bootsave() file if one was written, but they do not enter any studentized object: the percentile-t p-values and intervals, the simultaneous bands, the subgroup contrasts and the joint, equality and adversarial tests are computed on the replications that returned an estimable standard error, with the counts in row n of e(boot_tstud), e(boot_tstud_pwsgs) and e(esband_n_*). A design that produces exactly-fitting resamples is one the pairs cluster bootstrap is not reliable on; more clusters, or permute(), is the remedy. |
| `wooldid_bootrun` | ``nsesmall' > `nnonv'` | Caution: `nsesmall' of the `ndone' completed bootstrap replications reported at least one standard error below 1e-5 of the outcome's own standard deviation, and `nnonv' of them were excluded outright by the 1e-8 cut; the `=`nsesmall' - `nnonv'' in between are kept and used in full. A studentized statistic divides by these, so a handful of them can drive an implausibly wide simultaneous band or percentile-t interval. More clusters, a coarser cluster(), or permute(), is the remedy. Both counts are in e(boot_sesmall) and e(boot_novar). |

| `wooldid_bootwire` | `"`wdb_bcbad'" != ""` | Caution: the bias-corrected family is withheld for: `wdb_bcbad'. The pairwise subgroup contrasts are studentized the same way, and the joint pre-trend, subgroup equality and adversarial tests are bootstrapped as test statistics. The studentized kernel is in e(boot_tstud) and the draws in e(bootdist) and e(bootdist_se). |

| `wooldid_ccscreen` | ``catastrophe' > 0` | Note: the multicollinearity caution above was triggered `catastrophe' times total. |

The att-side results are unaffected. |

| `wooldid_sgdom` | `(unconditional at that point)` | Caution: the subgroup variable `subgroups' is missing on `f13pmn' in-sample row(s) of treated units of `i' that carry a subgroup value on their other rows; proceeding under safety(off) using share weighting. | `wooldid_sgdom` | ``sgmaxlen' >= 8 \| (`sgmaxlen' == 7 & "`makecfxplotsbysg'" != "")` | Caution: the subgroup variable takes values up to `sgmaxlen' digits long. wooldid embeds subgroup values in the names of stored results, matrices and plots, which Stata caps at 32 characters, so values 8 or more digits long (7 with makecfxplotsbysg) can cause r(198) invalid-name errors; recode the subgroup variable to small integers first (egen newsg = group(oldsg)). Values above 2,147,483,647 fail outright at Stata's factor-variable level cap. |
| `wooldid_sglbl` | ``ndrop' > 0` | Caution: the `kk'relative period effect(s) `droplbl' are not estimable for subgroup `sg' (margins reports them as omitted), so they are dropped from that subgroup's `kk'event study; its remaining relative periods, and all other subgroups, are unaffected. This typically arises when the specified fixed effects absorb the subgroup-by-time variation (for example fe() interacting the subgroups() variable with time), or when a subgroup has no usable observations at that relative period. |
| `wooldid_sglbl` | `"`keep'" == ""` | Caution: no `kk'relative period effect is estimable for subgroup `sg', so its `kk'event study is skipped entirely. |
| `wooldid_icscreen` | ``live' == 0 & `drop' > 0` | Caution: every treated cohort-period slope term for the interactive control `nm' was dropped for multicollinearity: `nm' does not vary within the cohort-period cells (for example i is as fine as the unit and `nm' is a unit-level variable, or `nm' is constant across units within a period), so the treatment effects cannot vary with it and this run is identical to controls(i.t#c.`nm'). Coarsen i so that cells hold variation in `nm', or move it to controls(). |
| `wooldid_advstat` | `"`sg'" == ""` | Caution: adversarial pre-trend testing was cancelled: after accounting for gaps in the data, too few pre-treatment coefficients remain to fit the adversarial reference line. |
| `wooldid_advstat` | ``advnpre' < 1` | Caution: the adversarial pre-trend test was skipped for subgroup `sg': after accounting for gaps in the data, too few pre-treatment coefficients remain to fit the reference line. |
| `wooldid_advstat` | `"`sg'" == ""` | Caution: adversarial: the composite-null covariance is degenerate; no p-value computed. |
| `wooldid_advstat` | `(else branch of the preceding condition)` | Caution: adversarial: the composite-null covariance is degenerate for subgroup `sg'; no p-value computed. |
| `wooldid_amescreen` | ``fire'` | Caution: with standard errors clustered on `clusvars', `ncells' treated i-t cell(s) lie completely within a single cluster, preventing valid analytic estimation of SEs for AME estimates (and with some adverse implications for the ATTs as well). Consider switching to use of bootstrap SEs, IM p-values, or randomization inference if finer clustering levels are non-viable. |
| `wooldid_amescreen` | `r(N) > 0` | Caution: under contreatcontrols(byCohort), `bck' treated cohort(s) (smallest: `=r(min)') have untreated/reference-period variation in `contreat' below 1/1000 of its sample standard deviation, so their cohort-specific baseline dose slopes are nearly unidentified. This is not numerical noise and regtol() does not reach it; treat the reported average marginal effect for these cohorts as possibly a wrong answer rather than a noisy estimate, and examine or drop the affected cohort(s). |
| `wooldid_amescreen` | ``sdup' >= 3` | Caution: `sdup' reported standard errors appear to be identical under a cluster-type VCE. This usually indicates a failure of analytic clustered standard error estimation; consider using a different approach to SE estimation (or consider clustering at a finer level of aggregation). |
| `wooldid_amescreen` | `"`contreat'" != "" & "`clusvars'" != ""` | Caution: estimation returned `vznoun'. The issue is likely related to continuous treatment effect estimation coupled with use of analytic clustered SEs, with clusters occuring at a high level of aggregation. Consider switching to use of bootstrap SEs, IM p-values, or randomization inference if finer clustering levels are non-viable.`vzres' |
| `wooldid_amescreen` | `"`clusvars'" != ""` | Caution: estimation returned `vznoun'. This usually reflects a degenerate clustering (very few clusters, or clusters at a high level of aggregation relative to the design). Consider switching to bootstrap SEs, IM p-values, or randomization inference.`vzres' |
| `wooldid_amescreen` | ``vzero' & "`r1fired'" != "1"` | Caution: estimation returned `vznoun'. Nothing in this run was clustered, so this is not a clustering problem: Stata replaces a variance matrix it judges nonsymmetric or highly singular with zeros, which is what a near-singular design does to the sandwich estimator.`=cond("`latflag'" == "1", " With lattice() the usual cause is treated cohort-by-period cells that hold too few distinct values of the continuous treatment once the lattice splits them; a coarser lattice, a base-period lattice type, or a coarser i gives a usable variance matrix.", "")' Bootstrap SEs and randomization inference (permute()) do not use this matrix; impvalues does not either, but it needs a cluster variable.`vzres' |

### Errors and refusals

Errors stop the run with a return code. A condition mentioning `safety` or `safetyinput` is one that `safety(off)` bypasses.

| program | refuses when | message |
|---|---|---|
| `wooldid` | `"${WOOLDID_LASTRC}" != ""` | Error: the last wooldid call failed; refit wooldid before replaying. |
| `wooldid_worker` | `"`weight'" == "fweight" & "`wdrepmode'" == ""` | Error: wooldid does not accept fweights. Specify aweights or pweights. |
| `wooldid_worker` | `_rc != 0` | Error: the customweightmultiplier() variable contains negative values. To ignore this error, specify safety(off). |
| `wooldid_worker` | `(see the preceding lines of the program)` | Error: the treatment onset variable `ttre_asgiven' is not constant within `i' (e.g., see `i' level: `f13badid'). To ignore this error, specify safety(off). |
| `wooldid_worker` | ``failure' == 1` | Error: cannot find sufficient pre- and post-treatment periods for the treated units. |
| `wooldid_worker` | `r(N) == 0` | Error: you have no never-treated control group and no not-yet-treated control group whose treatment falls outside the sample window. |
| `wooldid_worker` | `r(N) == 0` | To ignore this error, specify safety(off) or modify the sample window manually. |
| `wooldid_worker` | ``si8demand' > c(maxvar)` | Error: this specification will breach the current maxvar limit; at least `si8demand' variables are needed. Please either raise the maxvar limit or reduce the number of needed variables. If this error seems surprising, check whether your i variable is set to individual units within your dataset, rather than something more aggregate like groups of units by treatment onset (in which case, consider using your unit variable for fe(), and a treatment onset group variable for i).  |
| `wooldid_worker` | `(see the preceding lines of the program)` | Error: impvalues cannot be used with cluster() variables finer than `i', or which crosscut `i'.   |
| `wooldid_worker` | `e(N_clust) < 2` | Error: too few clusters. |
| `wooldid_worker` | `"`fastnose'" == "" & `df_r' < 1` | Error: the model leaves no residual degrees of freedom (e(df_r) = `df_r'), so no variance can be estimated. |
| `wooldid_worker` | ``wdhit'` | Error: the fitted model carries no estimable treatment-effect coefficient at any of the requested event-study relative periods. Check your specified controls and fixed effects.  |
| `wooldid_worker` | `(see the preceding lines of the program)` | Error: no treated unit is observed at any of the requested event-study relative periods.  |
| `wooldid_validate` | `_rc != 0` | Error: wooldid requires the package `requiredpackage', which does not appear to be installed. |
| `wooldid_validate` | `wordcount("`varlist'") < 4` | Error: command line input should feature all of y i t ttre; too few variables specified. |
| `wooldid_validate` | `wordcount("`varlist'") > 4` | Error: command line input should feature only y i t ttre; too many variables specified. |

| `wooldid_validate` | `"`adversarial'" == "adversarial" & "`impvalues'" == "impvalues"` | Error: adversarial is not allowed with impvalues-type inference. |

## 15. Comparisons to related estimators

The rules for reproducing other estimators, which double as checks that a wooldid specification means what you think:

| estimator | wooldid settings that match | where they differ |
|---|---|---|
| `did_imputation` (Borusyak, Jaravel, Spiess) | `att`, pooled reference, `esprelength(0)`, no controls, analytic standard errors | with controls only in the fine-cohort case; requesting pre-horizons changes the pooled reference |
| `hdidregress twfe, notyet` (Stata 18) | `att`, pooled reference, `unconditionalse`, weights constant within units | panel imbalance within onset groups; controls must be written as `i.t#c.x` |
| `csdid`, `xthdidregress` | `gteffects` with `i` = onset cohort gives the group-time table | conventions differ by default, so the aggregates need not match without an option mapping |
| `honestdid` | `e(b_es_type)`, `e(V_es_type)`, `e(numpre_type)` under `esfixedbaseperiod` | none when the geometry conditions hold |
| `pretrends` (Roth) | the same three objects, or `pretrendpower()` directly | `pretrendpower()`'s joint test, *mdt_wald* and slope interval use the regression's degrees of freedom (the bootstrap covariance and `bootdf()` under `bootstrap()`); its *mdt_nis* column and NIS size are under the package's normal reference |
| `ritest` | `permute(shuffle) permrawp` reproduces the plain share | wooldid's default is the equal-tailed add-one form |

The rest of this chapter is the help file's account of the first two comparisons.

### From the help file

### Comparison to Related Estimators

**Comparing Output to hdidregress twfe:** The STATA 18 command `hdidregress` has an option for implementing Wooldridge (2025) style estimates. Its implementation takes the Mundlak approach to estimation and uses the command `regress`, in conjunction with `margins`, to compute estimates. Estimates from *hdidregress twfe* with the option *notyet* (i.e., allowing use of not-yet treated cohorts as controls) should be comparable to estimates from *wooldid* using the *att* estimand-type, a pooled reference period (i.e., not using *esfixedbaseperiod*), unconditional standard errors, and sample weights which (if present) do not vary within units. Estimates produced by the two will still differ, however, when certain types of panel imbalance are present. For example, when a treatment onset group contains multiple cohorts and some of those cohorts are not observed in periods when others are observed, *wooldid* and *hddiregress twfe* will yield different results. In such cases, *wooldid* approaches the situation by presenting the average treatment effect among only the observed cohort-periods. For example, suppose cohorts 1 and 2 are in the same treatment onset group, cohort 1 is observed for the first 3 periods after treatment onset, and cohort 2 is only observed for the first 2. In this case, *wooldid* and *hdidregress* will report the same treatment effects for the treatment onset group for the first 2 periods after treatment. The two programs will disagree on the third effect: *wooldid* will report the same 3rd period effect that it (and *hdidregress*) would report if cohort 2 were not incldued in the dataset at all; *hdidregress* does not. Finally, in order to get estimates using controls to match between the two methods, a *wooldid* user will have to manually adjust the controls as needed (e.g., given a control that is constant within cohorts, a *wooldid* user will need to specify i.t#c.control to replicate the behavior of including that control in *hdidregress*). 

**Comparing Output to did_imputation:** The Borusyak, Jaravel, and Spiess (2023) difference-in-differences imputation estimator is implemented in the package `did_imputation`. Wooldridge (2025) proves some equivalence results between their estimator and the Wooldridge estimator. For binary treatments, *wooldid* will yield the same estimates as *did_imputation* given that one uses the *att* estimand-type, uses a pooled reference period (i.e., not using *esfixedbaseperiod*), requests no pre-treatment horizons (*esprelength(0)*), does not use unconditional standard errors, and does not use controls. With controls, *wooldid* and *did_imputation* will still yield the same results provided that each cohort-time period cell contains only 1 observation. In this 'fine cohorts' case, the inclusion of i-t cell indicators ensures that the included controls have their coefficients determined solely from the same portion of the sample as is used to estimate the regression in the BJS estimator. However, outside of special cases, results with controls will not match if cohorts are coarse (contain multiple observations per i-t cell). To assist in this case, the *wooldid* option *coarsecohortcontrols()* will add to the *wooldid* regression your controls plus interactions between your controls and the i-t cell indicators associated with periods for which treatment effects are requested. While the *coarsecohortcontrols()* option will not yield the same estimates as *did_imputation*, it arguably reflects an approach closer to the spirit of BJS in that it increases the degree to which the effect of the controls is independent between the control/reference portion of the sample and the portion of the sample where treatment effects are of interest.

## 16. Recipes and pitfalls

### Recipes

The help file's examples follow at the end of this chapter; these are the designs that come up most often, written with the recommended coarse `i`:

```stata
* staggered binary treatment, unit panel: coarse i, unit fixed effects, unit clusters, event study with tests
gen ttre_groups = ttre
replace ttre_groups = 9999 if ttre == .
wooldid y ttre_groups t ttre, att cluster(unit) fe(unit) espre(3) espost(3) esfixedbaseperiod jointtests adversarial makeplots

* few treated clusters (states): randomization inference over onset, coarse i
* (cluster() must contain whole values of i; with i = onset cohort and states inside cohorts, permonsetcohorts allows it)
wooldid y ttre_groups t ttre, att cluster(state) permute(shuffle) permreps(999) rseed(1) permonsetcohorts

* cluster bootstrap with studentized joint tests
wooldid y ttre_groups t ttre, att cluster(state) espre(3) espost(3) esfixedbaseperiod jointtests bootstrap(standard) reps(499) rseed(1) boottstat

* subgroup (treatment-arm) contrasts, and a triple difference
* (a subgroup must not vary within an i-by-period cell, so i is refined to onset cohort by subgroup)
egen newi = group(ttre_groups arm), missing
wooldid y newi t ttre, att cluster(unit) fe(unit) subgroups(arm)
egen newi2 = group(ttre_groups g), missing
wooldid y newi2 t ttre, att cluster(unit) fe(i.unit i.g##i.t) subgroups(g)

* treatment intensity that is zero outside treatment
wooldid y ttre_groups t ttre, att cluster(unit) fe(unit) contreat(dose) contreatcontrols(untreatedZero)

* a dose observed everywhere, identified against period-specific dose effects
wooldid y ttre_groups t ttre, att cluster(unit) fe(unit) contreat(dose) contreatcontrols(basic byTime) espre(2) espost(3)

* fast specification search, then the reported run
wooldid y ttre_groups t ttre, att fe(unit) espre(3) espost(3) fastnose
```

### Pitfalls checklist

1. `i` at the finest level: slow, and clustering at `i` breaks the analytic tests. Coarsen `i`, put the unit in `fe()`.
2. Never-treated units need a positive integer code in `i` and plain missing in `ttre`.
3. `ttre` recorded only from the onset period onward (missing on pre-treatment rows): refused as not constant within `i` under default safety; under `safety(off)` the unit is silently split into two cohorts. Fill it on every row.
4. IDs or dates stored as float above 16,777,216: adjacent values merge. Store as double.
5. `anticipation()` is in units of `t`; on a biennial grid an odd value lands between periods.
6. `jointtests` or `adversarial` with `cluster()` at or above `i`: the p-values are not calibrated. Use `bootstrap()` (`boottstat` for the adversarial p-value) or `permute()`. `pretrendpower()` reads the same covariance; under `bootstrap()` it uses the bootstrap covariance, and it is refused under `permute()`.
7. `contreat()` with `cluster()` at or above `i`: `ame` standard errors are missing by design. Same remedies.
8. `unconditionalse` with aweights, or with `att_it`/`att_i`/`att_itime`: cautioned or refused for a reason.
9. `permute()` without a `cluster()` that contains whole values of `i`: refused unless `permonsetcohorts`. Reassignment is at the `cluster()` level, which with a unit-level `i` and `cluster(unit)` is the unit.
10. `permreps()` or `reps()` too small: the p-value floor exceeds .05 and nothing can reject. Several hundred replications for reported inference.
11. A subgroup variable that splits a unit, varies within a cell, or is missing on some rows of a labelled unit: refused. Redefine `i` at the finer level with `egen newi = group(i sg), missing`.
12. Subgroup codes with 8 or more digits: stored-result names overflow. Recode with `egen group()`. (Cohort codes need only double storage above 16,777,216.)
13. `contreatwithin` with `contreatcontrols(byTime)`: the period slopes absorb the effect. Refused.
14. `contreatcontrols(untreatedZero)` when the dose is not zero outside treated cells: poorly identified estimates with no error.
15. A lattice with more bins than units per cell: pieces drop for collinearity and the `ame` averages only the survivors.
16. `fe()` terms no coarser than the cohort-by-period cells, or `ccc_absorb()` of a variable constant within cells with an event-study window: the treatment terms are absorbed.
17. The dose variable itself inside `controls()`: corrupts the margins stage. Use a renamed copy.
18. `if` keeps only rows equal to exactly 1; `if flag` with flag = 2 drops those rows. Write `if flag != 0`.
19. `esprelength()` and `espostlength()` count integer relative periods; gaps in the grid shorten the realized window.
20. `honestdid` handoff without `esfixedbaseperiod` at the default reference, or with gaps: the geometry is wrong and nothing warns. Check `e(estimate_type)` and the `relyr` column of `e(escomp)`.
21. `e(V)` for a test across two families or between a family and a pooled estimate: off-diagonals are zero and mean "not estimated". Use `e(V_es_type)` within a family.
22. `poisson` estimates are on the linear-index scale; `poisexpresults` no longer exists.
23. `fastnose` results reported as final: no standard errors were computed. Re-run without it.
24. Replay (`wooldid` alone) after a failed call: refused until a call completes; refit first.

### From the help file

### Examples

0) Conventional diff-in-diff, targeting the ATT and clustering standard errors by cohort:

`wooldid y i t ttre, cluster(i) att`

1) Conventional diff-in-diff, targeting both ATT and ATT_it, with production of histogram showing cohort-i specific effects: 

`wooldid y i t ttre, att att_it histogramcohorteffects`

2) Conventional diff-in-diff, with added controls and fixed effects:

`wooldid y i t ttre, controls(controlvar1) fe(i.fe_bonus)`

3) Conventional diff-in-diff, using coarse cohort controls and including cohort-specific time trends:

`wooldid y i t ttre, coarsecohortcontrols(c.controlvar1 i.categoricalcontrolvar) timetrends `

4) Event study with 3 pre-treatment and 3 post-treatment estimates using a pooled reference period and generating event study plots, as well as testing the hypothesis that all pre-treatment effects are 0 (the `adversarial` composite test that the estimates and the reference period fall on the same line requires `esfixedbaseperiod` - the pooled reference has no single omitted period - so here it returns the fitted line without a p-value, under a caution): 

`wooldid y i t ttre, espre(3) espost(3) makeplots jointtests adversarial`

5) Event study with 3 pre-treatment and 3 post-treatment event study estimates, calculating all estimates relative a fixed reference period (fixed to the period 2 periods before treatment):

`wooldid y i t ttre, espre(3) espost(3) esfixedbaseperiod esrelativeto(-2)`

6) Diff-in-diff with separate treatment effects calculated by treatment onset cohort:

`wooldid y i t ttre, subgroups(ttre)`

7) Event study with a fixed reference period, treating treatment as anticipated one period before its recorded onset, with Roth (2022) pre-trend power diagnostics at 50 and 80 percent power:

`wooldid y i t ttre, att cluster(i) espre(3) espost(3) esfixedbaseperiod anticipation(1) pretrendpower(50 80)`

8) Difference-in-differences with a treatment intensity variable (one that is 0 outside the treatment period), estimated using a stepwise-linear function and a lattice with 5 quantiles (formed based on the treated group's treatment period), and generating continuous treatment effects plots for the post treatment period:

`wooldid y i t ttre, contreat(intensity) contreatcontrols(untreatedZero) makecfxplot lattice(baseTreatedPost 5)`

9) Difference-in-differences with a continuous treatment variable that varies within all cohorts, with a lattice containing 5 quantiles (formed separately by treatment group and by period), controlling for the continuous treatment variable by treatment group and by time period, with continuous treatment effects plots for the pre-treatment period (which require an event-study window - here via *esprelength*):

`wooldid y i t ttre, contreat(contreatvar) contreatcontrols(byTreated byTime) espre(3) espost(3) makecfxplot lattice(byTreatedByTime 5) cfxplottypes(pre)`

10) Event study followed by a Rambachan & Roth (2023) HonestDiD pre-trends sensitivity analysis, using the full event-study variance-covariance matrix wooldid returns. (Requires the `honestdid` package. A fixed reference period at the default *esrelativeto(-1)* is **required** for this positional handoff — the example relies on the reference being the single omitted period at relative time -1; see the *e(b_es)* Stored Results entry for the full geometry conditions.):

`wooldid y i t ttre, att espre(4) espost(4) esfixedbaseperiod`

`matrix b = e(b_es_att)`

`matrix V = e(V_es_att)`

`honestdid, numpre(`=e(numpre_att)') b(b) vcov(V) delta(rm) mvec(0.5(0.5)2) parallel(0)`

The `parallel(0)` is not optional housekeeping. `honestdid` defaults to `parallel(4)` on any machine where the `parallel` package is installed, so each `delta()` call silently starts four additional Stata processes - multiplicative inside a loop over cases or over `mvec` values.

A Rambachan-Roth robust confidence set can legitimately come back **empty** on data with strong pre-trends: under the C-LF construction no parameter value survives the assumed bound on post-treatment violation. That is a finding about the data - the pre-trend evidence rules out every value at that *M* - and not a failure of the handoff or a sign that the wrong matrix was passed.

11) Fast exploratory event study (point estimates only), also returning the cohort-by-relative-period building-block table:

`wooldid y i t ttre, att cluster(i) espre(2) espost(2) fastnose gteffects`

12) Randomization inference: permute treatment-onset dates across clusters, holding onset-group counts fixed, with 500 replications (set the seed for reproducibility); every reported p-value and interval is then permutation-based, and the run is a fast (no-SE) run unless `permtstat` or `permanalyticse` is added:

`set seed 12345`

`wooldid y i t ttre, att cluster(i) permute(shuffle) permreps(500)`

13) Cluster bootstrap standard errors with 500 replications, reproducible, and joint pre-trend tests formed from the replications' own test statistics (`boottstat`; without it they are instead the fixed-metric rank test of the replication draws, as *e(boot_jt_family)* records) - the sanctioned route when clustering at or above the cohort level:

`wooldid y i t ttre, att cluster(i) esprelength(3) espostlength(3) jointtests bootstrap(standard) reps(500) rseed(42) boottstat`

14) Bayesian (Dirichlet reweighting) bootstrap, which leaves every cluster in every replication and so never loses the comparison group to an unlucky resample:

`wooldid y i t ttre, att cluster(i) bootstrap(bayes) reps(500) rseed(42)`

### Bugs and Assistance

**Known Issues:** 

With cohort-specific time trends, do not manually specify time-trend variables for inclusion in the fixed effects (i.e., in the *absorb()* statement when using *reghdfe*); let *timetrends* build them. Estimates are better conditioned when *esfixedbaseperiod* is also specified.

Estimation with continuous treatment controls sometimes faces numerical stability issues as well. Where the difficulty really is numerical, a higher *regtol()* or the option *ccnoabsorb*, which keeps the continuous treatment controls out of *reghdfe*'s *absorb()* statement, give more consistent results. Neither reaches a distinct case that can look similar: when a treated cohort carries almost no variation in the dose across its untreated or reference-period observations, that cohort's baseline dose slope is nearly unidentified, and the reported average marginal effect is then deterministic rather than noisy - it can sit on the exactly-collinear wrong answer across several orders of magnitude of tolerance, so tightening *regtol()* does not move it. *wooldid* cautions when it sees this and refuses outright when the variation is smaller still; the remedy there is to examine or drop the affected cohort(s), not to change the tolerance.

The automatic tests for model identification that *wooldid* implements are not fully comprehensive: *wooldid* will almost always succeed in identifying when a treatment effect has been partialled out by a fixed effect in the binary treatment case and will often succeed in doing so when a continuous treatment variable is specified without a lattice variable or any polynomial terms. However, a continuous treatment is specified with a lattice and/or a polynomial term, and especially in the presence of an unbalanced panel, *wooldid* will be unlikely to detect problems related to important treatment effects being partialled out by controls or fixed effects. As such, one should be very careful when specifying one's fixed effects and *contreatcontrols* when using a continuous treatment.

Under various circumstances, *wooldid* cannot estimate standard errors for all requested objects (for example, when an Ibragimov-Muller estimate at some event-study horizon has only a single contributing cluster, or when per-cluster estimates are numerically identical on effectively noiseless data). If standard errors are identified for some but not all reported estimates, *wooldid* still returns *e(V)*: the identified estimates carry their variances as usual, while each affected estimate carries a placeholder entry that displays as a missing standard error (and that *test* drops and *lincom* propagates as missing). The placeholder is stored as a large negative number, which every Stata display and inference surface renders as missing - but code that reads *e(V)* cells directly for its own matrix algebra should treat negative diagonal entries as not identified. If no reported estimate has an identified standard error, *wooldid* returns point estimates without *e(V)*, which can cause the functionality of certain post-estimation tools to break. In these situations, *wooldid* prints a caution; when the cause is unidentified standard errors, the caution states how many estimates were affected.

If you encounter a bug - be it the program crashing or just the program delivering a weird result - please reach out to me about it. This program is still under development, so assistance finding and correcting bugs is much appreciated. I am also happy to help and offer guidance on how to use this program.

Note: if you encounter unusual, hard to track down errors, try uninstalling and reinstalling *reghdfe* and *ftools* first. This can fix some problems.
